I believe <note>original: "apprehend"</note> few books are rarer than this work on large paper <term>large paper: a deluxe edition of a book printed on high-quality, oversized sheets with wide margins, often produced in very limited quantities for collectors</term>. Although I have collected this type of writing for many years, I have never seen another copy, nor can I find one in any catalog. What is even more remarkable <note>original: "singular"</note> is that it is not mentioned in the descriptive catalog <note>original: "Catalogue raisonné"</note> attached to the biography of John Toland <note>John Toland (1670–1722) was an influential and controversial rationalist philosopher, best known for his book "Christianity Not Mysterious" (1696).</note>.

# TETRADYMUS <note>original Greek: *Tetradymus*, meaning "fourfold" or "a collection of four"</note>

->CONTAINING<-

### **I. HODEGUS** <note>original Greek: *Hodēgos*, meaning "the guide"</note>; or the **PILLAR OF CLOUD AND FIRE**, that guided the Israelites in the wilderness, **NOT MIRACULOUS**: but, as faithfully related in *Exodus*, a thing equally practiced by other nations, and in those places not only useful but necessary.

### **II. CLIDOPHORUS** <note>original Greek: *Kleidophoros*, meaning "the key-bearer"</note>; or of the **EXOTERIC AND ESOTERIC PHILOSOPHY**, that is, of the *External* and *Internal Doctrine* of the ancients; the one open and public, adapted to popular prejudices and the established religions; the other private and secret, wherein, to the few capable and discerning individuals, was taught the real **TRUTH** stripped of all disguises.

### **III. HYPATIA**; or the *history of a most beautiful, most virtuous, most learned, and* **EVERY WAY ACCOMPLISHED LADY**; who was torn to pieces by the **CLERGY** of *Alexandria*, to gratify the pride, rivalry, and cruelty of their **Archbishop CYRIL**, commonly but undeservedly styled *Saint* **CYRIL**.

### **IV. MANGONEUTES** <note>original: *Mangoneutes*, likely a coined term meaning "one who exposes imposters" or "the hunter of Mangey"</note>: being a **DEFENSE OF NAZARENUS**, addressed to the Right Reverend **JOHN**, Lord Bishop of London; against his Lordship’s Chaplain Dr. **MANGEY**, his dedicator Mr. **PATTERSON**, and (who ought to have been named first) the Reverend Dr. **BRETT**, formerly belonging to his Lordship’s Church.

---
->**By Mr. TOLAND.**<-
---
->I will utter riddles from of old <note>original Hebrew: *Abbi’ah hidot minni qedem* (Psalm 78:2)</note><-

<meta>This is the title page of John Toland’s "Tetradymus," published in 1720. Toland was a controversial Irish-born philosopher and rationalist, often associated with Deism.</meta>

# TETRADYMUS.
<note>original: "Tetradymus" is a Greek word meaning "four-fold," referring to the four distinct essays contained in this volume.</note>

->CONTAINING<-

**I. HODEGUS; or the PILLAR OF CLOUD AND FIRE,** that guided the **Israelites** in the **Wilderness, NOT MIRACULOUS:** but, as faithfully related in ***Exodus***, a thing equally practiced by other nations, and in those places not only useful but necessary. <note>original: "Hodegus" is Greek for "The Guide." Toland argues that the biblical pillar of smoke and fire was a signal fire used for military navigation, a common practice in the ancient Near East.</note>

**II. CLIDOPHORUS; or of the EXOTERIC AND ESOTERIC PHILOSOPHY,** that is, of the *External* and *Internal Doctrine* of the ancients; the one open and public, accommodated to popular prejudices and the established religions; the other private and secret, wherein, to the few capable and discreet, was taught the real **TRUTH** stripped of all disguises. <note>original: "Clidophorus" is Greek for "The Key-bearer." This essay explores the idea that ancient philosophers hid their true, often radical, beliefs from the general public.</note>

**III. HYPATIA; or the history of a most beautiful,** most virtuous, most learned, and **EVERY WAY ACCOMPLISHED LADY;** who was torn to pieces by the **CLERGY** of *Alexandria*, to gratify the pride, rivalry, and cruelty of their **Archbishop CYRIL,** commonly but undeservedly styled *Saint* **CYRIL.**

**IV. MANGONEUTES: being a DEFENSE OF NAZARENUS,** addressed to the Right Reverend **JOHN**, Lord Bishop of **London;** against his Lordship’s Chaplain **Dr. MANGEY,** his Dedicator **Mr. PATTERSON,** and (who ought to have been named first) the Reverend **Dr. BRETT,** once belonging to his Lordship’s Church. <note>original: "Mangoneutes" is a Greek-derived term meaning "a detector of impostures" or "one who exposes a juggler." Toland is here defending his previous book, *Nazarenus*, which argued that early Jewish Christians (Ebionites) held the original, true version of Christianity.</note>

<meta>This is the second page of the preface (page ii), where the author begins explaining the motivations behind his study of ancient governments and the laws of Moses.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring two cherubs (putti) flanking a central foliate urn-like element, surrounded by symmetrical floral and vine-like scrollwork within a rectangular border.</image-desc>

# PREFACE.

I.
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' depicting a classical landscape scene with one figure standing and another seated near a tree, set within a floral border.</image-desc>

THE title pages of the following *Dissertations* <note>original: "Dissertations" — here referring to formal, written treatments of a subject.</note> clearly enough indicate their various subjects. However, the intent behind the writing and the reason for publishing each one require me to provide some necessary information to the reader before they begin to read them. 

After acquainting myself as best as I could with the various origins and unique constitutions of the Greek and Roman Republics—not forgetting the Persian and other monarchies—I judged that my efforts might be just as pleasantly rewarded if I used the same methods to gain as clear an understanding as possible of the MOSAIC *Theocracy* <note>The government of the Israelites as established by Moses, characterized by the belief in the direct rule of God.</note>, or the government of the Israelites. The curiosity that is natural to man, which makes him so ambitious to be characterized as one 

-><gloss>original Greek: Παλαιά τε πολλά τε ειδώς — "knowing many things and ancient things"</gloss><-

strongly urged me to look carefully into such remote antiquities. Furthermore, my duty—since the religion I professed was founded on the MOSAIC *Institution* <note>The system of laws and religious practices established by Moses.</note>—inspired me with even greater passion in my research. 

But as the political structure of the Jews could not be learned without their history (as both are contained in the same books and interwoven in the same narrative), I constantly made discoveries in them despite the almost impenetrable obscurity of which those books are accused. These discoveries related to matters that were either different from what was taught by my guides, or that were not taught by anyone at all. These findings proved

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that after studying classical Greek, Roman, and Persian governments, he turned his research toward the "Mosaic Theocracy" of the Israelites. He notes that while the biblical texts are often considered obscure, his diligent study has led him to new insights that differ from traditional teachings.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his study of the Mosaic Theocracy, shifting focus to how "miraculous" events in the Bible can often be understood as natural occurrences described through the hyperbolic language of the era.</meta>

->P R E F A C E.<-

...to me no less entertaining than instructive. One major point among others was that several transactions generally understood to be *miraculous* were, in reality, very *natural*—contrary to what the superstitious fears of some and the careless reading of others led them to believe. But such things, once taken for granted, soon become universal preconceptions. Consequently, they are never examined afterward, or if they are, it is with universal opposition toward the person who dares to make those inquiries. 

The discoveries I made of this kind created in me a higher respect for MOSES than was ever instilled by my teachers, and for better reasons. These discoveries also armed me against those who, not content with their own crude <note>original: "gross"</note> interpretations, add the monstrous *traditions* of others. They also protected me against those who reject the *Pentateuch* <note>The first five books of the Hebrew Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.</note> as entirely fictional, and who refuse to acknowledge either the wisdom or the holiness of the MOSAIC *Laws*. 

One of those discoveries is the subject of *HODEGUS*, or the first *Dissertation* <note>original: "Hodegus" — a title likely derived from the Greek word for "guide" or "leader." This dissertation examines the "pillar of cloud and fire" as a natural signal fire.</note>; in it, several other points are discussed in passing that are well worth noting. Therefore, my goal in this publication is to make MOSES better understood and, consequently, more easily believed. This serves to defend him both against those who unreasonably do not believe in him at all, and against those who, through their absurd beliefs, make him seem unbelievable. 

The style of the *Old Testament* is extremely hyperbolic <note>original: "hyperbolical" — using exaggeration for emphasis or effect.</note>, even in the books that are written in prose; but in the poetic books, it is wonderfully magnificent, sometimes even when describing the most ordinary events. For example, a storm is often described in such grand terms that it seems as if the entire structure of nature were convulsed and the universe were on the brink of ending. 

<margin>Genesis 23:6, 1 Samuel 26:12, 1 Chronicles 12:22, 2 Chronicles 14:14, Psalm 36:6, Psalm 65:9, Psalm 80:10 <note>OCR lists 19, but the reference to "cedars of God" is in verse 10.</note>, Jonah 3:3</margin>Everything great, beautiful, or excellent of its kind is attributed to GOD or named after Him. For instance, a mighty prince like ABRAHAM is called a *Prince of GOD*; a deep sleep is a *sleep from the LORD*; a numerous army is *the army of GOD*; extreme fear is *the fear of the LORD*; high mountains are *the mountains of GOD*; a great river like the *Jordan* is *the river of GOD*; tall cedars are *the cedars of GOD*; and a very large city like Nineveh is *the great city of GOD*. Many more such examples could be produced at will. 

Therefore, everything that is hyperbolic should not immediately be considered supernatural, nor should anything that is merely magnificent be admired as a miracle. Furthermore, whatever can be explained by ordinary means—anything whose phenomena are easily solved and of which the like...

<summary>The author argues that many biblical events typically viewed as miracles are actually natural events described using the hyperbolic "magnificent" style of Hebrew literature. He aims to make Moses more believable by distinguishing between supernatural intervention and linguistic idiom.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Pentateuch, Hodegus, biblical interpretation, miracles, naturalism, Hebrew idiom, hyperbolic style, Nineveh, Abraham</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface, specifically addressing the origin and development of his theory regarding the "Pillar of Cloud and Fire." He compares his work to that of contemporary scholars like Herman von der Hardt, distinguishing his naturalistic explanation from theories that suggest a "sacred fire" was passed down from Adam through the Patriarchs.</meta>

### P R E F A C E.

...as has often happened elsewhere, will be considered a miracle by no one who is not strongly biased by the errors of their education. Several years have passed since I wrote this *Dissertation*. I sent the plan for it to a learned gentleman still living, who showed it to some good judges and received their approval. Since then, I have seen a discourse on this subject written by <term>HERMAN VON DER HARDT</term>, professor of Oriental languages at Helmstedt—a man no less insightful and honest than he is judicious and learned, though, despite these good qualities, he is not infallible.

His idea ¹ is that there was a sacred fire kept unextinguished on an altar in <term>ADAM</term>'s time, preserved by <term>NOAH</term>, and successively passed down by all the <term>Patriarchs</term> to <term>MOSES</term>. He claims that <term>MOSES</term> ordered it to be carried by <term>AARON</term> before the army of the <term>Israelites</term> as a symbol of <term>DIVINE</term> presence and favor. He attempts to defend this account against various people who have sent their objections to him in <term>Letters</term> ²; but in the 24th section of my *Dissertation*, I prove his theory to be not only uncertain but in every respect false and groundless. However, my arguments were actually directed against another author who wrote to the same effect long before <term>Mr. VONDERHARDT</term>, whose work I had not yet seen at that time. 

In other respects, I gratefully acknowledge that I have benefited from his labors, and I am confident he will not think less of me for differing from him in this or any other particular. I have lately seen a book against him by one ³ <term>MUNDENIUS</term>, whom I have likewise answered without naming him by refuting the common notions that he defends. He would have succeeded better had he made proper use of what ⁴ <term>LIPSIUS</term>, ⁵ <term>BUXTORF</term>, ⁶ <term>SCHURTZFLEISCH</term> explicitly, and others occasionally—such as ⁷ <term>BRISSONIUS</term>, ⁸ <term>SPENCER</term>, and the <term>Annotators</term> on some of the Classic

### P R E F A C E.

<margin>Exodus 23:20, 21, 22, 23.</margin>
...authors have written concerning the *sacred fires* of the ancients. Many who fail in proving their main argument still become useful or pleasant to read through their supporting research. What I have proposed concerning the <term>ANGEL</term> mentioned in the 23rd chapter of *Exodus*, I do not expect will be properly received until the work appears to which that explanation properly belongs. However, I am not so doubtful about my idea of *the Angel* of God in the 19th and 20th verses of the 14th chapter of the same book, nor of my investigation concerning <term>RAGUEL</term>, <term>JETHRO</term>, and <term>HOBAB</term> <note>These three names refer to Moses's father-in-law and his family; the author argues they are often confused in biblical commentary</note>, who are strangely confused by most writers. I hope my account of the origin of the <term>Kenites</term>—explaining that <term>HOBAB</term> was rewarded according to the promise of <term>MOSES</term> for guiding the <term>Israelites</term> in the wilderness, where he was the <term>Fire-bearer</term> or General Guide of the Camp—will satisfy all impartial lovers of truth. 

As for others, I do not care what they think or say. If anyone should dispute the detail of *the Ark* being carried on men’s shoulders—because in some historical illustrations of the *Bible* it is depicted being carried like our <term>sedan chairs</term> <note>original: "Chairs"; the author is referring to portable enclosed chairs carried by poles</note>—he is welcome to his opinion. But since the *Ark* was intended to be visible, and its presence provided significant encouragement to the Army, I believe it was carried the other way <note>meaning high on the shoulders to be seen by the crowd</note>. Its rings and poles would work either way, but the nature of the situation—and the solemn ritual of saying, "*Rise up, LORD, let your enemies be scattered, and let those who hate you flee before you*" whenever the *Ark* was to move forward, along with many other passages to the same effect—leads me to believe the matter is settled in my favor. 

This *Dissertation*, to conclude what I have to say of it here, is mentioned in a note within *the Voyage of <term>JAQUES MASSÉ</term>* <note>A fictional travel novel by Simon Tyssot de Patot, often used to voice radical or deist ideas</note>, written by a gentleman of great wit and judgment, to some of whose friends I showed my manuscript at the Hague in the year 1708. His words on page 168 are these: *"We have heard mention of a learned man from*

He argues that the fire was a standard signaling method used by ancient caravans and identifies Hobab as the guide responsible for it. ## PREFACE.

<margin>See the smoke during the day and the flame during the night. He claims that the person who directed this fire and served as a guide to the Israelites was none other than **HOBAB**, the father-in-law of **MOSES**. He attempts to prove this through verses 29 and 30 of the 10th chapter of *Numbers*, and through several other passages of Holy Scripture. Published in Bordeaux, 1710. <note>original: "Voir la fumée pendant le jour, et la flame pendant la nuit. Il pretend que celui, qui a eu la direction de ce feu... n'etoit autre chose que Hobab... A Bordeaux 1710."</note></margin>
...in England, who not long ago composed a dissertation wherein he undertakes to prove that there was nothing miraculous, nor even extraordinary, in that **Pillar of Fire** which led the Israelites through the wilderness. He intends to show, by citing the best ancient and modern authors, that it has always been the custom in those types of deserts to use fire to direct the march of armies or caravans. These fires were carried ahead of them by guides so that the entire multitude could see the smoke by day and the flame by night. He claims that the person who directed this fire and served as a guide to the Israelites was none other than **HOBAB**, the father-in-law (or, as I previously suggested, the brother-in-law) of **MOSES**. He endeavors to prove this through the 29th and 30th verses of the tenth chapter of *Numbers*, and through several other passages of Holy Scripture. The reason why I have not entitled the work *Hobab* instead can be found in Section 27, to which I refer the reader.

II. Having been so detailed in relating the occasion for writing *HODEGUS*, I shall be more brief in explaining the reasons for publishing *CLIDOPHORUS* and *HYPATIA*. The first of these would be either unnecessary or more complete if we still possessed the book said by <term>Eunapius</term> <note>A 4th-century Greek historian and philosopher</note> to have been written by <term>Porphyry</term> <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who often sought to clarify complex philosophical systems</note>. Porphyry—commending the "medicine of clarity" and having experienced its benefits—wrote *A Treatise Concerning the Secret Doctrines of the Philosophers*. These philosophers had wrapped their teachings in obscurity, much like the myths of the poets, but he brought them to light. What made the philosophers so cautious, and made them go to such great lengths—for it is a much easier task to write of things plainly and directly, than in a complex and indirect manner...

<margin>While the philosophers concealed their secret doctrines in obscurity, just as the poets did with myths, Porphyry wrote a commentary to serve as a beacon and brought them to light. <note>original: "Των δε φιλοσοφων τα απορρητα καλυπτοντων ασαφεια, καθαπερ των ποιητων τοις μυθοις, ο Πορφυρειος, το μεν φαρος εποιησεν υπομνημα γραφας, εις φως ηγαγεν." From Eunapius, *Life of Porphyry*, page 18, 1616 edition.</note></margin>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of why ancient thinkers used allegory and hidden meanings—the "exoteric" and "esoteric" styles. The previous page introduced his works *Hodegus* and *Clidophorus*, focusing on how philosophers often masked their true beliefs to avoid persecution.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>

# PREFACE.

...words under the veil of allegory or fable) I have explained fully in the *Dissertation* itself. In fact, the poets were not exempt from the same danger that commonly attended the philosophers; for it is a major mistake to suppose that the former always wrote in a figurative style out of pure choice, or simply because it was as amusing or alluring as their poetic meter. No, no: those poets and philosophers who would rather gain the people's favor than run any risk in correcting or instructing them (for I exclude the most generous-spirited) must, no less than those corrupt orators and historians who seek the applause of the masses, approve of the public's favorite opinions, as well as tickle their ears and fancies.

Listen to a man perfectly acquainted with this matter. "Whoever writes anything for the public," (says <gloss>11</gloss> **SYNESIUS**, who was both a *poet* and *philosopher*) "or addresses his speech to them, must necessarily appear 'popular' in his doctrine, and invent or discuss what gratifies them. For they, being uneducated, are therefore stubborn and intractable defenders of their senseless prejudices; so that if anyone deviates from his country's traditional rites, he must immediately drink the juice of hemlock <note>The juice of the hemlock plant was used in ancient Athens as a method of execution, most famously for the philosopher Socrates.</note>. What do you think **HOMER** would have suffered from the Greeks if he had told the naked truth concerning **JUPITER** <note>original: *Jupiter* (Zeus)</note>? And what if he had not sold those monstrous fictions which terrify children?"

All foolish people are children, no less in other times than in that of **HOMER**; a secret well known to **EMPEDOCLES**, along with some other poetical philosophers. And I must admit that when I observe learned men—or those who are presumed to be so by their professions—laboring through indirect means to make others who are equally learned (and perhaps much more deserving on all accounts) vulnerable to the folly or madness of the common crowd, I cannot help but be filled with equal parts contempt and abhorrence. But I feel this above all when they are **Clergymen** (the professed preachers of charity, fair dealing, and moderation) who are guilty of such [unmanly]...

---

<gloss>11</gloss> "Whoever writes for the people and speaks to the people must necessarily be popular in his opinions, so that he may shape his words and discourse from what pleases them. For being ignorant, they are strong-minded and difficult to turn from their absurd prejudices; so that if anyone disturbs the traditions of the fathers, he will quickly drink the hemlock. What then do you think **HOMER** would have suffered from the Greeks if he had told the truth about **JUPITER**, and had not made up such monstrous tales to frighten little children?" <note>original Greek: Ὅστις δ᾽ οὖν δήμῳ γράφει καὶ δήμῳ λέγει... In Encom. Calvit. Edit. Par. pag. 72.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the social and religious intolerance that forces thinkers to hide their true beliefs. It moves from the historical reasons for secrecy (the "exoteric" and "esoteric") to contemporary examples of religious persecution in France and England.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>

## PREFACE.

...unmanly, even wicked schemes. Without even looking abroad at the methods of undermining and slander used by the various religious orders of the Roman Church against one another, or going into detail about the atrocious slanders of the *Constitutionists* <note>The "Constitutionists" were supporters of the 1713 Papal Bull *Unigenitus*, which condemned certain Jansenist doctrines. This caused a massive rift in the French Catholic Church.</note> against the *Anti-constitutionists* in France—where the poverty and plague currently prevailing there are blamed by the former on the latter—what shameful examples, I ask, of such foul practices could I produce right here at home? What gossiping, what insinuations, what prying <note>original: "pumping"</note>, what twisting and straining of words or actions occurs among both members of the Established Church and Dissenters <note>Dissenters were Protestants who did not belong to the Church of England.</note>, all under the guise or pretense of religion?

The examples are too obvious and the subjects too well-known for me to go into specifics, whether I wish to avoid resentment or avoid appearing biased. But between the misrepresentations and insults of enemies, the angry disapproval and abandonment by friends, and the suspensions, removals from office, and expulsions carried out by those in power (to say nothing of the legal disabilities and various other sufferings), people have become suspicious of one another. They are hesitant to speak their minds on most subjects, vague in their language, and compliant in their behavior. Because of this, not only is mutual trust in danger, but all worthy intellectual progress is at risk of being completely destroyed. 

What sneaky evasions and wretched excuses and tricks are men of great talent forced to use because of human weakness, simply to escape disgrace or starvation? And this often happens over abstract metaphysical ideas or fantasies that never existed anywhere but in the empty heads <note>original: "hollow noddles"</note> of daydreamers.

Reflecting on these issues—which are fully explained in the work itself—prompted me to publish ***CLIDOPHORUS***. I did this so that people today might see their own likenesses accurately portrayed in the history of past ages and learn both the causes and the cure for their current social sickness, even if they offer no thanks or reward to the "physician" <note>The author likely refers to himself here as a doctor diagnosing a sick society.</note>. 

However, outrages even worse than those I have already described—such as attempts to mob or even harass men of learning and virtue because they disagree with others on theoretical opinions—led me to write ***The Life of HYPATIA***. I was especially moved to undertake this work by the illegal and unchristian treatment that Mr. **WHISTON** <note>William Whiston (1667–1752) was a mathematician and theologian who was famously expelled from Cambridge for his unorthodox religious views.</note> received from Dr. **SACHEVERELL** <note>Henry Sacheverell (1674–1724) was a high-church Anglican clergyman known for his aggressive rhetoric against religious and political dissent.</note>, who insisted on forcibly driving him...

<summary>The author describes how religious factionalism and persecution in England and France force intellectuals into secrecy and hypocrisy, motivating him to publish "Clidophorus" and "The Life of Hypatia" as warnings.</summary>

-> # PREFACE. <-

...him out of the church in the middle of the divine service; though the one failed in his attempt, and the other escaped with no more harm than being called a *Rogue* by the pious old women—who were said to look like wise old men from an almshouse <note>An "alms-man" was a person supported by private charity, typically an elderly person living in a church-sponsored residence.</note>—and hooted at (as I heard) by some boys instead of them learning their Catechism. But Mr. Whiston <note>William Whiston (1667–1752) was a mathematician and theologian who was expelled from Cambridge for his Arian views, which denied the traditional doctrine of the Trinity.</note> may—next to God's providence—thank the good nature of the English people for his preservation. For in some other parts of the world, following much fewer threats and protests from two priests (not to mention the pulling and hauling of their officer), he would have been torn to pieces despite his clerical gown and his innocence.

As for the rest, there was never a story better supported by evidence than that of **Hypatia** <note>Hypatia of Alexandria (c. 350–415 AD) was a renowned Neoplatonist philosopher and mathematician who was murdered by a mob of Christian monks.</note>, whether you consider the authority of her contemporaries **Synesius**, **Socrates**, **Philostorgius**, and **Damascius**—the last of whom, if not her schoolmate, was certainly her father’s student—or whether you consider the subsequent accounts of **Photius**, **Suidas**, **Hesychius**, **Nicephorus Callistus**, **Nicephorus Gregoras**, and others. Between these authors, there is hardly any variation and not the least appearance of contradiction, regarding either her incomparable character or her unparalleled murder. I do not trouble the reader with what modern authors occasionally write to her advantage or discredit, since they can form no judgment except by using the same authorities I have used. Therefore, everyone may judge for themselves without being biased by the prejudices or self-interested views of others.

III. **Mangoneutes** <note>original: *Mangoneutes*; a Greek-derived title meaning "The Juggler" or "The Imposter." The author uses it here as the title for his defense.</note>, or the fourth *Dissertation*, I wrote in defense of another treatise of mine titled **Nazarenus** <note>original: *Nazarenus*; "The Nazarene."</note>. The purpose for which I compiled this last work, and what right I had to do so, is so fully explained at the beginning of the first dissertation that I need say very little about it here. The same apology holds true regarding my addressing *Mangoneutes* to the Lord Bishop of London. I have reason to hope that he will be as ready to grant me justice against those subjects under his authority as any gentleman would be sure to show his displeasure against a servant who insulted another man—an act for which the gentleman himself would otherwise be held accountable. One of my adversaries...

...ries <note>This likely completes the word "contraries" or "adversaries" from the preceding page.</note> has dedicated his book to his Lordship; another (not to dwell here on the rest) is his domestic chaplain, and consequently his servant, despite all that **Mr. Collier** <note>Jeremy Collier (1650–1726) was a prominent theologian and critic, known for his attacks on the "immorality" of the English stage.</note> would do to make the chaplain the master, at least over a layman. Both of them have not only willfully misrepresented me, but have directly lied about me. This is particularly true of <term>Dr. Mangey</term> <note>Thomas Mangey (1688–1755) was an Anglican clergyman who published a sharp rebuttal to Toland’s "Nazarenus" in 1718.</note>, who in several instances makes me say things I never even thought, and many things expressly contrary to what I have written. This must cause no small amazement to fair-minded readers. As for those others—who show themselves to be neither rational men (if they will not hear both sides) nor Christians (if they accept an accusation against any man without weighing his defense)—I ignore their criticism as much as everyone else ought to despise them.

That meddling arrogance, which is the inseparable companion of superficial reading; that nitpicking at minor details where a solid argument would break the teeth of superficial scholars <note>original: "smatterers."</note>; 그 angry ranting that replaces reason, and that malice which reveals the narrow-mindedness of a political or religious faction—I leave these to be gathered from the examples I have noted. I have intentionally overlooked many more, as they are too petty for my own or anyone else's consideration; a generous opponent would think them unworthy of notice even if they were actual mistakes.

I would not trouble the world with any kind of answer (having a more noble occupation for my thoughts and time), except that it gave me an opportunity to add further proof and explanation to the subject I had been treating. For, as I said just now, I neither feared the judgment of those who would read both sides, nor did I value the opinion of those who might applaud my challengers without ever seeing my book. 

But <term>Dr. Mangey</term>, beyond his constant legalistic trickery and scolding, has displayed such shameful ignorance in secular <note>original: "profane."</note> and sacred history that it was necessary for his Patron—for the sake of his own reputation—to know it, as he might be deceived by Mangey’s arrogant and confident air. This will appear, first of all, in my reply to his positive assertion *that I falsely stated it was the constant and general belief of the Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans."</note> that the Pentateuch, the Psalms, the Gospel, and the Quran <note>original: "Alcoran."</note> constituted the foundation of their religion, and formed what they therefore called the **RELIGION OF THE FOUR BOOKS**.* The passages which I have added here to those already in <term>Nazarenus</term> to prove these facts may perhaps be welcome to the curious; just as I know my defense <note>original: "apology."</note> of <term>Reland</term> <note>Hadrian Reland (1676–1718) was a pioneering Dutch scholar whose work sought to provide a more objective view of Islamic beliefs.</note> and others regarding their supposed favoring...

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his book "Nazarenus" against his critics, specifically Thomas Mangey. # PREFACE.

...favoring Islam <note>original: "Mahometanism"</note>, will appear so to the candid. But, in the second place, I know several churches where ordination would be refused to any candidate for the ministry who appeared no better acquainted with the origin of the word *Nazarene* than the Doctor—not to mention his strange visions regarding the history of the **Nazarenes**. It is, in effect, to be so ignorant of the history of **Jesus of Nazareth** that one would think it is not a recommendable quality in a preacher of his Gospel. After not knowing, or not taking notice, that it was prophesied of **Jesus** <margin>Matthew 2:23.</margin> that *he should be called a Nazarene*, he quibbles with me about titling my book ***Nazarenus***. Meanwhile, he pronounces with absolute authority <note>original: "ex cathedra," a Latin phrase meaning "from the chair," referring to an authoritative pronouncement.</note> that *Nazaraeus* is the proper word, and that *Nazarene*, in the writings where it occurs, is due more to the negligence of transcribers than to the judgment of the author. 

I have, on the contrary, proved that *Nazarene* and *Nazarean* were originally and interchangeably used for the exact same thing, whether the words relate to **Jesus** himself or to those named after him. *Nazarean* is used only once in **Matthew**, while *Nazarene* is used three times in **Mark**, as well as once in **Luke**. *Nazarean* appears often elsewhere in the **New Testament**, according to the analogy of the Hebrew word <note>13</note> *Notzeri*, just as *Nazarene* follows Greek and Latin formations. In our English translation, we rightly express both words by the phrase "**Jesus of Nazareth**," according to the reason given by **Matthew**: that he *should be called a Nazarene* or *Nazarean* (it matters not which) because of his dwelling in the city of **Nazareth**. Therefore, where one person writes "**Jesus the Nazarene**" or "the **Nazarean**," another says in parallel passages "**Jesus from Nazareth**" <margin>Acts 10:38.</margin>, which is all the demonstration one needs. 

<margin>The one from Nazareth. <note>original Greek: 'O ἀπὸ Ναζαρὲτ</note></margin>
<margin>Of the one from Nazareth. <note>original Greek: τοῦ ἀπὸ Ναζαρὲτ</note></margin>

The Doctor will perhaps say that his distinction regards the writings of the **Church Fathers**. To this I answer that the situation is the same: the interchangeable use of the words is the same in them as in the **New Testament**, even after *Nazarene* or *Nazarean* had changed its original meaning and also become a term of reproach to the non-Christians <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>. **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420), the scholar who translated the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note>, in his translation of the **New Testament**, uses *Nazarean* only once and *Nazarene* eighteen times. The interchangeable use of the words was authorized by the Apostolic writings, of which I am sorry any Doctor...

<note>13 More properly *Notzeri*, as may be seen in the 19th Note on *Mangoneutes* <note>A work by Toland titled "Mangoneutes: being a defense of Nazarenus" (1720).</note>, page 368.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Thomas Mangey (referred to as the "Doctor"), specifically addressing his confusion over the etymology of "Nazarene" and "Nazarean," and defending the author's work "Nazarenus" against Mangey's "Mangoneutes" (a disparaging title for Mangey's rebuttal).</meta>

### P R E F A C E.

...a Doctor of Divinity should be ignorant. He has further recourse (as will be seen in *Mangoneutes* <note>original: "MANGONEUTES," likely referring to Thomas Mangey’s critique of Toland titled "Remarks upon Nazarenus."</note>) to a false or unscriptural derivation of the word, adopted by some modern writers from the quaint ideas of certain venerable Church Fathers. To support this criticism, he quotes three or four writers who clearly mean the *Old Testament Nazirites* (or more properly *Naziræans*), whom they distinguish in those same passages from the *Christian Nazarenes* <note>original: "Nazarens or Nazareans"</note>. 

Thus, in the forged Letter of the equally forged **Lentulus**, a supposed "Governor of Judea," **Jesus** is described <margin>14</margin> as looking like a *Nazirite*—which I demonstrate he never was. Consequently, the Doctor’s derivation of *Nazarean* from the <margin>15</margin> root of that word, and the references others make to it, are not merely uncertain but absolutely false. 

Haste does not permit me to make the remarks that so naturally arise regarding some of his authors. Among them is **Ricoldus** (or **Richard**, if you prefer), whom the justly admired Dean of <margin>16</margin> **Norwich**—as well as the Doctor—calls "judicious." I suppose the Dean meant he was simply *less* injudicious than others whose books are stuffed with even more fables and falsehoods; nevertheless, **Ricoldus** still has his fair share of them. One of his "judicious" arguments to prove the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> is not divine is that it is written in <margin>17</margin> verse; he then denies that God ever spoke...

<margin>14</margin> <note>original: "Pili capitis ejus vincti coloris usque ad fundamentum aurium, sine radiatione erecti, et a fundamento aurium usque ad humeros contorti ac lucidi, et ab humeris deorsum pendentes, bifido vertice dispositi in morem Nazaraeorum."</note> "The hair of his head was the color of a ripe hazelnut, straight down to the ears, and from the ears to the shoulders it was somewhat curly and bright, hanging down from the shoulders and divided in the middle after the manner of the Nazareans."

<margin>15</margin> He must mean the Hebrew root <term>נזר</term> *Nazar* <note>original: "נזר"</note>, meaning "to separate," though he has not explicitly stated it. <note>original: "Separavit" (Latin: 'He separated')</note>. From this comes <term>נזיר</term> *Nazirite* <note>original: "נזיר"</note> or a separated person. In contrast, the root of *Nazareth* (and consequently of *Nazarene*) is <term>נצר</term> *Natzar* <note>original: "נצר"</note>, meaning "to ward" or "to preserve." <note>original: "Custodivit" (Latin: 'He guarded')</note>. From this comes the name *Nazareth*, due to its strong position on a hill.

<margin>16</margin> In the list of authors added to his *Life of Muhammad* <note>original: "MAHOMET"</note> and *Letter to the Deists*.

<margin>17</margin> <note>original: "Neque stilum neque modum habet [Alcoranum] Legi divinae similem, aut convenientem; est enim stilo rhythmicum, et affectatione plenum.—Vidimus enim in divina scriptura Deum loquentem cum Moyse et cum aliis prophetis, et nunquam rythmici vel versibus loquentem. Moysis autem Legem et Evangelium dicit MAHOMETES a Deo esse, et Deum illos libros MOYSI et CHRISTO dedisse: sed hi non sunt metrici vel rythmici, et nullus aliorum prophetarum qui divinam vocem audi-"</note> "The style and manner of the [Quran] is neither similar nor suitable to Divine Law; for it is written in a rhythmic style and is full of affectation. For we see in the holy scriptures God speaking with Moses and other prophets, and never speaking in rhythm or verse. Now, Muhammad says that the Law of Moses and the Gospel are from God, and that God gave those books to Moses and Christ; but these are not in meter or rhythm, nor did any of the other prophets who heard the divine voice [speak in verse]..."

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the polemicist Ricoldus and the contemporary critic Dr. Thomas Mangey. ### PREFACE.

...[God ever] spoke in this manner to any of the prophets, or that any of their books are in verse—a style which even philosophers, according to him, scorn to use. He fails to consider the many examples to the contrary in the *Old Testament*, of which several significant books—besides entire songs and fragments in some of the rest—are undeniably in verse. This is to say nothing of **Empedocles** <note>Empedocles (c. 494–434 BC) was a Greek philosopher who wrote his works in the form of poetry.</note> and a great many other poetical philosophers. 

**Ricoldus** <note>original: "RICOLDUS," referring to Ricoldus de Montecroce (c. 1243–1320), a Dominican friar known for his polemical writings against Islam.</note> likewise denies that **Mahomet** <note>Muhammad</note> could be a prophet for the whole world. He gives this "judicious" reason (to produce no more of his "choice" observations): that he wrote only in <margin>18</margin> *Arabic*. In this, he forgets or does not know that the *New Testament* was written only in *Greek*. 

I repeat on this occasion what I said in the Preface to *NAZARENUS*: that I generally take care *not to overburden my reader with citations of any sort. I content myself with proving or illustrating my claims with only as many authorities as are necessary, though I often have many others that I judge unnecessary or reserve for those who might try to answer me*. 

The outcome has sufficiently proven this. Despite my second release of work, I still have a generous supply of research regarding all the subjects I have addressed. **Doctor Mangey** may continue to call them "gleanings," and others may use their threadbare phrase of "borrowed learning." I readily confess to the charge, for I have gleaned and borrowed them in three universities where I was actually a member, and in two others where I stayed for a considerable time for this very purpose—following the example of the ancient philosophers. I will not even emphasize those universities I visited briefly, nor all the hard work I performed outside of them. 

I do not publish these things out of vanity, as anyone can see, but as a necessary defense of myself against the unfair or malicious suggestions of half-witted or half-learned priestlings <note>original: "Priestlings," a derogatory term for minor, narrow-minded, or inexperienced members of the clergy.</note>, who judge others by themselves—the very worst rule anyone can follow. It is to the moderate, serious, and learned theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> that I make my appeal, and not to those who, though they would seem to know everything, in reality know very little. And

Confutatio Legis Saracenicae, cap. 4."</note>

<margin>18</margin> **Mahomet** says that he is a universal prophet; but nevertheless, the **Quran** <note>original: "Alcoranum"</note> was given by God in Arabic letters, and he knew no other language besides Arabic. *Ibid. chapter 6.* <note>original: "Dicit { MAHOMETES } se prophetam esse universalem; sed tamen Alcoranum Arabicis literis a Deo datum esse, neque ullam aliam linguam se scire praeter Arabicam. Ibid. cap. 6."</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the contemporary clergy and his scholarly opponents. He argues that many clergymen are more interested in social climbing and politics than in genuine study. He specifically addresses the criticisms of Mr. Wotton and Dr. Brett regarding his work *Nazarenus*. Catchword: "Prayers"</meta>

# PREFACE.

How should they know any better? They seldom or never study anything. Instead, they spend what little time they have left—after chasing after church appointments <note>original: "benefices," which are church offices that provided a living or income</note> and fawning over those who can grant them—reading or talking about politics, visiting others or plotting, learning the fashions of dress or the language of the theater, and making up for their lack of theological skill with "a-theological" zeal. Nevertheless, their pretense of defending the CHURCH, even when nobody is attacking it, is usually just a manufactured passion (if I may put it that way). It is politically faked to trick the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note>, to please troublesome politicians and clergymen, or to serve as a cover for their own envious and disparaging character.

<margin>19 *A Letter to* EUSEBIA *in answer to the* LETTERS TO SERENA, *printed for Mr.* GOODWIN *at the* Queen's Head, *across from St.* DUNSTAN'S *Church in Fleet Street.*</margin>

IV. It is true that Mr. **Wotton** <note>William Wotton (1666–1727), an English theologian and scholar.</note>, like some others I might mention, is not directly involved in the responses made to *NAZARENUS* <note>Toland's controversial work *Nazarenus: or, Jewish, Gentile, and Mahometan Christianity* (1718).</note>. However, even though the <gloss>19</gloss> piece he previously wrote against me did not require a reply—in my own opinion and that of better judges—the few passages I have cited from it now are only intended as relevant examples of the point I was making where I quoted them. That point is: *some people will not allow a man to be praised for anything if he disagrees with them on specific details. If they oppose him on one thing, they feel obligated to oppose him on everything else. There is nothing, no matter how improbable or false, that they will not say without shame or regret in order to diminish or blacken their opponent's reputation.* 

In this way, they go to great lengths to make themselves look ridiculous and provide their opponents with an easy victory. In addressing what **Dr. Brett** <note>Thomas Brett (1667–1744), a clergyman who argued for the necessity of tradition in the Church.</note>—a person of distinguished talent and great composure—has argued against *NAZARENUS* in the preface to his treatise, *Tradition Necessary to Explain and Interpret the Holy Scriptures*, my intention was more than just defending myself. I also wanted to defend the first and best Protestants against certain half-professed or fully disguised "Papists" <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics or those suspected of Catholic sympathies.</note>. These people want to restore the priesthood, sacrifice, and altars that were rightly removed from the Church during the Reformation. They also want to bring back extreme ideas about spiritual ministers, holy oil, **chrism** <note>consecrated oil used in baptism and other religious rites</note>, and the practice of mixing water into the sacramental wine.

<summary>Toland criticizes the worldly ambitions of the clergy and defends his work against critics like Mr. Wotton and Dr. Brett, whom he accuses of trying to reintroduce Catholic rituals into the Protestant church.</summary>

<keywords>John Toland, William Wotton, Thomas Brett, Nazarenus, anti-clericalism, High Church, Reformation, Papism, religious controversy, church benefices</keywords>

# PREFACE.

<note>original: "spoke in this manner..." This continues from the previous page's argument about the nature of divine revelation.</note> spoke in this manner to any of the Prophets, or that any of their books are in verse—a style which even philosophers, according to him, scorn to use. He fails to consider the numerous examples to the contrary in the *Old Testament*, of which several significant books, besides entire songs and fragments in some of the others, are undeniably written in verse. This is true even without dwelling upon Empedocles <note>An ancient Greek pre-Socratic philosopher who wrote his philosophy in verse.</note> and a great many other poetical philosophers. 

**Ricoldus** <note>Riccoldo da Monte di Croce (c. 1243–1320), a Dominican missionary known for his polemics against Islam.</note> likewise denies that **Muhammad** <note>original: "MAHOMET"</note> could be a prophet for the whole world, for which he gives this "judicious" reason (to produce no more of his choice observations): that he wrote only in <gloss>18</gloss> *Arabic*. He forgets, or does not know, that the *New Testament* was written only in *Greek*. 

I repeat on this occasion what I said in the Preface to *NAZARENUS*: that *I take care in general not to overburden my reader with citations of any sort. I content myself with proving or illustrating my claims with no more authorities than are necessary, though I often have many others in reserve, which I judge needless or save for those who might try to answer me.* 

Events have sufficiently proven this to be true; despite my second publication <note>original: "discharge"</note>, I still have no small supply of evidence regarding all the subjects I have handled. **Doctor Mangey** <note>Thomas Mangey (1688–1755), an English clergyman who wrote a stinging critique of Toland's *Nazarenus*.</note> may go on to call them *gleanings*, and others may use their threadbare phrase of *borrowed learning*; I readily confess to the charge. I have "gleaned" and "borrowed" them in three Universities of which I have actually been a member, and in two others where I stayed for a considerable time for this very purpose, following the manner of the ancient philosophers—not to mention those I have visited briefly, or all the effort I have exerted outside of them. 

I do not publish these things out of vanity, as anyone can see, but as a necessary defense of myself against the unfair or malicious suggestions of half-witted or half-learned minor priests <note>original: "Priestlings"</note>. These men judge others by themselves—which is the very worst rule anyone can follow. It is to the moderate, serious, and learned theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> that I make my appeal, and not to those who, though they would seem to know everything, do in reality know very little. And

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of his clerical opponents, specifically attacking their lack of genuine scholarship and their political motivations for defending the Church against perceived threats. He shifts to address specific critics like William Wotton and Thomas Brett.</meta>

xiii
# PREFACE.

How should they? They seldom or never study anything, but spend the little time they have left after searching for church positions <note>original: "hunting benefices"</note> and paying servile court to those who can distribute them. Instead, they read or talk about politics, visit others or plot in secret <note>original: "caballing"</note>, learn the arts of fashion or the language of the theater, and supply their lack of theological skill with anti-theological <note>original: "atheological"; here meaning zeal that is hostile to or lacking in true divinity</note> zeal. Nevertheless, their pretense of defending the CHURCH when no one is attacking it is usually just an artificial passion (if I may call it that), politically faked to deceive the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note>, to please troublesome statesmen and clergymen, or to serve as a cover for their own envious and disparaging <note>original: "detracting"</note> humor.

IV. It is true that Mr. Wotton <note>William Wotton (1666–1727), an English theologian and scholar who engaged in the "Battle of the Books."</note>, along with some others I may mention, was not directly involved in the responses made to *NAZARENUS*. However, the <gloss>19</gloss> piece he previously wrote against me did not require a reply in my opinion, nor in the opinion of better judges. The few passages I have cited from it now are only intended as relevant examples of what I was demonstrating when I quoted them: namely, *that some people will not allow any man to be praised for anything if he disagrees with them on certain points; that they feel obligated to oppose someone in everything if they oppose them in one thing; and that there is nothing, however improbable or false, they will not say without shame or remorse to diminish or smear their adversary.* Thus, they go to great lengths to make themselves look ridiculous and provide their opponents with many opportunities for victory.

In noting what Dr. Brett <note>Thomas Brett (1667–1743), a prominent Nonjuror clergyman who argued for the necessity of tradition alongside Scripture.</note> (a person of distinguished talents <note>original: "parts"</note> and great composure) has argued against *NAZARENUS* in the preface to his book, *Tradition Necessary to Explain and Interpret the Holy Scriptures*, my intention was—besides my own defense <note>original: "apology"</note>—to defend the first and best Protestants against certain half-professed or fully-disguised Papists <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics, used here to criticize High Church Anglicans who favored rituals and doctrines similar to Catholicism.</note>. These people are restoring the concepts of priesthood, sacrifice, and altars—things that were rightly removed from the Church during the Reformation—along with exaggerated notions of spiritual ministers, oil, chrism <note>Consecrated oil used in religious rites.</note>, and the mixing of water in the sacramental cup,

# PREFACE.

Prayers and offerings <note>original: "Oblations"</note> for the dead, private confession and authoritative absolution, ornate sideboards <note>original: "gorgeous Buffets"; likely referring to elaborate displays of church plate or liturgical furniture</note> and other vain decorations, frequent bowing and making the sign of the cross, woodcuts and pictures in prayer books—especially in the public service books <note>original: "publick Offices"</note>, which often depict legendary fables. Nor could I forget how they invalidate Christian ceremonies <note>original: "ordinances"</note> performed by people who were not ordained by bishops <note>original: "Episcopally ordain'd"</note>. By doing this, they remove the "church" status—and consequently the "Christian" status—of most Protestants just to please Roman Catholics <note>original: "Papists"</note>. 

To these might be added their rebellious encroachment on the power of the government <note>original: "Magistrate"</note> by pretending that the Church is independent of the State. Finally, they impiously set themselves up in place of **GOD** by claiming ownership of anything ever dedicated to His service, no matter how unjustifiable or superstitious the reason. For example, they claim the right to restore church lands from the era of Roman Catholicism <note>original: "Popery"</note>. This "right" was modestly argued for by some of their predecessors, who never gave a reason for hoarding wealth or seizing the endowments intended to support pagan temples and the sacrifices of demons. 

Too little has been said about these matters by certain learned individuals in high positions, from whom better behavior was expected. They should not be offended if they are suspected of having the same motives until they act with more honesty and openness in this affair, or at least until they show more support for those in their profession who do so. This free and Protestant nation will not allow itself to be enslaved by ambitious deceivers <note>original: "seducers"</note> or superstitious tricksters <note>original: "sophisters"</note>, under any pretext whatsoever. Our leaders are too perceptive to be cheated out of their common sense, power, or interests by English Catholicism <note>The author refers to the "High Church" movement as a form of "English Popery"</note> any more than by Italian Catholicism. 

My account of the **LITURGY** published by some of the new "non-jurors" <note>original: "separatists"; referring to clergy who refused to swear allegiance to William III and Mary II</note> is written mostly in their own words and does not differ from their intended meaning in any way. **Dr. BRETT** <note>Thomas Brett (1667–1744), a prominent non-juror and theologian</note>, through a very strange paradox, claims that when I refer people to the "Law and the Testimony"—that is, to the New Testament alone for guidance on doctrine and discipline—I am actually plotting to undermine and destroy Christianity.

### PREFACE.

...sermons, but bad ones at that; and he insisted too much upon *Scripture*. This is a book, said the so-called <note>original: "pretended"</note> head of Christians <note>This likely refers to the Pope.</note>, to which if anyone holds strictly, he will completely ruin the *Catholic Faith*. And he certainly will—I mean the faith as it is defined by the Church of *Rome*, as well as all those initial innovations I have noticed in the recently invented <note>original: "late-coin'd"</note> *English Liturgy*. For these, there is no justification <note>original: "color"</note> or authority in the <note>Text is missing or fragmented in the original here.</note> even if there is in the old *Church Fathers*. 

Because they promote things as part of Christianity that are either outside of or against the authority of the books of the *New Testament*, I have always expressed (and I do not deny it) a total <note>original: "soverain"</note> contempt for most of the *Fathers*. This is also due to other significant reasons regarding their integrity, their knowledge, and certain other disqualifying traits. No more than three of the *Greek Fathers* from the first four centuries (if I can even strictly allow that many) knew anything about the original *Hebrew* language. Similarly, with no more than three exceptions, the *Latin Fathers* understood just as little of either Hebrew or the original *Greek*. 

Yet, contrary to common sense, these men are claimed to be the best interpreters of *Scripture*. Likewise, the *Church Councils* made up of such men are supposedly the best qualified to make unalterable decisions and *Canons* <note>Church laws or decrees.</note>, issuing terrible curses <note>original: "dire anathemas"</note> against all dissenters, even if those dissenters are masters of theology <note>original: "Jerusalem"</note> and philosophy <note>original: "Athens"</note>. 

Those *Fathers* succeeded in their task as one might expect. By using word-sounds, allusions, metaphors, vague adaptations <note>original: "accommodations"</note>, and other unreliable methods—such as assuming the very thing they were trying to prove or arguing from false premises—they failed to pay attention to the context <note>original: "scope of the place"</note> or the true meaning of the words in *Scripture*. Consequently, they could make anything mean everything, and everything mean anything. In this way, imaginative people see whatever shapes they like in the *clouds*, and claim to learn wise lessons from the ringing of bells. 

This approach might still work well enough in a sermon <note>original: "preachment"</note> given to manual laborers and uneducated women. However, it risks being scrutinized and will likely cause laughter if used in books of theological debate <note>original: "Controversy"</note> or in a *Sermon* delivered to a learned audience—unless, of course, that audience consists entirely of clergymen <note>original: "all in black cloaths"</note>. It is a total illusion and blatant logical trickery <note>original: "errant sophistry"</note> to claim (as I will demonstrate later) that because the *Fathers* lived closer to the time of the *Apostles* than we do, they are therefore better interpreters of *Scripture*. Even their own supporters cannot agree on how far their authority extends. At the very least, they claim the Fathers are the best at explaining church discipline and worship, according...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of religious authority, moving from a condemnation of high-church rituals and political overreach to an attack on the intellectual and linguistic credibility of the early Church Fathers. ## PREFACE.

...sermons, though bad ones at that. He also insisted *too much* on *Scripture*; which, according to the so-called head of Christians <note>referring to the Pope</note>, is a book that will completely ruin the *Catholic Faith* if anyone sticks closely to it. And certainly it will—I mean the faith as it is judged by the *Church of Rome*, as well as all those fancy innovations I have observed in the recently invented English Liturgy. There is no justification or authority for these things in the *New Testament*, whatever support they might find in the writings of the ancient *Fathers* <note>The "Church Fathers" were early Christian theologians whose writings are often used as authority alongside or instead of the Bible</note>. 

For this reason—of promoting anything as a part of Christianity that is outside of or against the authority of the books of the *New Testament*—I have always declared (and I do not deny it) a supreme contempt for most of the *Fathers*. I hold this view for other very significant reasons regarding their integrity, their knowledge, and certain other "disqualifying qualifications." Not more than three of the *Greek Fathers* from the first four centuries (if I am being generous enough to allow that many) knew anything of the original Hebrew. Furthermore, the *Latin Fathers*—with almost no exceptions—understood just as little of either Hebrew or the original Greek. 

Yet, despite all common sense, these men must be considered the best interpreters of *Scripture*. Likewise, the *Councils* <note>Ecumenical Councils, where church leaders gathered to set doctrine</note> made up of such men must be considered the most fit to make unalterable decisions and <term>Canons</term> <note>original: "Canons"; ecclesiastical laws or rules</note>, backed by terrifying <term>anathemas</term> <note>original: "anathemas"; formal curses or excommunications</note> against all <term>Dissenters</term> <note>those who disagree with established church doctrine</note>, even if those dissenters are masters of the languages of *Jerusalem* and *Athens* <note>meaning scholars who actually knew Hebrew and Greek</note>. 

Those *Fathers* succeeded accordingly. By using sounds, vague allusions, similes, "accommodations," and other such unreliable methods—such as assuming the very point they are trying to prove, or arguing from false premises—they interpreted anything to mean whatever they wanted, and turned nothing into everything. They never paid attention to the context of a passage or the true meaning of the words of *Scripture*. In this way, fanciful people see whatever shapes they like in the clouds, or imagine they hear wise lessons in the melodious ringing of bells. 

This approach may still work well enough in a sermon preached to day-laborers and old women; however, it runs a risk of being scrutinized and will likely cause some laughter if used in books of *Controversy* or in a *Sermon* before a learned audience—unless, of course, that audience is composed entirely of men in black clothes <note>meaning the clergy, who wore black clerical dress</note>. It is a mere illusion and total <term>sophistry</term> <note>false or deceptive reasoning</note> to say (as we will demonstrate in the proper place) that because the *Fathers* lived closer than we do to the times of the *Apostles*, they are therefore better interpreters of *Scripture*. The extent of their authority is not even agreed upon by their own defenders. At best, they are merely the explainers of church discipline and worship as practiced in their own times...

<margin>2 Corinthians 11:30</margin>
...is so far from being said out of any ostentation, that many on the contrary, who are good judges of people or matters, will apply the words of the Apostle Paul to me: *If I must boast, I will boast of the things which concern my weaknesses.* Now, whether the study of the Fathers <note>The "Church Fathers" were the influential Christian theologians and bishops of the early centuries of the Church</note> is considered disreputable or otherwise—since experts disagree, as they say—it is undoubtedly because I understand them too well (at least in my own opinion, which is all anyone has to show for their reading) and not because I have neglected to read their writings, aided by all necessary helps, that I hold such a low opinion of them, either as good interpreters of the **Scriptures**, or as reliable guides in **religion**.

Consonant to this verdict, Mr. Wotton pronounces—though not solely with respect to the Fathers—*It seems by those Letters (speaking of those to Serena <note>A reference to Toland's 1704 work, "Letters to Serena," which discussed the history of the soul and the origins of idolatry</note>) that he knows where the truth lies in every particular; and if that will not convince him, how can I?* How indeed? For I assure him that I shall immediately tip my hat <note>original: "veil my bonnet," a gesture of yielding or showing respect</note> to **truth** wherever she appears to me as such; and in those letters, I asserted nothing but what I maturely concluded to be true. I would ask Dr. Harpin and Dr. Brett to believe the same of me in what concerns my judgment of the Fathers; for I shall never be damned for willful ignorance, however much I may be mistaken after my best endeavors—which might just as easily be their own case. But I will never allow that I am actually mistaken simply because I *possibly* may be; which is the silly fallacy <note>original: "sophism"</note> of the Sceptics on one hand, and on the other, of those who would dogmatically subject **reason** to **authority**.

V. I made an appeal before, and I make one here again, to all moderate, serious, and learned theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> who are not biased: have I ever shown by any of my writings that I am an enemy to religion, though I am an avowed enemy to superstition? Religion pure and perfect, as it was originally taught, without the corrupt additions and alterations of ignorant or self-interested persons, I both profess and recommend, as will appear (to refer to no other books) from the conclusion of this very volume. I never wrote a syllable against any specific Article of the Church of England, though several of my accusers have introduced <meta>catchword: many</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of religious authorities who use their positions to claim worldly power and suppress dissent. He defines his own view of the ministry and religious liberty in opposition to those he considers "priest-ridden."</meta>

## PREFACE.

...many odd or perilous whimsies <note>original: "whimfies," meaning fanciful or erratic ideas</note> against most of them; as if they claimed a privilege to say anything, however extravagant, while others are forbidden from saying what they ought to out of duty. "Oh, but you are an enemy to **priests**," say their mindless followers <note>original: "implicit tools," referring to those who follow authority without questioning</note>. To this I answer: I acknowledge no **Christian priests** in the literal sense, nor any other **sacrifices** except those of prayer and thanksgiving offered on the **altar** of the heart. These may be offered by everyone in private, or by all in public, by devoutly joining with the person who offers a common prayer on their behalf—whether it is spontaneous or a set form. 

I respect and highly approve of such pastors and ministers of the congregation who are lawfully ordained to this role and are duly qualified, which prevents confusion in public worship—even though every Christian is figuratively both a king and a priest <note>A reference to the "priesthood of all believers" found in the New Testament (1 Peter 2:9 and Revelation 1:6)</note>. Similarly, I approve of national **churches**, which I believe are perfectly consistent with **tolerated churches**—provided that those in the latter suffer no legal disadvantages <note>original: "incapacities"</note> in civil affairs. **Persecution** (which is suffering of any degree or kind based on mere opinion) does not come from **God**; rather, it is the certain mark of a false church, at least in this regard. 

The sworn enemies of these free sentiments, who claim a status for the priesthood in the literal sense, are those who condemn every man who warns of the dominion they are laboring to establish, or who courageously opposes it. I frankly declare myself an enemy to the designs of these adversaries of civil and religious **liberty**. Nor do I wonder that they pretend **religion** or the church is in danger, so that all men (as someone has rightly observed) may be enlisted like **soldiers in their cause**. Whereas, if they showed their true private interests, few or none would risk themselves in their quarrel. 

Anyone who dares to differ from them on any point is called a **heretic**. And if a person will not join them in attacking another man's doctrine without understanding it, misrepresenting his reasons, suppressing them, attributing the worst possible consequences to him (even if he absolutely disowns them), and making him out to be a man of immoral actions or impious ideas—he, I say, who will not join in this outcry, is immediately suspected himself. His reputation is ruined as someone who does not favor their projects, and whose fairness or moderation exposes their behavior. They are so aware of their own tricks and so haunted by their guilt that they see themselves being attacked even when they have not been mentioned <note>original: "find themselves where they were never put"</note>. If a man does nothing more than compile a political romance, built altogether upon **pagan ideas** <note>original: "Heathen ideas"</note>, and mostly in the...

...the very terms of the ancients; they immediately imagine that their *Palladium* <note>In Greek mythology, a statue that protected the city of Troy; here it refers to a sacred belief or institution that supposedly protects a group from harm.</note> has been stolen and profaned by some new **Ulysses**, who intends to overthrow their **Troy**. This maxim was shamelessly defended by Father **Stapleton** <note>original: "STAPLETON," referring to Thomas Stapleton, a prominent 16th-century English Catholic controversialist.</note> and is practically supported by some who are Protestant in name only. They demand profound respect and reverence as people who have a personal acquaintance with the Deity and who, by their blessings and curses, can make men happy or miserable in a future life.*

# PREFACE.

...Troy: instead of a lance to cure, they bring a dagger to kill. <note>The author likely refers to the mythical spear of Achilles, which was said to be able to heal the wounds it inflicted. He suggests his critics have no such intention of "healing" through correction, only of destroying.</note> As for any noise they may raise, I shall not be in the least troubled by it, rarely letting any of their "flowers" <note>original: "flowers," likely referring to "flowers of rhetoric," used here sarcastically to mean their insults or elaborate arguments.</note> be lost; it is what I expect as a matter of course, and a diversion to which I am reasonably well accustomed. Neither do I value any company or assembly of such persons when I see no reason to fear any one of them individually, or to be hesitant about challenging him. To think otherwise would be to believe that a group of craftsmen <note>original: "artificers"</note> could collectively finish some extraordinary piece of work when every one of them was individually a ²⁴ bungler.

It has long since been a rule for me that just as **ERROR** and **FALSEHOOD** may be disproved as well by one *man* as by one *thousand*, so **REASON** and **TRUTH** have no more to fear from one *thousand* than from one *man*. As long as I obey the *laws* of my country, which shall always be sacred to me, I have nothing to fear (however little I may gain) for making whatever inquiries, discoveries, or improvements I may be able, whether in letters or otherwise. Nor am I worried that the malicious acts of *Bigots*—who would gladly see their opponents starve, according to their "praiseworthy" custom in all ages—can be any obstacle to my fortune, as I am not ambitious for either rank or title, nor desirous of any office or promotion.

I despise the vanity of such unreliable possessions as much as I hate the dependence they inevitably create. Let those who form other ideas of things, and who have not thoroughly reflected on the favors of fortune, suppose what they please; I would not exchange that **LIBERTY**, for which I have been ever strenuously pleading on my own behalf and that of my fellow creatures, for the highest dignities and honors in the world. **NATURE IS CONTENT WITH A LITTLE**, and he wants least who has fewest desires, or keeps the strongest control over his passions. Books and bread enough, with select company in a pleasant retirement <note>In this context, "retirement" refers to a quiet life of study and reflection away from the pressures of public office or city life.</note>, give that solid satisfaction which is fruitlessly expected from the scraping together or hoarding of treasure, or from anxiously counting or recklessly spending it. There I shall contribute my best efforts for the good of mankind, and never do anything to disturb the public peace, which is the nurse of the common welfare. For the *Peace* which the Deceivers mean is a stupid and listless resignation of men’s understanding to others, who undertake to think for them. For this reason, these "Undertakers" <note>Those who take it upon themselves to manage the thoughts of others.</note> are against all examination, lest their own thoughts should be found bad or of little worth, and so men should not pay them wages to think for them when they might think perhaps much better for themselves. The true clergyman <note>original: "Divine"</note>, who is always as true a Patriot, acts quite the opposite of all this: appealing to men’s understanding for the truth of whatever he proposes for their belief or practice, and constantly exhorting them to use their Reason, to stand in defense of their Religion and Liberty against all spiritual or worldly <note>original: "temporal"</note> usurpations. 

To draw toward a conclusion, I admit as much as anyone that the abuse of a thing ought not to take away the use of it, though an excess on one hand has often thrown people into an excess on the other. Nor is *Divinity* <note>Theology or the clerical profession.</note> the only profession that has suffered in this manner, as may be learned from the following old *poem*; which, though very plain in both style and meter, is yet cleverly concise <note>original: "nicely epigrammatical"</note> and contains a beautiful *climax*.

# PREFACE.

...destroy; instead of a surgical lance used to heal, they bring a dagger to kill. As for any outcry they might raise, I will not be in the least disturbed by it; I rarely let any of their rhetorical flourishes <note>original: "flowers"</note> go to waste. It is exactly what I expect, and it is a distraction to which I am reasonably well accustomed. Nor do I value the collective opinion of such a group, since I see no reason to fear any one of them individually, nor would I be hesitant to challenge them one-on-one. To think otherwise would be like believing that a club of craftsmen could jointly finish an extraordinary masterpiece when every one of them individually was an amateur <note>original: "bungler"</note>. 

It has long been my personal maxim that just as **error** and **falsehood** *can be proven wrong by one man as easily as by a thousand*, so **reason** and **truth** *have no more to fear from a thousand people than from one man*. As long as I obey the laws of my country—which I will always hold sacred—I have nothing to fear (no matter how little profit I make) for pursuing whatever inquiries, discoveries, or improvements I can achieve, whether in literature or other fields. Nor am I worried that the malicious efforts of bigots <note>In this context, those stubbornly or intolerantly devoted to their own opinions and prejudices.</note>—who would gladly see their opponents starve, as has been their "praiseworthy" custom throughout history—can block my success. I am not ambitious for rank or title, nor do I desire any high office or promotion <note>original: "preferment"</note>. 

I despise the vanity of such unstable possessions as much as I hate the dependency they inevitably create. Let those who have different ideas, and who have not thoroughly reflected on the whims <note>original: "caprices"</note> of luck, imagine what they please. I would not exchange that **liberty**—for which I have always strenuously advocated on my own behalf and for my fellow human beings—for the highest honors and distinctions in the world. **Nature is content with little**, and he who has the fewest desires, or maintains the strongest control over his passions, lacks the least. Having enough books and bread, along with chosen company in a pleasant retreat <note>original: "Retirement"; meaning a quiet life away from the public eye.</note>, provides that solid satisfaction which is sought in vain by scraping or hoarding treasure, or by anxiously counting it or spending it lavishly. Therefore, I prefer the innocent amusements of the country (where alone real pleasures are...
