# PREFACE.

Therefore, by right, he claims
To have the superiority.
MEDICINE <note>original: "Physick"</note> prescribes remedies <note>original: "recepts"</note> for health,
Which men value more than their wealth:
Therefore, by right, he claims
To have the superiority.
Then immediately steps up the modest <note>original: "demure"</note> PRIEST,
Who takes care and cure of men's souls:
Therefore, by right, he claims
To have the superiority.

If JUDGES decide this TRIPLE PLEA,
The LAWYERS shall hold all the power.
If EMPIRICS <note>original: "Empirics" — at this time, medical practitioners who relied on experience rather than formal theory, often used as a derogatory term for quacks.</note> give their verdict,
PHYSICIANS will thrive best of all.
If BISHOPS judge the case,
The PRIESTS must have the highest place.
If HONEST, SOBER, and WISE MEN judge,
Then ALL THREE of them may pack their bags <note>original: "trudge"</note>.

For let men live in peace and love,
And they need not experience the LAWYERS' tricks:
Let them avoid excess and riotous living,
And they need not survive on a DOCTOR'S diet.
Let them listen to what GOD teaches,
And they need not care what PARSONS preach.
But if men choose to be FOOLS and KNAVES,
They will be ridden like an ass <note>original: "asse-ridden"</note> by ALL THREE.

I shall add no more, except that I intended to publish the main part of the fourth *Dissertation* over a year ago, along with the *Treatise of Tradition*. Upon second thought, the latter has been delayed for a few months; I judged that the former would arrive at an appropriate time at the end of the three *Dissertations* which I am now sending out into the world. Regarding the whole work, I have inserted the original sources for all the citations of the first and last pieces in the inner margin. This could not be done for the second and third pieces without making the notes nearly as long as the text itself. However, the locations where they appear in the books from which I have extracted them are so clearly marked that they will be easily found by anyone who wishes to verify them.

# HODEGUS:
<note>original: "Hodegus" — a Greek word meaning "The Guide."</note>

## OR,

### *The Pillar of Cloud and Fire*
### *not Miraculous.*

<margin>I.</margin>
<image-desc>An ornamental initial capital 'I' set within a square woodcut border, depicting two robed figures standing in a scene with architectural or natural elements.</image-desc>
I HAVE often wondered why many people, deservedly famous for their learning <note>original: "literature"</note> and refinement <note>original: "politeness"</note>, have in a way expressly neglected the history of the most ancient and famous nation of the Jews. At the same time, they devoted a great deal of effort, and showed no less discernment, in their inquiries concerning Greek, Roman, and other antiquities. At first, I thought the reason was the small size of the Jewish territories, the lack <note>original: "sterility"</note> of their warlike exploits (compared to those nations I have mentioned), and their ignorance of arts and useful inventions—unrewarding subjects for pleasure or instruction. But however just this criticism...

<summary>In this opening, the author questions why scholars of his era focus heavily on Greek and Roman history while neglecting Jewish history, despite the latter's antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Jewish history, antiquities, Hodegus, Greek history, Roman history, scholarship, biblical criticism</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 3, where he began questioning why the history of the Jews is neglected by secular scholars despite its significance and connections to other great ancient civilizations.</meta>

->HODEGUS.<-

...may be regarding the Jews in certain details, yet their affairs were at all times closely linked either with those of the Egyptians, or the Assyrians, or the Persians, or the Phoenicians, or the Arabians. These were nations of which some were not inferior to the Greeks in learning, but rather surpassed them in that as well as in commerce; others equaled the Romans in feats of arms no less than in the arts of government; and all of them went far beyond both the Greeks and the Romans in terms of antiquity. Not to mention that the Jews, in the course of time, had (as everyone knows) matters of the greatest importance to handle with these same Greeks and Romans, to whom they were also subjects or tributaries <note>original: "tributaries" — nations that pay tax or tribute to a dominant power</note> in their turns. It is strange, therefore, that they should be so much neglected!

<margin>II.</margin>
BUT experience taught me at last that the true reason why Judea has remained so uncultivated by the laity <note>original: "laity" — those who are not members of the clergy; secular scholars</note> is that the clergy has completely monopolized <note>original: "ingrossing"</note> that province for a long time for themselves, yet they spent neither sufficient labor nor money on its improvement. This has made a soil appear very barren that is otherwise fruitful enough and capable of rewarding the industry of a wise critic. Just as some nations, the better to keep their mines for themselves, reported they were haunted by frightful dragons or infested with poisonous vapors, so these clergymen proceeded with no less cunning—and they often used violence—to discourage others from the study of the ancient Jewish books. They made it a sacrilege even to peek into them without their permission. They claimed that reading them would drive men mad <note>original: "turn men's heads"</note> and fill them with strange delusions. Indeed, we all know that eventually they completely and entirely forced them out of the hands...

<summary>The author argues that Jewish history is vital due to its links with other great ancient powers, but claims it has been neglected because the clergy intentionally discouraged secular study of Jewish texts to maintain their own control.</summary>
<keywords>Judea, ancient civilizations, clergy vs. laity, scholarship, censorship, Jewish books, historical context</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 4, where the author argued that the clergy used various tactics to discourage the laity from studying ancient Jewish texts. This page explains how the clergy maintained control over the interpretation of the Bible through structured systems and how the common perception of the Old Testament as a book of miracles has deterred serious scholarly inquiry.</meta>

# HODEGUS.
<note>original: "Hodegus" — a Greek word meaning "Guide" or "Leader." This was a common title for works intended to lead a reader through a difficult subject.</note>

...hands of the laity for many ages. And though, since Martin Luther’s time, those in the northern regions of Europe have forcibly recovered them for common <note>original: "promiscuous" — here meaning undiscriminating or general</note> use, they must still mostly read them through the spectacles of their own priests. They guess at their meaning using certain rules created by these priests (not to their own disadvantage, certainly) called *Systems* and *Formularies*, to which all things must necessarily be reduced, forced <note>original: "wiredrawn" — strained or pulled out with excessive subtlety</note>, and adapted, in both content and expression. These are excellent methods for meditating without ideas, speaking without thinking, and knowing everything in the *BIBLE* without reading a single word of it.

But nothing has contributed more to creating a dislike among noble-minded people for studying the *Old Testament* than the belief, adopted without question since childhood, that it is entirely a collection of incomprehensible things and a complete system of miracles. They believe these are not proper subjects for <term>criticism</term> <note>original: "criticism" — in this context, the scholarly analysis and evaluation of texts</note>, nor a broad enough field for <term>philosophical inquiries</term> <note>original: "disquisitions" — formal inquiries or discussions</note>. And it is indeed amazing to consider the power of upbringing in this matter. Miracles are undoubtedly recorded there, yet they are comparatively very few. I am being overly cautious when I claim that not even one-third of what are commonly thought to be miracles in the *Pentateuch* <note>original: "Pentateuch" — the first five books of the Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy</note> (for example) actually are—and the same proportion applies to the other books. To avoid all <term>ambiguity</term> or <term>word-strife</term> <note>original: "logomachy" — a dispute or argument about words</note>, I mean that the writers of those books neither recorded these events as miracles themselves, nor intended for them to be understood as such by others.

III. NOW I expect that people will immediately ask for examples, as the only adequate proof of this claim. The demand is extremely reasonable...

<summary>The author argues that the clergy maintains control over the Bible through rigid systems of interpretation and asserts that most events in the Pentateuch commonly viewed as miracles were not intended as such by the original authors.</summary>
<keywords>clerical control, Martin Luther, Systems and Formularies, Old Testament, miracles, Pentateuch, biblical criticism, logomachy</keywords>

# HODECUS.
<note>original: "Hodegus" (OCR: Hodecus) — a Greek word meaning "Guide" or "Leader." This was a common title for works intended to lead a reader through a difficult subject.</note>

...while this may be true of the Jews in certain details, their affairs were at all times intimately linked either with those of the Egyptians, the Assyrians, the Persians, the Phoenicians, or the Arabians. These were nations where some were not inferior to the Greeks in learning—and indeed surpassed them in both knowledge and commerce—while others equaled the Romans in military feats as well as the arts of government. All of them far exceeded both the Greeks and the Romans in antiquity. This is not even to mention that the Jews, in the course of time, had (as everyone knows) very important matters to handle with these same Greeks and Romans, to whom they were likewise subjects or <term>tributaries</term> <note>original: "tributaries" — nations that pay a tax or tribute to a more powerful state</note> in their turn. It is strange, therefore, that they should be so much neglected!

## II.

But experience finally taught me that the true reason why Judaea has remained so unexamined by the <term>laity</term> <note>original: "laity" — people who are not members of the clergy or professional religious orders</note> is that the clergy has completely monopolized that field for a long time, yet they have invested neither enough effort nor resources into its study. This has made a subject appear very barren that is otherwise quite fruitful and capable of rewarding the hard work of a judicious critic. 

Just as some nations, to keep their mines for themselves, reported they were haunted by terrifying dragons or infested with poisonous vapors, these clergymen acted with similar cunning. They even often used violence to discourage others from studying ancient Jewish books. They declared it a <term>sacrilege</term> <note>original: "sacrilege" — a violation or misuse of what is regarded as sacred</note> even to glance at them without their permission. They claimed that reading them would confuse people and fill them with strange ideas. In fact, we all know that eventually, they completely snatched them out of the hands

## HODEGUS.

...hands of the laity for many ages. And though, since Martin Luther’s time, those in Northern Europe have forcibly reclaimed them for general use, they must still, for the most part, read them through the lenses of their own priests. They guess at their meaning according to certain rules framed by these priests (certainly not to the priests' own disadvantage) called *Systems and Formularies*. <note>original: "Systems and Formularies" — structured sets of doctrines and official statements of belief, such as creeds or catechisms, used by churches to standardize how the Bible is interpreted.</note> To these, all things must necessarily be reduced, stretched, and adapted, both in subject matter and expression. These are excellent shortcuts for meditating without ideas, speaking without thinking, and "knowing" everything in the BIBLE without reading a single word of it.

But nothing has contributed so much to creating a dislike among noble spirits for the study of the *Old Testament* as a belief, taken up unquestioningly from childhood, that it is entirely a series of incomprehensible events and a complete system of miracles. They believe these are not a proper subject for criticism, nor a broad field for philosophical investigations. It is indeed amazing to consider the power of education in this case. Miracles are undoubtedly recorded there, yet they are comparatively very few. I am speaking with too much caution when I state that not even a third of the events in the *Pentateuch* <note>original: "Pentateuch" — the first five books of the Bible, attributed to Moses.</note> (for example)—or what are commonly thought to be miracles—actually are such; the same applies in proportion to the other books. To avoid all ambiguity or <term>logomachy</term> <note>original: "logomachy" — a dispute about words or language rather than the underlying ideas.</note>, I mean that the authors of those books neither recorded such things as miracles themselves, nor intended they should be understood that way by others.

# HODEGUS.
<note>original: "HODEGUS" — A Greek word meaning "Guide" or "Leader." This is the title of the first treatise in John Toland's book *Tetradymus* (1720).</note>

...reasonable, and I readily acknowledge that I am obligated to answer it. It is well known that I have already promised ¹ **THE COMMONWEALTH OF MOSES**, which I consider the most excellent and perfect of all forms of government. However, I will provide such a new perspective (yet the genuine and true one) not only to the entire political system and several specific laws of this incomparable legislator [Moses], but also to many of the circumstances and historical incidents in the shortened—and therefore in several ways obscure—account found in the *Pentateuch* <note>original: "Pentateuch" — the first five books of the Bible.</note>. Because of this, I find it highly necessary to publish a short sample of my work beforehand, if only to prevent surprise and to help my readers get used to these types of explanations. 

The subject of the sample I am choosing now is just a detail; it specifically concerns the *Pillar of Cloud and Fire* mentioned so often in the *Old Testament*. This is almost never mentioned by others except as a stupendous wonder <note>original: "Prodigy" — an amazing or unusual thing, often interpreted as a supernatural omen or miracle.</note>, if not the greatest and most long-lasting of all miracles. Therefore, in this current *Dissertation* <note>original: "Dissertation" — a formal essay or treatise on a particular subject.</note>, I prove through reasoning and undeniable facts that it was a *Pillar of Smoke*, and not an actual *Cloud*, that guided the Israelites in the wilderness. I also show that there were not two pillars (as most people believe), but one and the same *Pillar*, directing their march with the *Cloud* of its *Smoke* by day and with the *Light of its Fire* by night. 

To further illustrate my subject, I show that there was no miracle <note>original: "Prodigy"</note> involved in this matter, and that *FIRE* was used for the same purposes by other Eastern <note>original: "oriental"</note> nations. It did not move among the Jews, any more than among those other nations, on its own accord...

<meta>This page continues Toland’s argument that the "Pillar of Cloud and Fire" described in the Exodus was not a supernatural phenomenon, but a standard military practice of the time. <sig>A 4</sig>

[smok]e; not [con]ducted <note>The text likely reads "conducted," meaning guided or moved along.</note> or miraculously moved, as is no less needlessly than absurdly imagined; but carried in proper machines of mere human contrivance, which might well be called *ambulatory Beacons* <note>original: "ambulatory Beacons" — mobile or walking signal fires.</note>.

IV. THE reason for such a *portable fire* is this: In well-inhabited countries, the route of armies—even when extending very wide in the front for the convenience of foraging or travel—is marked by milestones <note>original: "milliary stones"</note>, posts, rivers, hills, cities, villages, castles, and other notable landmarks. Thus, they know by their orders how far they may spread out and where they must gather back together to form a single camp or body. 

But in vast and uninhabited <note>original: "unpractis’d" — unused or unfamiliar.</note> deserts, where there are no buildings, no famous hills, no frequent rivers, or even the ruins of ancient buildings, it is necessary to have a visible guide leading the main body. By this guide, the wings <note>original: "wings" — the side flanks of an army.</note> may coordinate their march and stay within a convenient distance so as not to wander off or be lost, and to know instantly when the army stops or sets up camp. 

Now, there is no silent signal in the world that can perform this at all times except for FIRE alone; since the *cloud of its smoke* is (as everyone knows) seen from very far by day, just as the *light of its flame* is no less conspicuous by night. I say "at all times" because in deserts of shifting sands—of which there are many in Africa and quite a few in Asia—armies or caravans traveling through them were forced to do so only in the night time. This was not merely to avoid the scorching heat of the sun, which becomes intolerable when reflected from the sand, but also because all previous tracks were usually swept away or filled in by the blowing of strong winds. They had no landmarks to guide them, and therefore they steered (as we may say) by the stars,

QUINTUS CURTIUS <note>original: "TUS CURTIUS"; refers to Quintus Curtius Rufus, a Roman historian who wrote a history of Alexander the Great.</note> writes of the *Bactrian* <note>Bactria was an ancient region in Central Asia, mostly in modern-day Afghanistan and Uzbekistan.</note> deserts in *Asia*. A great part of that country (*says he*) is covered by barren sands; and being parched by heat, it affords nourishment for neither men nor vegetables. But when the winds blow from the *Pontic* sea <note>The Black Sea.</note>, they sweep before them all the sands that lie on the plains; which, when heaped together, look from afar like great mountains, while all footsteps of former travelers are quite vanished <note>original: "abolish'd"</note>. Therefore, those who pass over those plains do, like *sailors*, observe the *stars* in the night, and steer their course by their motion; the twilight <note>original: "shade of the night"</note> being there almost clearer than the day. Therefore, this region is impassable by day because men find no tracks to follow, and the *stars* are then invisible. But now the *compass* renders such places traversable <note>original: "practicable"</note> by day, as we are just going to see.

V. In other deserts that are not so sandy, where tracks remain for some time and are occasionally inhabited, and where men who have long lived or been raised <note>original: "bred"</note> in them know the ways, the passage is easier. But yet for a great multitude, there must be, as we said, some visible *guide* preceding the main body, whereby the rest may know what distance to keep, where to stop, and when to proceed. Nor is it unworthy of our notice that the invention of the *compass* has proved beneficial to travelers on land as well as to those at sea; for by this means, people may now travel over those deserts by day, which they could not do before except by night. In this way, according to the signals and orders given to the army, they could know when to march or to halt, depending on whether it moved or stayed still. I am now going to show, as I promised, that the *Pillar of Cloud and Fire* was of this kind.

However, in doing this, I sincerely declare—and the outcome will prove this even to the most skeptical—that my intention is as much to do justice to Moses (whom I respect deeply, as I have said many times) as it is to clarify a historical fact in a very ancient book. The credibility of our holy religion is founded on this book, and an inquirer into ancient customs and manners—even among the greatest non-believers—will admit that it deserves explanation no less than the works of Herodotus <note>original: "Herodotus"; an ancient Greek historian known for documenting the customs of various nations.</note> or Livy <note>original: "Livy"; a Roman historian who wrote a comprehensive history of Rome.</note>.

I also promise that I will not deceive my readers by using the tricks of certain men (whose profession should actually prevent them from using such tricks) who make "everything out of anything" and "anything out of nothing." By this, I mean I will not resort to switching from a literal meaning to an allegorical, tropological, or anagogical sense of *Scripture* <note>These terms refer to the "four-fold sense" of medieval biblical interpretation: literal (historical), allegorical (symbolic), tropological (moral), and anagogical (spiritual/mystical).</note>.

I detest and despise these maneuvers <note>original: "finesses"</note>, which would not be tolerated even when explaining the most secular author. They are especially unwelcome when used in a book we consider sacred, and which needs them less than any other. In truth, these "squeezing engines" <note>The author's metaphor for forced or strained interpretations that "squeeze" meanings out of text.</note> are not built to soften anything in the book, but rather to find things in it that are not there—or, if they are there, to change the true meaning or even deny the facts entirely. Indeed, if the *Scripture* itself did not support me, I would not be so bold as to base any...

### VII. 
Thus, to stay with our current subject for examples, **flame** and **smoke**, naturally rising, are therefore called **Pillars**; just as not only smoke, but even dust, is very significantly called a **Cloud**. <margin>Hebrews 12:1</margin> **Quintus Curtius** <note>original: "Quintus Curtius"; a Roman historian who wrote a biography of Alexander the Great.</note> uses the phrase <gloss>9</gloss> *a cloud of dust that mounted up to the sky*; and, by a bolder figure of speech, we read of *a cloud of witnesses* in the *Epistle to the Hebrews*. Even water is called a pillar. **Pliny** <note>original: "Pliny"; referring to Pliny the Elder, a Roman author and natural philosopher.</note>, describing a waterspout which is often seen at sea, says it is called <gloss>10</gloss> *a pillar, when the water, condensing and standing upright, is sustained by itself.* For a similar reason, **Lucretius** <note>original: "Lucretius"; a Roman poet and philosopher.</note> compares a certain fiery wind or tempest (called by the Greeks <margin>Prester <note>original: Πρηστήρ; a Greek term for a fiery whirlwind or waterspout.</note></margin> *Prester*) to <gloss>11</gloss> *a pillar descending from the sky.* 

The thick <margin>Ezekiel 8:11 and 10:4</margin> cloud of **incense** in *Ezekiel* is something that no one, I suppose, will deny is a **cloud of smoke**, since incense can only form such a cloud by its smoke when burning. After Joshua had set the city of Ai on fire by a strategic maneuver, and it is said that the <margin>Joshua 8:20, 21</margin> *smoke of the city ascended up to heaven*, I hope nobody will deny that it might then be justly compared to both a **Pillar** and a **Cloud**. Yet, lest anyone should, out of stubbornness or ignorance, deny something so evident, we find express mention made in the *Old Testament* of **Pillars of smoke**. When Gibeah...

anywhere on metaphors, though they are common to all languages; for since the original names of things are few, the rest are necessarily created from similarity or by comparison.

VII. Thus, not to leave the subject before us for examples, *flame* and *smoke*, naturally ascending, are therefore called *pillars*; just as not only *smoke*, but even *dust*, is very significantly called a *cloud*. Quintus Curtius <note>A Roman historian known for his history of Alexander the Great.</note> uses the phrase of a **cloud of dust, which was carried toward the sky** <note>original: "Nubes pulveris, quae ad coelum ferebatur." Even *water* is called a *pillar*. Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman author and natural philosopher.</note>, describing a *waterspout* (which is often seen at sea), says, **"it is called a pillar when the water, thickening and standing upright, sustains itself."** <note>original: "Vocatus & Columna, cum Spiffatus humor, rigensque, ipse se sustinet." VII. Thus, to stay with our current subject for examples, *flame* and *smoke*—which naturally rise—are for that reason called *pillars*; just as not only *smoke*, but even *dust*, is very meaningfully called a *cloud*. Quintus Curtius <note>a Roman historian of Alexander the Great</note> uses the phrase <gloss>9</gloss> *a cloud of dust that rose up to the sky* <note>original Latin: "Nubes pulveris, quae ad coelum ferebatur"</note>. By a bolder figure of speech, we read of *a cloud of witnesses* in the *Epistle to the Hebrews*. Even *water* is called a *pillar*. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author and naturalist</note>, describing a waterspout which is often seen at sea, says it is called <gloss>10</gloss> *a pillar, when the water thickens and, standing upright, supports itself* <note>original Latin: "Vocatus & Columna, cùm Spissatus humor, rigensque, ipse se sustinet"</note>. For the same reason, Lucretius <note>a Roman poet and philosopher</note> compares a certain fiery wind or tempest—called a *prester* by the Greeks—to <gloss>11</gloss> *a pillar descending from the sky* <note>original Latin: "Nam fit, ut interdum, tanquam demissa Columna In mare de coelo descendat"</note>. I suppose no one will deny that the thick cloud of *incense* mentioned in *Ezekiel* is actually a *cloud of smoke*, since incense can only form such a cloud through the smoke it produces while burning. After Joshua used a strategy to set the city of *Ai* on fire, and it is said that the *smoke of the city rose up to heaven*, I hope no one would deny that it could then be justly compared to both a *pillar* and a *cloud*. Yet, lest anyone should deny something so obvious out of stubbornness or ignorance, we find the *Old Testament* explicitly mentions *pillars of smoke*. When Gibeah

VIII. To know whether this is so or not, let us recall what we said before about how armies or caravans pass through vast deserts—historically guided by the stars, and more recently by the compass. IX. Their first station was in Rameses, the second in Succoth, and the third in Etham. Up to this point, the country was passable <note>original: "practicable"</note> and well-inhabited, so they could direct their march by known towns and other recognizable landmarks. Thus far, there was no need for steering by the stars, or by the compass (had it been in use then), or by other similar methods. Indeed, we do not read of any such methods used in these marches, whether ordinary or extraordinary. But at Etham began the wilderness of the Red Sea, just as on the other side a vast and terrifying <note>original: "horrible"</note> wilderness continued, where a visible guide became absolutely necessary. Even Moses was not enough, though he had lived there for many years; for something had to be seen by the entire army and managed in such a way as to provide certain signals. By these, they might all halt or proceed at once (as explained before) and know when they were to camp, where to stay for a significant time in one place, and when to leave it again. 

These signals (which are now performed by cannons both at sea and on land) were no less necessary to the Israelites by night than by day, because haste or other requirements sometimes forced them to march in the night. Being, therefore, in Etham on the edge of the wilderness, the thing appointed for making signals was **FIRE**, whose **flame** (but not its *smoke*) is seen from very far away at night, just as its **smoke** (though not its *flame*) is perceived at a great distance by day. Before I come to specific proofs from Scripture, I shall briefly describe the manner of managing this **FIRE**. It was under the direction of a designated officer and held high in a certain machine on a pole, which was carried...

->### HODEGUS.<-
<note>original: "HODEGUS." This title comes from the Greek word *hodegos*, meaning a guide or leader.</note>

<margin>Ibid. 10. 1—10. <note>original: "Ibid." (Latin: *ibidem*), meaning "in the same place." This refers back to the Book of Numbers, chapter 10, verses 1 through 10.</note></margin>
...carried before the first line of the army, from where it could be seen by all the rest. But as soon as the *Tabernacle* was made and set up (which was the *Tent* where God, as King, was present through His symbols and His ministers), then this fire was immediately placed on the top of it, and so became visible to the whole army. They did have trumpets made of silver, designed for distinct signals—as you read in *Numbers*—for the *calling of the Assembly* and the *movement of the camps*. But as these could not possibly be heard throughout the entire camp, which consisted of over 600,000 men, the signals were instead provided by the *Flame* and the *Smoke* as follows: As long as the Fire continued on the top of the *Tabernacle*, whether for days, months, or years, they remained in the camp. But whenever it was taken off, whether by night or by day, then they immediately moved and began their march after it until it was set again on the top of the *Tabernacle*. This was the known sign for pitching their tents, of which they were undoubtedly informed by officers assigned to watch for that purpose.

X. Now, before I come to the proofs I promised from *Scripture*, I think it is proper here to give some instances showing that this guidance by *Fire*—so natural in such places and so convenient <note>original: "commodious"</note>—was practiced by other nations. This was particularly true of the *Persians*, who not only had great wildernesses in their own and in the neighboring territories, but also made frequent invasions <note>original: "irruptions"</note> into *Egypt* through this very wilderness where the *Israelites* wandered up and down for forty years. It was not as if the Israelites did not know the shorter route into the land of *Canaan*, but for very practical <note>original: "expedient"</note> reasons (which will be read in the *Introduction* to our *REPUBLIC OF MOSES*) they turned away from...

->### HODEGUS.<-

from the known road. To begin with the most recent example, when **ALEXANDER** waged war in Asia against the Persians, it is recorded of him that he successfully changed many things in the discipline of his army, departing from the customs used by his ancestors. For no wise man will refuse to learn from his greatest enemy; moreover, circumstances will often force a General to change his usual behavior. Among the other changes made by **ALEXANDER**, one is recorded in these words:

*“When he wanted the camp to move, the signal was given by a trumpet; however, its sound was often not clearly heard because of the confused noise of the multitude. Therefore, he ordered a pole, which could be seen from all directions, to be placed on top of the General’s tent. On this pole, a signal was hung that was visible to everyone; specifically, **FIRE** was watched for by night, and **SMOKE** by day.”*

I appeal here to even the most prejudiced reader: could two drops of water be more alike than these signals used by **ALEXANDER** and **MOSES**? Both used trumpets, and both used fire and smoke. If we had no other proof, we should naturally conclude that there was no more of a miracle in the one than in the other. But we will not need to rely solely on this point. In the meantime, we should notice that **ALEXANDER** learned this from the Persians, among whom *fire* was raised high above the Royal tent—for no other purpose, in my opinion, says the equally wise and learned...

<meta>This page continues the argument that Alexander the Great’s use of fire and smoke signals was not a unique invention but was modeled after the military traditions of the Persians, which in turn parallels the biblical account of the pillar of cloud and fire.</meta>

->### HODEGUS.<-
<note>original: "HODEGUS." This title comes from the Greek word *hodegos*, meaning a guide or leader.</note>

It is **FREINSHEMIUS** <note>Johannes Freinshemius (1608–1660) was a German classical scholar famous for his "supplements" or reconstructions of lost portions of ancient histories like those of Quintus Curtius Rufus.</note> who believes it was intended to be a signal either for a march or a battle. For although a signal was given by a trumpet, it could not be heard by the whole army, especially one consisting of such a multitude of people. Thus, **ALEXANDER** seems, in the change mentioned by **QUINTUS CURTIUS**, to have borrowed his practice from here. We are now going to prove that this was no random conjecture.

XI. THE military discipline of the Persians, which they used in all ages, is described by **QUINTUS CURTIUS** <note>A 1st-century Roman historian who wrote the "Histories of Alexander the Great."</note> in his account of the march of **DARIUS** against **ALEXANDER**. This account agrees in most details with the report of **HERODOTUS** many ages before him. *“It has been a custom,”* he says, *“passed down to the Persians from their ancestors, to begin their march after sunrise. When it grew to be clear day, the signal was given by a trumpet from the KING's tent. On the top of this tent the image of the SUN, enclosed in crystal, shone so bright”*

***

<sup>15</sup>. <note>original Latin: "Porro hanc imaginem non alio fini super tabernaculum Regis fuisse arbitror, quàm ut signum esset vel profectionis vel pugnae. Quanquam enim signum buccina dabatur, exaudiri ab universo exercitu non poterat, praesertim in tanta hominum multitudine. Ut vel hinc mutuatus videri possit ALEXANDER institum suum, quod narrat CURTIUS."</note> “Furthermore, I believe this image was on top of the King’s tent for no other purpose than to be a signal for either departure or battle. For although the signal was given by a trumpet, it could not be heard by the entire army, especially in such a great multitude of people. So that even from here, **ALEXANDER** may be seen to have borrowed his institution, which **CURTIUS** relates.” *In the annotations to chapter 2, book 5 of QUINTUS CURTIUS.*

<sup>16</sup>. *Book 7, chapter 40.* <note>Referring to Herodotus.</note>

<sup>17</sup>. <note>original Latin: "Patrio more Persarum traditum est, orto demum sole procedere. Die jam illustri signum e tabernaculo Regis buccinâ dabatur. Super Tabernaculum, unde ab omnibus conspici posset, imago Solis crystallo inclusa fulgebat. Ordo autem agminis erat talis: ignis, quem ipsi sacrum et aeternum vocabant, argenteis altaribus praeferebatur. Magos trecenti et sexaginta quatuor, municis amicibus velati, diebus totius anni pares numero: quippe Persis quoque in totidem dies descriptus est annus. Currum deinde, Jovi sacratum, albentes vehebant equi. Hos eximiae magnitudinis equi, quem Solis vocabant, sequebatur."</note> “It is a tradition according to the ancestral custom of the Persians to proceed only when the sun has risen. When the day was already bright, the signal was given from the King’s tent by a trumpet. Above the tent, where it could be seen by everyone, an image of the Sun enclosed in crystal shone. The order of the procession was as follows: fire, which they themselves called sacred and eternal, was carried before on silver altars. Then three hundred and sixty-four Magi <note>Ancient Persian priests or wise men.</note> followed, veiled in white cloaks, equal in number to the days of the whole year; for among the Persians also, the year is divided into that many days. Next, white horses drew the chariot dedicated to **JUPITER**. These were followed by a horse of extraordinary size, which they called the Horse of the Sun.” *Book 3, chapter 3.*

It <margin>Exodus 32:39 <note>The author likely refers here to the descriptions of the Tabernacle found in Exodus chapters 33 or 39-40.</note></margin> *was* in *effect* the *Tent* wherein *JEHOVAH*, *the King* of the *Israelites*, was *present* by his *Symbols* and *Ministers*. *Sacred Fire* was carried *before* these *Nations*, which, though *acknowledged* *by* both to be a *Symbol* of the *Divinity* (as *Fire* *was* kept by *both unextinguished* in their *holy* *places*, and *affirmed* to have been *originally* kindled... <note>catchword: "kindl'd"</note>)

In short, I will show in my *MOSAIC REPUBLIC* many more similarities <note>original: "conformities"</note> between the Jews and the Persians, just as others have done between them and the Egyptians. For even before the Babylonian captivity, the Jews did not only sacrifice in high places like the Persians, but <margin>2 Kings 23:11</margin> they also had the consecrated horses and chariots of the Sun, for which the biblical commentators give no satisfactory <note>original: "tolerable"</note> reason.

XIII. The very reason and purpose for raising the signal so high was so it could be seen and understood by everyone. This practice was used by the Jews as well as the Persians, and also by ALEXANDER the Great during his wars in Asia. The same purpose was served among the Persians by a lamp placed in a crystal lantern. But just as it does not follow that the lamp over the tent of **Darius**, or the fire and smoke over **Alexander's** tent, lit or moved on their own—but are assumed to have been managed by appropriate officers—the same assumption should naturally be made concerning the fire over the tent of **Jehovah**. This is a certain sign that he commanded the *Cloud* as Commander-in-Chief <note>original: "Generalissimo"</note>, regardless of who had its immediate direction. Nor is it less obvious, as we have just been saying, that the actions of <term>MOSES</term>, <term>JOSHUA</term>, and other representatives are attributed to <term>GOD</term>, the King of <term>Israel</term>, in thousands of places. This has been the style in all ages, and it continues to be today with regard to monarchs and sovereigns. 

But because <term>Jews</term> and <term>Christians</term> are constantly reading about the *Fire* or *Cloud* going before and coming behind (more on which shortly), or about it resting on the top or before the door of the Tabernacle, they immediately think it moved on its own. They assume this merely because the details of how it was managed are omitted in that summary of the <term>MOSAIC</term> *History* we call the *Pentateuch* <note>the first five books of the Bible</note>. Men of immense learning, most of them theologians <note>original: "divines"</note> by profession, have undeniably proved the *Pentateuch* to be a real summary (though an infinitely valuable one) of some larger history of the Israelites. Among these, I am not sure if I should include my once-honored teacher <term>TRIGLANDIUS</term>, the worthy father of the current, equally worthy professor at <term>Leiden</term>. He wrote a *Dissertation*, which I have unfortunately lost, concerning the *Book of Jasher*;

<margin>

2 Samuel 1:19
</margin>

He proves that the last word of that title was incorrectly punctuated with vowels, and that its three consonants are actually an abbreviation for *Israel*. So, instead of reading it as the "Book of Jasher" or "the Upright"—as is now universally done—he reads it as the *Book of Israel*, a view for which he has my support <note>original: "suffrage"</note>. Yet, whether he believes the *Pentateuch*, the book of <term>JOSHUA</term>, and the book of *Judges* to be summaries of this primary History of Israel, I do not perfectly remember; consequently, I will not assert it.

### HODEGUS.

I declare this: it is a sacred principle for me to put forward no fact without evidence <note>original: "vouchers"</note> that cannot be contradicted. In the introduction to the so often mentioned *MOSAIC REPUBLIC* <note>The author's own forthcoming work on the political structure of ancient Israel</note>, I shall add more reasons to prove that the *Pentateuch* <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> is an abridgment. These will be in addition to those already produced by Monsieur **Le Clerc**, whose *Critical Commentaries* on the *Old Testament* far surpass all others of that kind ever published; I long to see them happily finished. 

In the meantime, I cannot help but observe, along with the Roman historian **Ammianus Marcellinus**,²⁰ that whoever strives for too much brevity in telling things unknown does not so much consider *what are the most remarkable things he should express*, as *what he should pass over in silence*. This observation, though not originally intended for the historical books of the *Old Testament*, is unfortunately very true for how they are read today. However, when those books were first written, or even when they were summarized, the details were very familiar to the readers of those volumes, and all the ideas relating to them were perfectly understood.

XVII.

[the peo]ple were to be abandoned, without a head or guide in the wilderness. When **MOSES**, after they had made the Golden Calf <note>The Israelites built a golden idol while Moses was on Mount Sinai, leading to divine displeasure and a temporary change in how God (or the signal) guided them.</note>, had removed the Tabernacle out of the camp, it happened that as he *entered* into the *Tabernacle*, the cloudy *pillar* descended and *stood* at the door of *the Tabernacle*. That is—as clearly appears from the context—it was taken down from the Tabernacle, yet not carried before the army as a signal for marching, but fixed before the door. This was because the **LORD** refused to go up with them until later, when, upon their repentance, they were led on as before. 
<margin>Exodus 33.</margin>

So, upon a similar occasion—specifically the rebellion of **Aaron** and **Miriam**—the *pillar* of the cloud was *taken down* (**NOTE WELL** <note>original: *nota bene*</note>; here it is **expressly** <note>original: *in terminis*</note> said *to be taken down*) and *stood* at the door of *the Tabernacle*. 
<margin>Numbers 12:10.</margin>

I hope by now I have set the nature and use of the *Pillar of Cloud and Fire*—which directed the marches and camps of the Israelites in the wilderness—in the clearest light. I have placed it in such a light, I say, that no person of good understanding or one free of superstition will any longer think it a *miracle*. For, according to a common rule agreed upon no less by theologians than by others: *a thing ought not to be considered a miracle if it can be explained by the laws of nature or ordinary means, and where a perfect account is given of all appearances.* 

It is for this reason that I have brought forward parallel examples (as I shall do further in a moment) from authors who lived after **MOSES**. I do this not for proof (since the proof comes from the thing itself), but for illustration. In the same way, all commentators explain ancient customs by the modern practices of the Eastern nations, of which countless instances occur in their books. In short, where things seem alike in all respects, they ought to be judged as having the same nature, even if one author is more detailed in relating circumstances than another, or may use a different style and manner of writing.

### HODEGUS.

XVII. I readily agree, however, that I am obligated to solve all the difficulties that can be reasonably raised against my account of the Cloud. Some objections that have been offered in casual conversation are extremely frivolous and irrelevant. But the objection I will begin with appears at first glance to be the most difficult of all—or, if people prefer, the most miraculous.

When the Israelites, in their flight or expulsion <note>The author suggests the Israelites were either fleeing or being forcibly driven out of Egypt.</note>, had reached Pihahiroth by the Red Sea, the Egyptians pursuing them had come close.
<margin>Exodus 14:5–12.</margin>
The Egyptians were far superior in numbers and possessed every other advantage. The Israelites were in a state of extreme panic, having the sea in front of them and the enemy behind. This led them to give themselves up for lost; they assumed they would be attacked and completely destroyed that very night, even before morning appeared.

The details are found in the fourteenth chapter of Exodus, where it is further recorded that the *Angel of God*, who traveled ahead of the *Camp of Israel*, moved and went behind them. The *Pillar of Cloud* also moved from the front (the vanguard) and stood behind them in the rear.
<margin>Ibid. 14:19, 20.</margin>
It came between the *Egyptian camp* and the *Israeli camp*. It was a *cloud and darkness* to the Egyptians, but it provided *light by night* to the Israelites, so that the two groups did not come near each other all night.

It is noteworthy that the Egyptians, who were accustomed to the methods of traveling through those deserts, were not amazed by the Cloud. They certainly would have been surprised if it were something unusual. Instead, they followed it, even though—as we will see shortly—it deceived and misled them. The *Angel of God* mentioned here is a subject upon which Christian theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> from all nations are divided. Some maintain it was the highest-ranking being in the heavenly hierarchy <note>The traditional ranking of different orders of angels.</note>, but most...

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the "Angel" and the "Pillar of Cloud" mentioned in Exodus were natural, human elements of military leadership. ### HODEGUS.

...most that it was no less a person than **CHRIST** himself) I shall prove in due course to have been no other than a mere mortal man: the Guide of the Israelites in the wilderness and the overseer or director of the portable fire, as part of his specific office and duty <note>original: "province"</note>. But as for the thing itself—*the moving of the **FIRE***, which is the point in question—it was a stratagem of war there, no less than elsewhere. I will explain this stratagem here for even the simplest understanding <note>original: "meanest capacity"</note> by a passage from the *Cyropedia* <note>Xenophon’s semi-fictional biography of Cyrus the Great, written in the 4th century BCE.</note>, which is exactly parallel. When **CYRUS** and **CYAXARES**, at the head of an army of Medes and Persians, lay encamped in the enemy's country, the army of the Babylonians, Lydians, and Egyptians, far superior in number, eventually caught up with them. *At this point (says <gloss>21</gloss> **XENOPHON**) they did not kindle the fires at night in the center of the camp, but in the front of it. They did this so that if any of the enemy moved in the night, the scouts might see them by the light of the fire, while the scouts themselves remained unseen. They likewise frequently placed the fire in the rear of the camp to deceive the enemy. This caused the enemy's scouts to stumble upon the out-guards, because the fires were behind the camp, leading them to suppose they were still at a considerable distance, believing the fire was in its ordinary place.*

<margin>
Book 8 and *Greek History* <note>original: *Historia Graeca*</note>, Book 6.
</margin>

I have noted a very similar passage in the same **XENOPHON**, and there are many more in other historians. Here then you have **FIRE** moving from one place to another, sometimes in front and sometimes behind...

the camp: but it was arranged so as to be a light and guide to the Persians, while at the same time it was darkness and deception to the Assyrians. *If any of the enemy moved in the night (says the Greek writer), the Persians might see them, but not be seen by them, by means of the FIRE. It was a cloud and darkness to the Egyptians (says the Hebrew writer), but it gave light by night to the Israelites, so that the one did not come near the other all night;* for the Egyptians, judging by the fire, thought the Israelites were much closer than they actually were. The only difference between the authors of the *Pentateuch* <note>original: "Pentateuch" — the first five books of the Hebrew Bible.</note> and the *Cyropedia* <note>Xenophon’s semi-fictional biography of Cyrus the Great.</note> is that the latter describes more specifically how this was done. Meanwhile, the other omits some circumstances—or rather words them differently—because the practice was familiar to everyone at that time. The author of the *Cyropedia* wrote about matters he learned from strangers, which were utterly unknown or seldom used in his own country. In contrast, the author of the *Pentateuch* relates what was well known or customary to his readers; therefore, he did not need to describe those minute details which he omits on a thousand other such occasions.

XVIII. I shall provide only this one example of such omissions. When people of clear understanding and sound judgment read in *Deuteronomy* that, during the forty years of wandering of <margin>Deuteronomy 8:4 & 29:5.</margin> the Israelites in the wilderness, *their clothes did not grow old, nor their shoes wear out, nor their feet swell for lack of being shod;* <margin>Exodus 10:9, 24, 26 & 12:32, 38. ...how a Church *Father*, or his even more gullible modern disciple—instructed by a mythical Jewish *Kabbalist* <note>original: "fabulous Jewish Cabalist"</note>—could be capable of conceiving or spreading such absurdities as are sold on this subject. But the land of illusions <note>original: "Chimeras," referring to mythical monsters or wild fantasies</note> is vast. Every man with an unquestioning nature <note>original: "implicite constitution," referring to someone who accepts dogma without evidence</note> is as obsessed and conceited regarding the wonderful, the extraordinary, and the "I-know-not-what" in his *Religion* as he is regarding his *Mistress*. Just as each of them must surely be a *one-of-a-kind* in their own way, he professes himself always ready to fight or suffer for both, no matter how formidable his rivals might be. Neither the religion nor the mistress is ever in the wrong; or if they are, they must never be contradicted. He is considered an envious or ill-bred fellow if he does not join in drinking to the health or singing the praises of both.

These are sensitive points of honor, where consulting calm reason would likely cool a heroic passion and prevent a stubborn persistence from being mistaken for the virtues of loyalty and perseverance. This comparison holds true for these *visionaries* throughout; *wandering knights* <note>original: "Knights-errant"</note> are equally ridiculous whether they act like bullies for the sake of *Religion* or for *Ladies*.

But not to lose sight of our military tactics <note>original: "stratagems"</note>, many of which were performed in ancient times—and still are—by the help of FIRE. VEGETIUS says that <note>original: "Si divisæ sint copiæ..."</note> ^23 *when armies are divided, they signal to their allies in the night-time by flame, and in the day-time by smoke, what can be done by no other means.* And FRONTINUS notes that the ^24 *Arabs, whose custom...*

---

23 <note>original: "Si divisæ sint copiæ, per noctem Flammis, per diem Fumo, significant socijs, quod aliter non potest nunciari. Lib. 3. cap. 5."</note> If forces are divided, they signal to their allies by flames at night and by smoke during the day what cannot be communicated otherwise. *Vegetius, Book 3, Chapter 5.*

24 <note>original: "Arabes, cùm esset nota consuetudo eorum, qua de adventu hostium interdiu Fumo, noctu Igne significare instituerant, ut sine intermissione ea fierent, præceperunt, adventantibus autem adversarijs intermitterent: qui cùm, cessantibus luminibus, existimarent ignorari adventum suum, avidiùs ingressi oppressique sunt. De Stratagem. lib. 2. cap. 5."</note> The Arabs, since their custom of signaling the arrival of enemies by smoke during the day and fire at night was well known, ordered that these signals should be made without interruption. However, they ordered the signals to stop when the enemies actually approached. The enemies, seeing the lights cease and thinking their arrival was unknown, entered the area more eagerly and were overwhelmed. *Frontinus, On Stratagems, Book 2, Chapter 5.*

...was well known to give notice of the enemy's approach by smoke in the daytime and by fire at night. He ordered this method to be observed without interruption, except when the enemy drew near. XIX. THE next difficulty arises from some people confusing one *cloud* with another, or rather with several others. In the first place, they confuse the guiding *cloud* with that of Mount Sinai. The latter was a cloud properly so-called, out of which issued thunder and lightning. <margin>Exodus 19:16.</margin> *And it happened on the third day in the morning,* as you find in the 19th chapter of Exodus, *that there were thunders and lightnings, and a thick cloud upon the mountain;* <margin>Verse 18.</margin> which, just after, is called *smoke* through a simple metaphor common to other <gloss>25</gloss> writers, who call the fog on mountains a reddish-brown <note>original: "russet"</note> *cloud* and also thick *darkness*. <margin>Verse 9.</margin> A little earlier in this same chapter, it is also called a thick *cloud*, <margin>Verse 21.</margin> just as in the following chapter it is called thick *darkness*. These are very natural descriptions of the weather before thunder and lightning. <margin>Ibid. 24:15–18.</margin> It is said to have covered the mountain, as clouds usually do, and is the same one mentioned again in <margin>Verse 5.</margin> the thirty-fourth chapter. After identifying these passages, it will be easy to distinguish this real *cloud* on the mountain from the metaphorical *cloud* on the Tabernacle <note>The portable earthly dwelling place of God used by the Israelites during the Exodus.</note> whenever it appears,

their hostility toward Judaism to an irrational extent; just as the mob, at this very time in Portugal <note>A reference to the intense anti-Semitism and the Inquisition in Portugal during the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>, would tear a man to pieces if he dared to claim that the Virgin *MARY* had been a *Jewess*. This altar was then burning abundantly for the first time and covering the area with its smoke; meanwhile, the flame—or the *Glory of the* <margin>Verses 26, 27.</margin> *LORD*—from the Altar of Incense filled the Tabernacle. A similar event happened long afterward to the priests in *SOLOMON’S* time; <margin>1 Kings 8:10, 11, 12 compared with 2 Chron. 5:14, 6:1, and 7:1, 2.</margin> though we have sufficiently demonstrated that these *clouds* were made by the priests themselves and were far from being the guiding *Cloud* used in the wilderness.

things: but if I have proved—and if the nature of the matter shows—that it was the *flame* and the *smoke* of the very same FIRE that served as a guide, the one by day and the other by night, then I think it is a very inaccurate way of speaking to call them TWO PILLARS. Even **MENASSEH BEN ISRAEL** <gloss>27</gloss> <note>Menasseh Ben Israel (1604–1657) was a prominent Portuguese rabbi and scholar who lived in Amsterdam and was instrumental in the resettlement of Jews in England.</note> does this, for whom I nevertheless hold an uncommon esteem when his judgment matches his learning. **MOSES** nowhere says they were TWO PILLARS, just as he never says they were MIRACULOUS. In fact, he speaks of it as one pillar in more than one place, though its dual use has led many Jews and Christians to believe there were two distinct pillars.

<margin>Exodus 14:20.</margin>
Thus in *Exodus*: IT *came between the camp of the Egyptians and the camp of Israel, and* IT *was a* CLOUD *and darkness to them, but it gave* LIGHT *by night to these.*

<margin>Numbers 9:21.</margin>
So in *Numbers: whether it was by day or by night that the Cloud was taken up, they journeyed.* In this passage, any person can clearly see—if they did not notice it before—that the Pillar of *Fire* by night and the Pillar of *Cloud* by day were one and the same thing.

Neither is there any more weight in the next imaginative idea: that the Pillar of *Fire* was so extraordinarily large and bright that it not only guided the whole army, but also provided such clear light to every individual that, far from tripping or stumbling, he could see a pin or a needle at his feet. When once the wonder-

...wonderful has got into a man's head, it wholly possesses him; it leaves no room for anything else, nor knows how to set any bounds for itself. Yet *Scripture* (indeed <note>original: "forsooth" — used sarcastically here to suggest the author finds the opposing argument ridiculous</note>) must be quoted for this dubious, or rather monstrous, supposition. The principal text they bring is the following from NEHEMIAH:

<margin>Nehemiah 9:19</margin>
> *Yet you, in your many mercies, did not forsake them in the wilderness: the Pillar of Cloud did not depart from them by day to lead them in the way, nor the Pillar of Fire by night, to show them light and the way in which they should go.*

Here, the fullest meaning of the words amounts to no more than that the *Pillar* showed the way to the whole army, which nobody denies. Nor would anyone deny it even if only the first column saw it, since the next would follow that one, and so on for the rest. It was, therefore, a general guide or landmark for the place they were heading toward, but not intended for every man's feet, much less for their private concerns—a thing which Jewish and Christian scholars <note>original: "doctors" — here referring to learned theologians or teachers of the law</note> have not been ashamed to affirm. To as little purpose do they cite this from the seventy-eighth Psalm:

<margin>Psalm 78:14</margin>
> *In the daytime also he led them with a Cloud, and all the night with a Light of Fire.*

Who denies it? But it would be abusing my reader's patience to insist longer on this point. I am sure it would be showing a very mean opinion of his understanding.

XXII. THE last text, however, reminds me of the hundred and fifth *Psalm*, from which a passage is alleged to prove that the base of the *Pillar* was so large that it covered the whole camp of Israel, over which it hung perpendicularly. The words are:

<margin>Psalm 105:39</margin>
> *He spread a Cloud for a covering, and Fire to give light in the night.*

This is a plain allusion—as appears from the verses of that *Psalm* going before and coming after—to the

the camp became a *covering*—that is, a protection—for it. The original word has a sense no less general than "covering" has in English, as when we say "to cover an army or a town" <note>Meaning to provide tactical protection or screen them from view.</note>. For instance, in the same sense that the *Cloud* was said to be *over the camp* (by being elevated on the Tabernacle when they were stationed, or on a pole during their marches), we say—and all men say—that such a body of men are *under* their colors <note>original: "colors" — military flags or banners.</note>, though not one single person is actually *covered* by them. No more is anyone "covered" by their officers being said to be *over* them.

Some place great stress, regarding this idea of a total covering, on a passage in the *First Epistle to the Corinthians*. There, Paul, creating an allegory out of the principal

For just as none of the Israelites could be said—in favor of immersion <note>original: "dipping"</note>—to have been under water during their passage over the sea, I am afraid just as little can be inferred on behalf of *sprinkling* from the *Cloud*. That "gentle dew" which some have fancied is merely a pure invention <note>original: "distillation of their own brains"</note> of their own. XXIII. The false interpretation forced upon the 39th verse of the 105th *Psalm*—by men who are not satisfied with the obvious sense of *Scripture*, or by others who do not find the *Scripture* supporting their empty traditions—has produced more extravagant imaginations than any of the strange interpretations <note>original: "Glosses"</note> occasioned by the *Cloud*. Some assert it covered the Israelites so completely that it not only kept off unwholesome dews or cold winds and the scorching heat of the sun, but that they never saw, nor indeed needed to see, the sun, moon, or stars during the forty years they wandered in the wilderness. 

The Rabbis <note>original: "Rabbins"</note> add that the *Cloud*, like wool sacks or sandbags <note>original: "bags of earth"</note> used in modern trenches, deadened the arrows and projectiles <note>original: "artillery"</note> of the Egyptians, who attacked the Israelites relentlessly all through the night. But not content with one *Cloud*, they talk of no fewer than seven *Clouds of glory* encompassing the camp: four toward the four winds, lest anyone should injure them with an "evil eye"; one above them, lest the sun (as we said) should hurt them; one under them, which would bear them up as a mother does her child; and the seventh marching three days' journey ahead of them, flattening the hills and raising the valleys into a level plain, destroying all manner of serpents, scorpions, or other harmful creatures, and marking the various places for setting up their camp. 

To be a Field Marshal <note>original: "Marechal de Camp"; a high-ranking military officer responsible for the layout and security of an army's camp.</note> in this way was nothing compared to its other services. The *Cloud* served each individual sentry as a laundress, a scourer <note>A person who cleans clothing or metal by scrubbing.</note>, a nurse (and what else?), purifying and whitening their clothes, and keeping their bodies clean from sweat...

...or vermin, and fanning them as they marched with refreshing breezes. Great disputes have been raised about the nature <note>original: "matter"</note> of the *Cloud*, so that I may here once and for all be done with such fantasies <note>original: "visions"</note>: for example, whether it was one of the things made before the creation of the world, but stored away until the proper time to use it? Whether it was created out of nothing and reduced to nothing again? Or if it was created out of something, whether it was from elementary fire and the usual material of other clouds? 

Several maintain that one side of it was dark <note>original: "obscure"</note> and the other bright; that it was a symbol of the Trinity, and that the second person of the Trinity [Jesus] lived within it. This Pillar has proved to be a fruitful field of allegories for the *Church Fathers* and other theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note>, who find in it the divine and human nature of **Christ**—along with other things no less remarkable and sublime—without any difficulty at all. 

When I mentioned the Rabbinical legends <note>original: "dreams"</note> about the creation of this *Pillar* or *Cloud*, I forgot to mention that several of them believed it was created on the evening of the first Sabbath, even though **God** is said to have rested on that day from all his works. But this evening was not considered part of the Sabbath according to the way modern Jews divide time. Certainly, the sacred pillars of the pagans <note>original: "Heathens"</note> had their origin in the guiding pillar of the Israelites, as many good Christians have claimed. <sup>28</sup>

In the *Evangelical Demonstration* <note>original: *Evangelica*; referring to Pierre Daniel Huet’s 1679 work *Demonstratio Evangelica*</note>, he finds it in the Temple of APOLLO, and even in the mysteries of BACCHUS; he gravely claims that it gave rise to the Pillars of HERCULES and to the other memorial pillars of the ancients. Finally, the Jews expect that, upon the future return of the twelve tribes at the coming of the MESSIAH from all the countries where they are dispersed, this *Cloud* will again lead them to the holy land. I wish them a good journey there; though, while they wait, I am far from being tired of their company here, where they are most useful subjects and many of them are my very good friends.

### XXIV.

In the works of a famous commentator—who was no less a fine gentleman than a polished scholar—I find there was someone (though we are not told where or when) who maintained that the fire and smoke preceding the Israelites in the wilderness was the *sacred inextinguishable fire* carried on the altar for burnt offerings and other sacrifices. The falsehood of this opinion is obvious at first sight. There is no mention of such a sacred, unquenchable fire among the early Egyptians, nor among the Hebrews who lived there as slaves, nor even among the Patriarchs before or after the Flood <note>original: "antediluvian or postdiluvian"</note>. Everything that can be offered to support this idea is clearly groundless and unstable. As for the ordinary fire, whe-

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the Israelites must have used ordinary fire for daily needs during the Exodus. He begins to contrast the temporary altars used initially with the later, portable altars of the Tabernacle to show that no special "sacred fire" preceded the formal establishment of the priesthood.</meta>

<header>HODEGUS.</header>

Whether for cooking meat <note>original: "dressing of meat"</note> or other necessary uses, fire could not be lacking in an army of more than six hundred thousand men. We could produce countless examples to show they had it, should anyone require proof of what all men (even without such proofs) will assume. The Israelites had no portable altar when they first came out of Egypt. <margin>Exodus 20:24, 25, 26.</margin> The first mention of any altar among them is a command to build one of earth or unhewn stones—neither of which would be portable—and intended only for temporary <note>original: "transient"</note> occasions.

<margin>Ibid. 24:4.</margin>
The first altar we read of that Moses himself built was made of unhewn stones. This would have been a waste of effort if there were a portable one already in the camp, as there was later on. Furthermore, neither Aaron (who was not yet a priest) nor any other priests brought "sacred fire" from Egypt. It was some of the *firstborn* <note>In early Israelite tradition, the firstborn sons functioned as priests before the Levites were officially set apart for the role.</note> who offered sacrifices on this first altar of Moses, with Moses himself officiating alongside them. There is not the slightest mention of sacred or miraculous fire. Hear the very words:

<margin>Verses 5, 6.</margin>
> *And he sent young men of the children of Israel, who offered burnt offerings and sacrificed peace offerings of oxen to the* LORD; *and* MOSES *took half of the blood and put it in basins, and half of the blood he sprinkled on the altar, etc.*

When the Tabernacle and its furnishings were ordered to be built, instructions were also given to make two portable altars. They never used any other altars besides these until they entered the land of Canaan.

<margin>Ibid. 30:1, etc.</margin>
To begin with the latter, one was the *altar of incense*, covered with gold and placed before the Ark in the Sanctuary. But this is not the one we are discussing.

<margin>Ibid. 27:1–6.</margin>
The other was the *altar of burnt offering*, five cubits square and covered with brass, equipped with rings and poles on each side for carrying it.

<margin>Leviticus 6:8–13. Ibid. 9:24.</margin>
On this altar, there was to be a perpetual *fire*, which was first kindled shortly after Aaron had been appointed High Priest. Now, that the

<summary>The author argues that the Israelites initially used temporary altars of earth or stone and did not possess a portable altar or "sacred fire" until the Tabernacle was constructed. <meta>This page continues the author's argument that the guiding "pillar of fire" was a practical signal fire, distinguishing it from the sacrificial fire of the Tabernacle altar.</meta>

# HODEGUS.
<page-num>45</page-num>

It is clear from various considerations—of which I shall only cite a few—that the fire on this Altar was not the same fire that was intended to guide the Israelites.

In the first place, these fires served different purposes: one was for offering sacrifice, and the other was for showing the way. If it were argued that one and the same fire could serve both purposes, I respond that the Israelites already had the fire for showing the way while they were still in Egypt and in the wilderness on the other side of the Red Sea. In contrast, the perpetual fire for sacrifice was only established on this side of the sea, at the foot of Mount Sinai in Arabia. There is no mention of any other use for it; in fact, the opposite is strongly implied.

Secondly, the sacred fire was kept in the courtyard of the Tabernacle, while the guiding fire was positioned on top of it. Similarly, during a march, the sacred fire was often carried in the middle of the camp, while the guiding fire was always held high at the front.

Thirdly, based on its description, the Altar was not designed to be hoisted to the top of the Tabernacle or onto a long pole at the front of the army. It could not have been easily—if at all—set up or taken down at will. However, an iron pot—or any similar device <note>original: "machine"</note> still used for the same purpose—can be easily hoisted to the top of a pole where it hangs on a hook. Such a pole could be fixed to the Tabernacle just as easily as a flagpole is attached to a castle or the stern of a ship.

Despite the modern use of the compass, this method of guidance by fire is still practiced among the caravans of the East, a fact upon which all travelers agree. The fires that go before the vast host of pilgrims traveling yearly from Grand Cairo in Egypt to Mecca in Arabia are called *Shamalars* <note>This likely refers to *mash'al*, a traditional Arabic torch or cresset used to light the way for night travelers.</note>. They are carried in iron pots on long poles, which is the most obvious method of managing fire and smoke for such a purpose. This method is so natural that it seems almost unnecessary for modern writers to have described it; and it is for

<summary>Toland argues that the "pillar of fire" was a practical signaling device—an iron pot of fire on a pole—rather than the sacrificial fire from the Altar, citing geographical timelines and modern caravan practices as evidence.</summary>
<keywords>Exodus, pillar of fire, Tabernacle, Mount Sinai, caravans, Shamalars, signaling, historical geography</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the "pillar of fire" was a practical, human-led signal fire, specifically addressing why historical accounts like the Pentateuch do not specify the type of fuel used.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

->H O D E G U S.<-

for this reason: that neither the author of the *Pentateuch* <note>The first five books of the Bible, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note>, nor the historians of Darius and Alexander, have specified the fuel of their fires used for signals or guidance.

XXV. After having demonstrated that the *Pillar of Cloud and Fire* was not miraculous, this idea of the *sacred fire* could not take up much of my time. Therefore, I think it is now appropriate to fulfill the promise I made above: to show that the *Angel* of the LORD, who carried the Pillar behind the Israelites, or between them and the Egyptians, was a mere mortal man—the overseer or director of the portable fire and the guide of the Israelites in the wilderness. Perhaps I may even acquaint you with his family before I am finished, which I suspect is a secret about "Angels" hitherto unknown. Therefore, if this is proven, it will not only ruin a world of clever allegories and typical <note>original: "typical" — referring to typology, a method of biblical interpretation where Old Testament events are seen as "types" or symbols foreshadowing the New Testament.</note> observations, but—to the great humiliation of a thousand booksellers—turn many entire treatises into wastepaper. This will probably cause certain people to make an outcry; but so long as I am persuaded that the truth is on my side, I shall be as much pleased as they are certain to be angry. 

To determine, then, whether it was a real and literal Angel that guided the Israelites and managed the Cloud, we must look at the word *Angel* <note>The Hebrew word *malak* and the Greek *angelos* both literally mean "messenger," which can refer to either a human or a supernatural being.</note>, the phrase "the *Angel* of the LORD," or the actions of the person who is called an *Angel*. As for his actions in managing the Pillar or Cloud, I have already explained them all. I have shown not only that they could be done by natural and ordinary means, but that other nations did the same in every respect, without needing or claiming the assistance of any supernatural or extraordinary power. I have performed this so thoroughly that I may well

...supersede repetition, as I have not overlooked (to the best of my memory) any single text where the *Cloud* or *Fire* is mentioned. It cannot simply and peremptorily be concluded from the word 
<margin>Messenger <note>original: מַלְאָךְ (*malak*)</note></margin> 
*Angel* that the director of the guiding *Pillar* was anything other than a man. For the Hebrew word is no 
<margin>Root: to send <note>original: לאך (*lak*)</note></margin> 
less general than the Greek word from which we 
<margin>Mission or work <note>original: מְלָאכָה (*melakah*)</note></margin> 
have formed the word *Angel*. It signifies any *Messenger* 
<margin>Messenger <note>original: Ἄγγελος (*Angelos*)</note></margin> 
whatsoever, mortal or immortal; so that circumstances alone can determine what kind of messenger is meant. This is the reason our translators, to avoid ambiguity, render it *Messenger* or *Ambassador* when it refers to a man, and *Angel* when it stands for a spirit. The rule 
<margin>Genesis 32:1, 3</margin> 
is good, provided it were accurately observed. In the thirty-second chapter of *Genesis* it is written, *And Jacob went on his way, and the Angels of God met him—and Jacob sent Messengers before him to Esau his brother.* Here it is the very same word that in the first verse is rendered *Angels* and in the third verse *Messengers*; but circumstances 
<margin>Genesis 16:7</margin> 
rightly guided the translators. Thus in *Genesis* the sixteenth chapter, and in the thirteenth 
<margin>Judges 13:3, etc.</margin> 
chapter of *Judges*, as in many other places, it signifies an *Angel* or *Angels* properly so called; 
<margin>2 Kings 6:32</margin> 
and in certain places of the books of *Kings*, *Chronicles*, Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, 
<margin>2 Chronicles 36:15, 16; Isaiah 30:4; Jeremiah 27:3; Ezekiel 17:15; Nahum 2:13</margin> 
and Nahum—to name no more—it signifies *Messengers* and *Ambassadors*. The original word, therefore, denotes men as well as Angels, and always the former where only human actions are described, since we must never have recourse to miracles except where nature falls short and is visibly defective. But the translators have not always scrupulously followed the rule they so happily proposed to themselves; for sometimes they translate the word *Messenger* when they do not understand it to be a mere man, what-

->HODEGUS.<-

...address myself in this discourse; but to such as understand, or are willing to learn, the style of the *Old Testament*. These readers will find that, because the Lord was the King of the Israelites, he was said to *go before them*. This was said because of the *Ark* and other symbols of his presence, and because his generals, Moses and Joshua, led them. Indeed, all the actions of these men and others—such as the high priests and the prophets (if in accordance with his laws)—are called God's actions; the actions of all ministers are always attributed to the Prince <note>In this context, the "Prince" refers to God as the sovereign ruler of the Israelites.</note> whom they serve and obey.

XXVII. The same is also true of the chief guide of the Israelites, who was an important officer subject only to the general himself. Such an institution is as ancient as it is natural. Vegetius <note>original: VEGETIUS. Publius Flavius Vegetius Renatus was a 4th-century Roman author known for his treatise on military matters, *De Re Militari*.</note> observes with great judgment that *from all antiquity, every nation has discovered this truth: that the whole army should know through signals, and obey, whatever the general alone should judge convenient.* But even the general, when ignorant of the way, must rely on the guide, who then properly directs the signals. For, as the same Vegetius has it, *whichever way the leader orders the banners <term>original: "Ensigns"</term> to move, in that direction the soldiers must necessarily march along with their colors*, to use our English expression. Having explained these things, it only remains to ask: who was this guide and director? It is not that the question is of very great importance, or that anything we have said so far regarding the main point would be...

***

29. <note>original: "Antiquus omnium gentium usus invenit, quomodo, quod solus Dux utile judicasset, per signa totus agnosceret et sequeretur exercitus."</note> The ancient custom of all nations found a way by which the whole army might recognize and follow through signals what the leader alone had judged useful. *Book 3, chapter 5.*

30. <note>original: "Quocunque enim haec ferri jusserit Ductor, eò necesse est, signum suum comitantes, milites pergant."</note> For wherever the leader orders these [the standards] to be carried, it is necessary that the soldiers go there, following their own standard. *Ibid.*

...any less true, even if we were unable to resolve it; but a lover of Truth is pleased with the unraveling <note>original: "un-ridling"</note> of even the smallest <note>original: "meanest"</note> circumstances, though purely incidental, as they still shed more light and certainty on the subject. I answer then, that for the majority of the time, if not during the entire time, this Guide was no other than **HOBAB**, the brother-in-law of **MOSES**, who was born and raised in the wilderness and consequently well-acquainted with its various regions.
