# H O D E G U S.

Therefore, MOSES is earnest with him to stay for two weighty reasons. The first of these is that HOBAB knew how they were to encamp in the wilderness, which shows that he was already employed in this matter. The second is that for the rest of their way to the land they were going to conquer, he might serve them instead of eyes, or be their Guide. To more effectively persuade HOBAB to make this choice, MOSES promises him a share in the good fortune of the Israelites, who were likely to take possession of a much better territory than Midian, a barren part of the wilderness. In comparison to Midian, Judea "flowed with milk and honey," or abounded with all things. 

Then it is immediately added—without any mention of HOBAB persisting in his resolution to leave, but with a plain implication that he accepted such advantageous conditions, which will presently appear to be the case—that they marched forward a three days' journey. The *Ark* was carried before them, representing the presence of JEHOVAH their King, and the Cloud of JEHOVAH showed them the way as it had done until then. 

The consideration of these particulars weighed so much with me some years ago that I then proposed to entitle this *Dissertation HOBAB*. However, as I wanted to guard against the unreasonable objections <note>original: "cavils"</note> of those who, finding nothing material to criticize, will nitpick <note>original: "be nibbling"</note> at unimportant details <note>original: "indifferent incidents"</note>, I afterwards changed my mind. For although it is not impossible that HOBAB might have continued in this post for forty years, and it is probable he went to join MOSES as soon as the Israelite expedition began; yet it is also possible that he might not have been the first Guide in the wilderness of Etham, nor lived until the passage of the Jordan, when the Pillar became useless for the future.

XXVIII. To crown our work, I shall here take some small pains to solve the difficulties that have been raised by quite a few commentators about this same Hobab. He is sometimes confused with his father, sometimes with his grandfather, and at other times with both. There have been those, therefore, who claimed that Raguel or Rehuel (depending on the different pronunciation of the Hebrew letter *Ayin* <term>ע</term> <note>original: "Hajin"; the author is referring to the Hebrew letter 'Ayin, which can have a guttural sound</note>), Jethro, and Hobab were all one and the same person. However, it is clear from our last citation out of *Numbers*, 
<margin>Numbers 10:29.</margin>
that at least Raguel and Hobab were not the same person, since Hobab is there expressly called *the son of Raguel the Midianite, Moses's father-in-law*. But which of them was the father-in-law is another point of doubt. Because of an ambiguity very common in the Hebrew language, the passage may be interpreted to make either Raguel or Hobab the father-in-law, which nothing but the context can decide. By looking at these circumstances, it will be evident that neither of them was literally and properly Moses's father-in-law, though either of them might be figuratively and customarily styled as such. While the grandfather lives, he is still the head of the family and is called "father" by all his descendants. This was a common usage in the East, of which there are many examples in the *Old Testament*, where even a deceased grandfather is called "father" by his grandchild.
<margin>Genesis 31:42.</margin>
> *Unless the God of my father (says Jacob to Laban), the God of Abraham, and the fear of Isaac, had been with me, surely you would have sent me away empty-handed now.*
<margin>Genesis 31:43.</margin>
Thus Laban—to provide no more authorities in a matter so clear—calls Jacob's children his own, because they were born of his daughters. Now, Raguel was the grandfather of Zipporah, Moses's wife;

I consider this practice of rearranging text to be a well-intentioned zeal in others who are sincere in this approach—Monsieur Le Clerc in particular—but in myself, I admit it would be both criminal and profane. It would be criminal to treat any book this way when I was certain it did not require it, doing so only to serve a specific purpose; and it would be profane to mix up a sacred book in a manner I would consider ridiculous in even the most minor work. The words of this learned man (lest I should be thought to charge him wrongly) are as follows:

Besides that, Moses seems to have spoken these things to him before he knew that the Cloud was to be a perpetual guide to the Israelites; for the contents of the 31st verse <note>Numbers 10:31, where Moses asks Hobab to be "eyes" for the Israelites in the wilderness.</note> are not at all consistent with the guidance of the Cloud. Our system is subject to none of these difficulties: Raguel <note>Also known as Reuel, often identified with Jethro.</note> never came to the camp of the Israelites, Jethro came once, and Hobab always accompanied Moses.

XXX. But leaving the excuse Monsieur Le Clerc makes for the aforementioned passage—supposing it was originally part of the book of *Numbers*—to the reader’s judgment, I will now show that Hobab and his family proceeded with the Israelites toward the land of *Canaan*. The offer that Moses made him was too tempting to be refused, and we have every reason to suppose that he gladly accepted the favor. I grant there is no further occasion to mention him on the way; but, after the settlement of the Israelites in *Canaan*, we find—according to the promise of Moses—ample provision made for the family of Hobab by name. They were a family in high repute, enjoying large territories, and eminently devoted to the *Jewish Law*.

### HODEGUS.

[The children of the Kenite, Moses'] father-in-law, went up from the City of Palm Trees <note>Likely referring to Jericho.</note>, *with the children of Judah*, into the wilderness of *Judah*, which lies in the south of *Arad*; and they went and lived among the people. The various meanings <note>original: "diverse acceptions"</note> of "father-in-law" having been explained before, the question now turns to this: who was the immediate ancestor <note>original: "stock"</note> of the flourishing branches of the *Kenites*? This is fully answered in the same book of *Judges*.

<margin>Ibid. 4:11.</margin> *Heber* the *Kenite*, who was of the descendants of *Hobab*, the father-in-law of *Moses*, had separated himself from the *Kenites* and pitched his tent as far as the plain of *Zaanaim*, which <margin>verse 17.</margin> is by *Kedesh*. A little later you read that there was peace between *Jabin*, the King of *Hazor*, and the house of *Heber* the *Kenite*. From this, it appears how powerful the descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note> of *Hobab* became as a result of the rewards promised to him by *Moses*; one branch of them was so significant <note>original: "considerable"</note> that they entered into a treaty with a Prince <margin>Ibid. verse 3.</margin> who possessed nine hundred iron chariots.

Incidentally, it was the wife of this *Heber*, named *Jael*, who murdered *Sisera* the general in his sleep. This occurred despite the fact that he came to her tent for shelter at her own invitation, that she had refreshed him with food, and that he should have expected total security due to the peace established between his master *Jabin* and her husband *Heber*. 

Some of these *Kenites* lived among the *Amalekites*. When *Saul* resolved to root out the latter, he <margin>1 Samuel 15:6.</margin> said to the *Kenites*, "Go, depart, get down from among the *Amalekites*, lest I destroy you along with them; for you showed kindness to all the children of *Israel* when they came up out of *Egypt*." So the *Kenites* departed from among the *Amalekites*. The obligation created by showing the way through the wilderness was never to be forgotten, as it certainly should not have been. They are mentioned again twice

<page-num>60</page-num>

# HODEGUS.

<meta>This page contains bleed-through from the following page in the original document.</meta>

...furnish us with an abundance of useful reflections; but this *Dissertation*, wherein I have endeavored that precision should reign alongside clarity <note>original: "perspicuity"</note>, is long enough already. I could easily enlarge it, especially in this latter part, if I were to produce all the excuses <note>original: "shifts"; referring here to the evasive arguments used by scholars to avoid a difficult truth</note> of the commentators—each serving his own hypothesis—concerning Raguel, Jethro, and Hobab. Those who suggest Hobab was Jethro’s brother come closest to the truth. 

But I shall never tempt the reader's patience when I have no prospect of instructing him. Besides, the main subject—that the *Cloud was not miraculous*—cannot be harmed by the arguments over names from those who would rather have any portion of the sacred writings pass for nonsense than have me demonstrate that it makes sense. In this, however, lies my comfort: that the Bible will always preserve its dignity, and that the truth will triumph at last over all the prejudices of the ignorant or the self-interested <note>original: "interested"; meaning those with a personal or professional stake in maintaining the status quo</note>.

->THE END.<-
<note>original: "FINIS"</note>

# <term>CLIDOPHORUS</term>,
<note>original: "Clidophorus"; from the Greek "Kleidophoros," meaning "The Key-bearer." This title suggests the author is providing the "key" to understanding how ancient philosophers hid their true meanings.</note>

->OR,<-

## Of the <term>Exoteric</term> and <term>Esoteric</term> PHILOSOPHY;
<note>These terms refer to two ways of teaching: "Exoteric" (external) for the general public, and "Esoteric" (internal) for a private circle of initiates.</note>

->THAT IS,<-

### Of the *External* and *Internal Doctrine* of the Ancients:

The one open and public, accommodated to popular prejudices and the RELIGIONS established by law; the other private and secret, wherein, to the few capable and discreet <note>original: "discrete"</note>, was taught the real TRUTH stripped <note>original: "stript"</note> of all disguises.

> ->It is necessary for you to learn all things:<-
> ->Both the steadfast heart of persuasive truth,<-
> ->And the opinions of mortals, in which there is no true certainty.<-

->PARMENIDES in DIOGENES LAERTIUS, Book 9, Section 22.<-

->LONDON, Printed in the YEAR 1720.<-

<summary>This is the title page for John Toland's "Clidophorus," a work exploring the historical distinction between public religious teachings and the secret, rational truths shared only among elite philosophers.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Clidophorus," where Toland explains the origins of the distinction between "external" (public) and "internal" (private) truths. He argues that this division was created by people seeking power and wealth.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

# CLIDOPHORUS

Through laziness or their occupations, few people are capable of acquiring possession of the truth. Some cunning persons thought they could not better attain authority over others (which naturally leads to wealth) than by pretending to be the masters of this same <term>TRUTH</term>. Next, they claimed that they could impart it to others without requiring any labor or distracting them from their business. As for the small expense, who would grudge paying a price out of his fleeting wealth <note>original: "transitory pelf"</note> for the invaluable jewel of knowledge? Or who would not think it fair to bestow a modest reward on men who could equally delight and benefit him? 

Nor did these crafty <term>Empirics</term> <note>original: "Empirics"; historically, this referred to quacks or pretenders to medical or philosophical knowledge</note> stop here. They knew that false facts and flawed reasoning might, at one time or another, be detected by people of insight. Therefore, just as the Devil is said to be "God’s ape" <note>A common early modern proverb meaning that evil imitates good in order to deceive</note>, they boasted of a superior and supernatural knowledge that was not subject to the rules of logic <note>original: "Criticism"</note> or a proper object for the human understanding. 

In fact, they went even further, openly maintaining that it was lawful to lie for the public good. They argued that because the common people were incapable of deep reflection, they ought to be managed by trickery and deluded by agreeable fables into obeying their governors. Thus <term>MNEVIS</term>, an Egyptian king, deceived his subjects by pretending to have an extraordinary communication with heaven. <term>ZOROASTER</term> successfully practiced the same art on the <term>Bactrians</term> and other neighboring nations. <term>PYTHAGORAS</term>, after hiding himself for some time (as if he were dead), appeared again at <term>Crotona</term>, preaching the joys and torments of an afterlife. His disciple <term>ZAMOLXIS</term> bragged of having received divine <term>Revelations</term> in a cave, through which he gained such authority that he could prescribe whatever laws he pleased to the unrefined <term>Scythians</term>. <term>MINOS</term> and <term>EPIMENIDES</term> in <term>Crete</term> published their conferences <note>original: "conferences"; here meaning private meetings or consultations with the divine</note>...

<meta>catchword: conferences</meta>

<summary>Toland describes how "crafty empirics" and ancient rulers used claims of supernatural revelation and "noble lies" to control the common people, citing figures like Zoroaster, Pythagoras, and Minos.</summary>
<keywords>political manipulation, noble lie, religious fraud, Mnevis, Zoroaster, Pythagoras, Zamolxis, Minos, Epimenides, ancient philosophy</keywords>

# CLIDOPHORUS,
<note>original: "Clidophorus"; from the Greek *Kleidophoros*, meaning "The Key-bearer."</note>

## OR

### Of the *Exoteric* and *Esoteric Philosophy*.
<term>Exoteric and Esoteric Philosophy</term> <note>Exoteric refers to "external" or public doctrines intended for the masses, while Esoteric refers to "internal" or private truths reserved for a select few.</note>

->I.<-

<image-desc>A large square ornamental woodcut initial letter 'T' within a decorative frame. The background of the frame depicts two figures in a classical or biblical setting; one figure stands on the left while the other is seated on the right.</image-desc>

To know the **TRUTH** is one thing; to tell it to others is another. And just as all people claim to admire the first, few practice the last as they should. While **TRUTH** is acknowledged to be more valuable than wealth or honor, most people prefer these things over truth without hesitation. This is evident not only from their eager efforts to gain riches and titles while they abandon the study of **TRUTH** or only half-heartedly pursue it, but also by the shameful, submissive respect they pay to powerful men and the indifference or neglect they usually show toward people of learning. 

I will not meticulously examine the causes of this behavior, which are many and varied; however, of all the reasons that can be identified, **SELF-INTEREST** <note>original: "INTEREST"; in this period, the term specifically referred to personal financial or political gain.</note> is certainly the most common and influential. For though humans are observed to be naturally fond of **TRUTH**...

<meta>catchword: through</meta>

<summary>In this opening section, the author argues that while people claim to value truth, they consistently prioritize wealth, titles, and social status over it due to self-interest.</summary>
<keywords>Clidophorus, Truth, Self-Interest, Exoteric Philosophy, Esoteric Philosophy, Learning vs. Power</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how ancient leaders claimed divine inspiration to manipulate their subjects. It specifically addresses how the threat of punishment forced philosophers to hide their true beliefs behind a "two-fold doctrine."</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>

# CLIDOPHORUS

...conferences they had with **JUPITER** in their several retreats from the society of other men, upon whom they imposed <note>original: "obtruded"</note> their own fabrications as divine commands. Who has not read of **NUMA’s** intimacy with the goddess **EGERIA**? <note>Numa Pompilius was the legendary second king of Rome; he claimed the nymph Egeria dictated his religious reforms to him.</note> From her, if his own word is to be believed, he learned the religion he taught the Romans. I shall name no more out of the long list which the learned have of such ancient deceivers <note>original: "antiquated seducers"</note>; nor any among those whose institutions still prevail, except for the sake of example: **FOHI**, **SAMMONOKODOM**, **MAHOMET**, and **GENGHIS KHAN**. <note>**Fohi** (Fu Xi) was a legendary Chinese ruler; **Sammonokodom** was a name used by early European travelers for the Buddha in Southeast Asia; **Mahomet** is a historical spelling of Muhammad; **Genghis Khan** is the founder of the Mongol Empire.</note> **DIODORUS SICULUS** and other ancient writers were not afraid to rank **MOSES** in this class, though his laws are truly divine, without any mixture of weakness or folly. <note>Toland likely includes this praise of Moses to avoid legal charges of blasphemy, while still noting that historians have categorized him alongside other lawgivers.</note>

But as this incomparable legislator should need no apology among Christians, I am solely concerned at present with those Heathen **IMPOSTORS**. Perceiving that what was built upon fraud could only be supported by force, they made it a capital crime to question their dictates, and highly disreputable even to examine—let alone to doubt—them. The priests, for their own interest, were never lacking anywhere in promoting such penal laws. The magistrates were commonly very ready to enforce those laws through what they called *wholesome severities* <note>A common ironic term in this period for religious persecution and physical punishment.</note>—partly through superstition resulting from their ignorance, and partly through policy, seeking to grasp more power than the laws allowed by using the assistance of the priests. 

Hence, no room was left for the propagation of **TRUTH**, except at the expense of a man's life, or at least of his honor and employment, of which numerous examples may be cited. The philosophers, therefore, and other well-wishers to mankind in most nations, were constrained by this holy tyranny to make use of a two-fold doctrine: the one Popular, accommodated...

<summary>Toland argues that ancient religious and political leaders used claims of divine revelation to enforce obedience, leading to a "holy tyranny" where priests and magistrates punished dissent. To survive, philosophers were forced to adopt a "two-fold doctrine," separating public teaching from private truth.</summary>

<keywords>Numa Pompilius, Egeria, Moses, impostors, religious persecution, wholesome severities, two-fold doctrine, exoteric and esoteric, John Toland, political philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the two types of philosophical doctrine—the public and the private—and begins a critique of how even Christians fail to live up to the ideal of transparency.</meta>

<header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

accommodated to the **PREJUDICES** of the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note>, and to the received **CUSTOMS** or **RELIGIONS**. The other was *Philosophical*, conforming to the nature of things and consequently to **TRUTH**. This second type, they communicated only to friends of known integrity <note>original: "probity"</note>, wisdom, and ability, doing so behind closed doors and with all other necessary precautions. They generally called these the *Exoteric* and *Esoteric*, or the *External* and *Internal Doctrines*. I am now going to show that they used such a distinction and practiced accordingly (which is the subject of this *Dissertation*) using a select collection of passages I have gathered from many others scattered throughout the ancient writers.

## II.

<image-desc>An ornamental drop-cap "Y" set within a square frame decorated with stylized leaves and vines.</image-desc>
Yet before I begin this collection, interspersed with my own observations, I cannot help but mourn the depravity of human nature. It is regrettable to find Christians anywhere forced <note>original: "constrain'd"</note> to regulate their conduct according to this pagan <note>original: "Heathen"</note> distinction, even though they profess the religion of Him who is **TRUTH** *itself*, and *whose service is perfect* **FREEDOM**. They are commanded to **LOVE** *each other* and to *speak the* **TRUTH** *to one another*, but they obey as if they were explicitly ordered to do the opposite. Not only does every sect furiously oppose every other—and all of them oppose anyone you can name—but even members of the same sect stand in opposition to each other over the most insignificant trifles, over abstract distinctions, and over partisan jargon <term>party-jargon</term> <note>Specialized language used to signal loyalty to a specific religious or political group rather than to convey clear meaning.</note>,

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<summary>Toland defines the "Exoteric" and "Esoteric" doctrines of ancient philosophers and begins a critique of modern Christians who, despite their faith, practice the same kind of deceptive social maneuvering and sectarian infighting.</summary>
<keywords>Exoteric, Esoteric, Internal Doctrine, External Doctrine, sectarianism, religious hypocrisy, truth vs. prejudice</keywords>

<header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

for favorite sounds, or even frequently for syllables and letters. They display every sign of total hatred by branding, defaming, and avoiding one another; their leaders always set the example, as if they were afraid the people might reach a better understanding and eventually realize they were quarreling over nothing. These are facts that cannot possibly be denied. And it can just as little be denied that, not content with this inward resentment <note>original: "rancor of mind"</note> or the narrowness of their specific schemes and notions, they enthusiastically torment each other with fines, legal disqualifications <note>original: "incapacities"</note>, exile, imprisonment, and countless other methods. This is not even to mention the greatest evil of all, death—which is at least their opinion—until persecution finally reaches its peak in the *Inquisition*, the ultimate perfection of this hellish *management* <note>original: "Oeconomy"</note> of faith. In short, daily experience clearly shows that in most places, one cannot discover—or at least cannot declare—**TRUTH** without risking one's reputation, employment, or life. 

These circumstances cannot fail to produce the miserable effects of insincerity, hypocrisy <note>original: "dissimulation"</note>, gross ignorance, and unrestrained <note>original: "licentious"</note> barbarity. What is most regrettable of all is that far too much of this leaven <note>referring to a corrupting influence, like yeast spreading through dough</note> ferments even in the purest *Churches*. What strange twists are given to controversies about matters that are in themselves trivial <note>original: "indifferent," referring to theological points not essential to salvation</note>, where both sides of the question could be innocently held? What secret insinuations, what blatant slanders, what unkind suggestions, and what harmful treatment are inflicted upon those who should be respected and cherished as brothers? But because they are thus forced to become enemies and are influenced by human weakness, they are provoked to pay back their adversaries in kind, with interest <note>original: "with usury"</note>. Among *Priests* and *Ministers* in particular (letting each person choose whichever title they prefer, for they agree on names no more than they do on anything else), what unmanly probing <note>original: "pumping," meaning to extract information or secrets through artful questioning</note>...

This must necessarily produce evasions, ambiguities, equivocations, and hypocrisy in all its shapes. These behaviors will not merely be called, but actually esteemed, *necessary cautions*. They are caused in all times and places by ambitious priests, supported by their followers—the "mob" whom they treat as their property. This deprives us of the peace of life and the truth of religion, and even of philosophical discoveries and improvements, to the great detriment of mankind. 

In all this affair, pride is always joined to self-interest, for never was any pride equal to the pretense of *infallibility* <note>the claim of being incapable of error</note>. This has prompted the accurate observation that just as the Church of *Rome* claims to be always in the right, so no other churches will ever confess themselves to be in the wrong. Therefore, you must submit, whether right or wrong, or it will be the worse for you in all the respects I have mentioned. ...than if anyone should see all who died in this age brought back to life; than if mountains were removed from their places; or than if a new world were created. In all these cases, there would still remain a suspicion of magical fraud, leading them to be considered not truly performed—which, one would think, would be more easily suspected of a fever <note>original: "ague"; a fit of shivering or a fever-induced hallucination</note>—but rather to be the fancies of our imaginations or the illusions of our senses. The long and short of it is that we must believe the Church rather than our own eyes—that is, if Berigardus <note>Claude Guillermet de Bérigard (1578–1663), an Aristotelian philosopher known for his work "Circulus Pisanus"</note> was not mocking as well as flattering the "Old Lady's" <note>likely referring to the vanity of the Church</note> vanity. I do not doubt, for my part, that he used the *exoteric* and *esoteric* distinction to "save his bacon," as we say. If anyone who has not read those *Circles* or *Dialogues* <note>Bérigard's work was written as a series of circular discussions or dialogues</note> insists that he did not, so be it; but I am about to demonstrate that others did, and that it was the common practice of all the ancient philosophers.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'W' with an ornate border</image-desc>
### III.

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, Book 9.</margin>
<margin>Section 22.</margin>
PARMENIDES <note>An ancient Greek philosopher (born c. 515 BC) who argued that reality is one and unchanging, and that the senses are deceptive</note> was unquestionably right when he affirmed that there are two sorts of philosophy: one according to TRUTH, the other according to OPINION. This is not only true of the Greeks and Romans, but just as certainly true of other much more ancient nations. I know that the words of Parmenides may be easily <note>original: "commodiously"</note> understood as referring to the fallaciousness and uncertainty of the senses, which often represent things differently than they truly are and must therefore be examined and
<margin>Ibid.</margin>

<meta>catchword: corrected</meta>

<summary>The author argues that philosophers like Bérigard used a distinction between public and private teachings to avoid conflict with the Church, then introduces Parmenides' theory that philosophy is divided into Truth and Opinion.</summary>
<keywords>Berigardus, Parmenides, exoteric, esoteric, philosophy of truth, philosophy of opinion, skepticism, Church authority, sensory illusion</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "twofold philosophy," moving from the general concept of sense vs. reason to specific historical examples of public (exoteric) and secret (esoteric) doctrines in ancient Greece and Egypt.</meta>

<header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

<margin>In Aristotle, *Physics*, Book 1.</margin>
...corrected by reason. From the senses arises opinion; from reason comes demonstration. On the former are piled up the prejudices of the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note> who follow the bare appearance of things; on the latter are founded the principles <note>original: "axioms"</note> of the wise, who consider things as they are in themselves. But this is not the specific twofold philosophy that our author intended; rather, he meant the one represented by Philoponus <note>John Philoponus (c. 490 – c. 570 AD), an Alexandrian philologist and commentator on Aristotle</note>. This writer assures us that Parmenides, in his *exoteric* works (books adapted to the public taste), says that fire and water are the beginning or principle of all things—as if he had acknowledged that the world had been created. However, in his *esoteric* works (books compiled according to the truth), he says the universe is one, infinite, and immutable. Here, we not only find the literal distinction between *exoteric* and *esoteric* teachings, but we also see an example of it: a beginning of things exoterically, but esoterically, the eternity and incorruptibility of the universe.

<margin>See Herodotus, Diodorus, Cicero, Plutarch, Iamblichus, and many others. *On Isis and Osiris*, Paris Edition, page 354.</margin>
The Egyptians, who were the wisest of mortals, had a twofold doctrine: one secret, and for that very reason sacred; the other popular, and consequently common <note>original: "vulgar"</note>. Who is ignorant of their sacred letters, hieroglyphics, forms, symbols, enigmas, and fables? Far and near, the fame of Egyptian philosophy spread, concealing things under the appearance of fables (as Plutarch says) and in speeches that contained obscure indications and arguments for the truth. They themselves expressly declare this by placing sphinxes before most of their temples, thereby suggesting that their doctrine concerning sacred things consists of a type of wisdom that is intentionally perplexed and hidden behind carefully crafted veils.

<margin>The same author, in the same place.</margin>
To provide a specimen of the things they concealed, the shrine <note>original: "fane"</note> of Minerva (says Plutarch again)—whom they believe to be the same with...

ISIS, therefore, whom the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> believed to have been a *Queen*, and of whom they had a thousand different fables, was the *Nature of all things* according to the philosophers. These thinkers held the **UNIVERSE** to be the primary **GOD**, or the supreme being, and consequently abstruse or obscure, since none see beyond the surface of Nature. But this they only discovered to those who were initiated. To that inscription of *Sais* corresponds another inscription still remaining at *Capua*: **TO YOU, WHO ALONE ARE ALL THINGS, O GODDESS ISIS.** Though I am far from intending to exhaust commonplaces <note>standard rhetorical topics or clichés</note> on this occasion, yet I cannot resist inserting here part of a speech put into the mouth of Nature by **APULEIUS**.

### IV.

<image-desc>Large decorative initial 'T' featuring a bird on each side within an ornate border.</image-desc>**THIS** double manner of teaching was also in use among other Eastern <note>original: "oriental"</note> nations, especially the Ethiopians and Babylonians, the ancient and modern Brahmins <note>original: "Bramins"</note>, the Syrians, Persians, and the rest—principally those instructed by **ZOROASTER**.
<margin>Diogenes Laertius, in the *Preface*, Section 6. Also Ammianus Marcellinus, Book 15, Chapter 9. <note>original Latin: "DioG. Laert. in prooem. Sect. 6. Item Ammian. Marcellin. lib. 15. cap. 9."</note></margin>The classic books and recent travel accounts on this subject are common knowledge. The Druids of the Gauls and Britons would by no means reveal their mysteries or secret doctrines to anyone except those who were initiated. I will say nothing of the Etruscans <note>original: "Hetruscans"</note> and other Western nations, nor of the present-day Chinese, Siamese, and Indians properly so called; the fact is so well known that no one can deny it. From these various peoples, let us proceed to the Greek philosophers.
<margin>Diogenes Laertius, *Life of Pythagoras*. Also Iamblichus, *Life of Pythagoras*, with many others. <note>original Latin: "DioG. Laert. in PyTHAG. Item Jam-blic. Pyr. cum multis aliis."</note></margin>The most celebrated of these is the *secret discipline* of **PYTHAGORAS**, which served as the original model for the **ARISTOTELIAN** *Acroatics*—or, if you prefer, *Acroamatics*. <note>From the Greek *akroamatikos*, meaning "designed for hearing," referring to advanced lectures for a private audience.</note> More will be said about these later. Everything was explained to the *Esoteric* students (though without witnesses) in a plain, clear, and thorough...

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "twofold philosophy," moving from the general practices of Eastern nations to the specific example of the Pythagorean school in Greece. It contrasts the clear instruction given to inner-circle students with the riddles used for the public.</meta>

<header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

<margin>Diogenes Laertius, Book 8, Section 15. <note>original Latin: "DIOGEN. Laert. lib. 8. Sect. 15."</note></margin>
...detailed and comprehensive speech. On the other hand, everything delivered to the *Exoteric* <note>meaning: those outside the inner circle</note> was presented in a perplexing, obscure, and enigmatic manner. Nothing was told clearly, except for popular and common matters. The silence required of the latter, or the probation they had to undergo, lasted five years, or at least two years. But the silence of the former—whom they also called and considered *perfect*—was perpetual. This was not toward each other (as many have mistakenly believed), but only toward all those who were not part of their fellowship. This gave rise to their most common maxim: *that all things ought not to be declared to all men.* They reserved their own doctrines for themselves as holy secrets. If any outsiders happened to be present, they communicated their thoughts to each other through symbols, riddles, or parables. Consequently, it has unluckily happened that almost nothing of use or importance from them has reached public knowledge; this is the true reason for the obscurity, or rather the almost entire loss, of the Pythagorean philosophy.

<margin>
Iamblichus, *The Life of Pythagoras*, Chapter 17, Number 75. <note>original Latin: "JAMBLIC. in vita PYTHAG. cap. 17. n° 75."</note>
</margin>
<margin>
Clement of Alexandria, *Miscellanies*, Book 5, page 680. Origen, *Against Celsus*, Book 2, Cambridge Edition, page 67, and Book 3, page 142. <note>original Latin: "CLEM. Alex. Strom. lib. 5. pag. 680. ORIGEN. contra CELS. lib. 2. Edit. Cant. pag. 67. et lib. 3. pag. 142."</note>
</margin>
I shall give one example of this conduct. Lysis the Pythagorean severely scolded his fellow-student <note>original: "condisciple"</note> Hipparchus for having published some points of the *Esoteric Philosophy*, and for having shared their master's doctrine with men who were neither initiated nor prepared by contemplation and the necessary sciences. As a result, he was expelled from the school, and a monument was built for him according to the custom of the Pythagoreans, as if he were actually dead.

### V.

<margin>Clement of Alexandria, *Miscellanies*, Book 5, page 681. <note>original: "Strom. lib. 5. pag. 681."</note></margin>
Nor was such silence and reserve unique to the Pythagoreans. Let us therefore proceed from the Samian <note>meaning Pythagoras, who was from the island of Samos</note> to the Stagyrite <note>meaning Aristotle, who was born in the city of Stagira</note>. The *Aristotelians* (says Clement of Alexandria) assert that of their writings, some are indeed *Esoteric* <note>inner or private teachings</note>, while others are *common* and *Exoteric* <note>outer or public teachings</note>. This is very relevant to the point, and the passage is confirmed, if not greatly clarified, by Aulus Gellius in the following words:

<margin>*Attic Nights*, Book 20, Chapter 5. <note>original: "Noct. Attic. lib. 20. cap. 5."</note></margin>
> It is said that of the books and arts which the philosopher Aristotle—the tutor to King Alexander—delivered to his disciples, he had two sorts. Some he named *Exoteric*, others *Acroatic* <note>meaning "to be heard," referring to advanced oral teachings</note>. Those were called *Exoteric* which taught rhetorical studies, the skill of legal pleading or subtle debates, and the knowledge of political affairs; but those were styled *Acroatic* that pertained to the contemplation of Nature and to logical investigations.

<margin>The same source as above. <note>original: "Id. ibid."</note></margin>
He admitted all his disciples without distinction, and even such of the public as pleased, to his *Exoteric* lessons, which he read in the evening. But he spent the morning in the Lyceum <note>the gymnasium in Athens where Aristotle taught</note> explaining the *Acroatic* or *Esoteric* doctrine, to which (continues Gellius) he did not admit everyone indiscriminately, but only with caution and choice.

<margin>The same source as above. <note>original: "Id. ibid."</note></margin>
To Alexander, who complained that he published and made common his *Acroatic* books, Aristotle answered: "Know that they are neither published, nor yet unpublished, since they will only be intelligible to those who have been my students."

> *The way is rugged, and the book obscure;*
> *But if he leads you into his meaning,*
> *All is plain, and like the sun itself, most pure.*

The readers wanted a key that might open a passage for them into his secret meaning. Such a key—if I may suggest it in passing <note>original: "en passant"</note>—is for the most part to be borrowed by the skillful from the writers themselves.

## VI.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'B' in a square frame featuring foliate patterns and a small bird or mask at the top.</image-desc>

<margin>Quoted in **Augustine**, *Against the Academics*, book 3. <note>original: "Apud AUGUSTIN. contra Academ. lib. 6." Note: Augustine's work actually consists of three books; the "6" may refer to a specific edition or volume.</note></margin>
But dismissing the rest, as it would be an endless task to go over each one specifically, it was the custom of the **Academics** <note>The "Academics" were philosophers of the Platonic Academy who, in this period, were known for "Academic Skepticism"—the practice of questioning all claims to certainty.</note> (says **Cicero**) to conceal their true opinions. They would not reveal them to anyone except those who had lived with them until old age. I would have thought this was an over-caution, if I did not know that superstitious people are so very jealous and always intent on causing harm. What **Cicero** wrote somewhere regarding others applies just as aptly to the **Academics**.

<margin>**Cicero**, *On the Ends of Good and Evil*, book 5, chapter 5. <note>original: "De fin. bonor. & malor. lib. 5. cap. 5."</note></margin>
He says there are two kinds of books: *one written for the public, which they called "exoteric" <note>intended for or likely to be understood by the general public</note>; the other written more precisely, namely the "esoteric" <note>intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge</note>, which they left in their private notebooks or finished scholarly works.*

<margin>In the same place. <note>original: "Ibid."</note></margin>
From this, he rightly concludes *that these same philosophers do not always seem to say the same thing, even though they remained of the same opinion*. This is as true as truth itself regarding many writers in our own time. Therefore, the apparent obscurity of the **Academics**, their habit of doubting, and their perpetual suspension of judgment <note>original: "suspension of assent"</note> were not proposed because they actually doubted everything (which is impossible) or because they held back their agreement because things were naturally impossible to understand. Rather, it was first because they were unwilling to declare their judgment on every subject to everyone indifferently; and second, they wanted to be able to safely debate any subject whatsoever by equally maintaining both sides of the question. Nor will any man in his senses, we may reasonably

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how religious teachers used different methods for the public and the private. It specifically transitions from discussing the parables of Jesus to how the early Church Fathers began to obscure simple Christian rites by turning them into "mysteries" similar to those found in pagan religions.</meta>

<header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

...between them and the nations or sects I have brought onto the stage in this discourse. These, as we have shown, made use of fables out of fear or craft; those employed parables for edification and the more effective delivery of their doctrine. 

Yet, so that we may not leave this subject abruptly, basic concepts <note>original: "first notions"</note> are not better known than are the symbols, tropes, types, allegories, shadows, and mysteries, which are cited on every occasion by all kinds of Christians. It is not, therefore, either so strange or wonderful that it should be eagerly debated on every side, that it should be a matter of the most precise <note>original: "nicest"</note> inquiry, and a question raised with no small concern among such a prodigious variety of sects: what was, in reality, the *original and genuine institution of JESUS*? And this is true whether it was because at the beginning it was involved in such sacred obscurity, or rather that it was afterward complicated by the inventions of the wily or the imaginations of the weak. 

But in whatever manner it happened, I am downright ashamed of those <term>Church Fathers</term>, who made such ordinary actions as eating bread, drinking wine, and dipping in water (or washing with it) pass for *tremendous* and *unutterable mysteries*.

<margin>

</margin>

We grant that they are very intelligible and appropriate figures, very significant of the things they represent and exhibit; but they contain nothing terrible or abstruse, much less unutterable or inconceivable. Nevertheless, what those <term>Church Fathers</term> only pretended were <term>MYSTERIES</term>, so that they might not fall short of the paganism <note>original: "Heathenism"</note> they had left, their successors took care should become truly unintelligible, and be "tremendous" in more senses than one—or in any sense except that of the <term>Gospel</term>. Yet, as awful and frightening as the <term>Church Fathers</term> described the Christian <term>MYSTERIES</term>, they ordered them to be most diligently concealed;

<summary>Toland argues that while Jesus used parables for instruction, the early Church Fathers transformed simple Christian rituals into complex "mysteries" to compete with the allure of pagan traditions. He suggests these rites were originally clear and symbolic, rather than the terrifying or incomprehensible ceremonies they later became.</summary>

### VII.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'B' with birds and foliage.</image-desc>But why do I insist on particulars? Since this distinction between *Exoteric* <note>Publicly accessible knowledge</note> and *Esoteric* <note>Private or secret knowledge meant for a select few</note> *doctrines* was, as it were, the universal <note>original: "Catholic"</note> establishment of all nations. This shows that *universality* is no infallible mark of Truth, unless it is maintained that there is more wisdom than folly, more honesty than wickedness, and more knowledge than ignorance in the world. I have a multitude <note>original: "cloud"</note> of witnesses to confirm what I have advanced: but let <term>Clement of Alexandria</term> <note>original: "CLEMENS Alexandrinus," a 2nd-century Christian theologian</note> be their spokesman and interpreter. <margin>Miscellanies <note>original Latin: *Stromata*</note>, Book 5, page 658.</margin> *All those (he says) who have treated divine subjects, whether Barbarians or Greeks, concealed the principles of things; but they delivered the Truth through enigmas and symbols, through allegories and metaphors.* And indeed, not to speak of scheming <note>original: "designing"</note> men, many others were persuaded that both natural and divine things might best be explained through figures. Among these, the <term>philosopher Sallustius</term> <note>A 4th-century Neoplatonist writer</note> is not the least significant; although I am far from agreeing with his authority on this point. Whereas concealing the Truth under fables (he adds) restrains the former from despising it, and compels the latter to*

...to *philosophize*; as it obliges them to inquire into the right meaning of such fables, which prevents time from lying idle on their hands. But where is the necessity—what is the goal—of teaching the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note> anything they cannot understand or put into practice? And as for the *learned*, it is a joke to talk of exercising their diligence; time will never fail them in applying their efforts to what is intelligible and useful. For all those studies are vain, superfluous, even ridiculous, that relate to matters in themselves incomprehensible, or of no concern to human life. It is one thing not to speak or profess plainly all that you think, or to do so through indirect language <note>original: "circumlocution"</note> and figures of speech; but it is quite another thing to speak positively against your own judgment, or against the TRUTH in any figure of speech whatsoever. Despite this, I am obliged as a historian (since the *History of Public and Private <note>original: "Exoteric and Esoteric"</note> Philosophy* is my present subject) to report that there have been in ancient times, as well as today, several great men who declared it their opinion that FRAUD and SUPERSTITION were necessary means to keep the common people in good order. Among these, <term>Strabo</term> <note>A Greek geographer and historian (64 BC – 24 AD) known for his vast work "Geography"</note>—a man himself not in the least superstitious—justly claims the foremost place. In the first book of his invaluable *Geography*, he expresses his judgment in the following words: *Those* [<insert>speaking of artisans and the rest of the mob</insert>] *are turned from their vices when, either by words or certain frightening representations, they learn that punishments, terrors, and threats <note>original: "comminations"</note> proceed from the Gods, or when they believe such things have been inflicted on specific persons. For it is impossible to govern the mass of women and the miscellaneous common people <note>original: "promiscuous vulgar"</note> by philosophical discourses, or to lead them into religion, piety, or faithfulness; but for this purpose, SUPERSTITION is found to be necessary, which...*

# CLIDOPHORUS.

...can never *exist without fictions and miracles*. Therefore **Jupiter's** thunderbolts, **Minerva's** shield, **Neptune's** trident, the torches and serpents of the **Furies**, and those spears which are considered the weapons of the gods, **are all fables**, as is the **entire ancient theology**. For these things were accepted by **legislators** as so many scary stories <note>original: "bullbeggars," an archaic term for hobgoblins or objects of fear used to frighten children</note> to keep the foolish part of mankind in order. <margin>Amsterdam Edition, page 285.</margin> Nor is **Strabo**, as I hinted before, alone in this opinion concerning the use of fables. **Homer**—as **Dionysius** of *Halicarnassus* tells us in his *Life of Homer*—uses a *paradoxical and fabulous narration of things so that he may fill his readers with diligence and admiration, and leave his listeners amazed*. I will neither stubbornly deny that the fear <note>original: "horror"</note> of superstition instilled into the minds of men ever produced any good, nor will I easily admit that it ever had such a desirable effect. Given that, when all circumstances are properly considered, it must necessarily happen that superstition causes more evil than good in the world. But even granting that superstition had at any time proved beneficial to the public, at countless other times—and in matters of incomparably greater importance—it will be found detrimental, destructive, and utterly ruinous. It is advantageous to no one except **priests** or **princes**, who skillfully turn it to their own advantage, though even they are not always able to control it as they wish. It not only everywhere disturbs private society and harmony, and sometimes brings its sacred and sovereign managers to their breaking point <note>original: "last extremities"</note>;

but

### VIII.

<margin>Transmigration of souls. <note>original Greek: Μεταμψύχωσις (Metempsychosis)</note></margin>
To skip over many passages from the most famous defenders <note>original: "Apologists"</note> of myths and fables—such as those I have already named, along with **Proclus**, **Porphyry**, **Iamblichus**, and **Macrobius** (not to mention **Orpheus**, **Empedocles**, **Parmenides**, and other ancient figures who wrote in allegories)—**Timaeus of Locri** <note>original: "Timeus Locrus"</note>, that excellent disciple of **Pythagoras**, teaches the same doctrine as **Strabo**. He believes that superstition is just as necessary as law for the restraint and government of the people. Although the passage stating this is quite long, I will include it here in its entirety from his "golden treatise" *On the Soul of the World*. I do this especially because it makes it perfectly clear that neither **Pythagoras** nor the Pythagoreans actually believed in the "transanimation" or "transmigration of souls" <note>the belief that the soul moves into a new body after death</note>, despite being famous for that belief. Instead, they understood this word *esoterically* <note>intended for a small group of initiated students</note> to mean the constant flux or motion of all things, and the perpetual change of forms in matter; in this view, one form never decays or dies except to begin and take on another. Meanwhile, they taught *exoterically* <note>intended for the general public</note> to the common people <note>original: "promiscuous," meaning the unrefined or uneducated masses</note>...

<summary>The author argues that superstition destroys governments and robs individuals of peace, while revealing that the Pythagorean doctrine of reincarnation was actually an esoteric metaphor for the physical laws of matter.</summary>

### IX.
<margin>On the Nature of the Gods, the second chapter. <note>original: "De nat. Deor. cap. alt."</note></margin>
<margin>On the Gods and the World, chapter 3. <note>original: "De Diis et Mundo, cap. 3."</note></margin>
<T>S</T>o that I may now return specifically to the subject of fables, be persuaded of this (says **Phurnutus** <note>Also known as Lucius Annaeus Cornutus, a Stoic philosopher of the 1st century AD.</note>, the philosophizing divine, to his son): the *ancients* were not contemptible people, but were capable of understanding the nature of the universe; they were particularly skilled in philosophically explaining it through symbols and figures. I can easily grant this, and agree that knowledge of the nature of things is much better conveyed by allegories than by fables; for allegories only cover the truth, while fables confound and completely overwhelm it. Nothing is better known. But fables, urges **Sallustius** <note>Sallustius was a 4th-century Neoplatonist writer.</note>, declare the existence of the gods to all men; yet who the gods are and what they are like, fables reveal only to those who are capable of understanding. I would gladly have it be so, but who are those who are capable? <meta>This page continues the argument that while ancient philosophers used symbols to convey deep truths, these fables often lead to public confusion and corruption. ### CLIDOPHORUS.

[...conveyed by all,] whether the point in question is a real matter of fact or a fable invented for amusement, especially when we see the learned and the unlearned, and even entire communities and nations, divided on this subject. What is history to Caius <note>original: "C A I U S"; a generic Roman name used here as a placeholder for any person, similar to "John Doe"</note> is a fable to Publius <note>original: "P U B L I U S"; another generic Roman name</note>. Where is the contemporary witness? Or who else can be trusted? What limit or end will there be to fables if you once allow them to be used? 

How many objects of reverence and false worship are the result of misunderstood fables? How many gods and goddesses have been created this way? Their rites and festivals lead to idleness and debauchery, drunkenness and sloth—the fruitful sources of every vice. Meanwhile, the priests of these imaginary powers profit from the public's loss. This corruption is something that cannot be said or even imagined regarding the use of philosophical figures and symbols. 

**Plato** was so aware of this danger that, in the second book of his *Republic*, he forbade all those fables concerning the arguments and wars of the gods, their rapes, adulteries, murders, lies, thefts, and suffering. He banned such crimes and calamities because they gave people wrong ideas about the gods and perverted natural morality through their examples; for men will never believe an action to be a vice or imperfection in themselves if it is seen as a virtue or perfection in the divinity. 

Therefore, the same **Plato** banished **Homer** from his city, though he did so with great marks of honor. He knew well that all of Homer's fables veiled <note>original: "vail’d", meaning hidden or covered</note> matters of natural science or philosophy; however, to the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note>, these stories seemed to be about real people and events that had actually occurred. In the same way, while a learned person might use an image to excite reverence for a higher concept, an unlearned person’s worship ends at the physical images themselves. For my part, I entirely agree with **Plato** in the aforementioned second book: that certain things, such as those mentioned above, *ought not to be publicly accepted, whether they are presented as fables or otherwise;* for *young people* are

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the danger of using fables and myths to convey religious or philosophical truths. It specifically addresses the defense of these methods by ancient authors like Dionysius of Halicarnassus and rejects their application to religion.</meta>

### CLIDOPHORUS.

...are not able to *distinguish* if such stories are fables or not; and the opinions that are imbibed in that tender age can scarcely ever be *rectified*, or they are *eradicated* with the greatest difficulty. This is proven undeniably by daily experience. 

"Oh, but," says <term>Dionysius of Halicarnassus</term> <note>original: "DIONYSIUS Halicarnasseus"; a Greek historian and teacher of rhetoric active in the 1st century BC</note> in the *Life of <term>Homer</term>*, "it ought not to appear strange if he delivers his conceptions through enigmas and certain *fabulous discourses* <note>meaning stories or myths</note>. For this is caused partly by the requirements of poetry, and partly by the custom of the **Ancients**: so that the lovers of learning, being delightfully attracted by a certain elegance, might the more easily both seek after truth and discover it; and so that the unlearned might not despise those things which they cannot understand. For there is a time when what is said figuratively becomes pleasant, and what is clearly explained becomes contemptible." 

This I allow to be a sufficient apology regarding everything except <term>RELIGION</term>; in which I think all fables to be intolerable, and that no allegories ought to be admitted, except only in expressing the Divine <term>NATURE</term> and <term>ATTRIBUTES</term> <note>such as using metaphors like "the hand of God" to describe divine power</note>. To say that fables will quicken our diligence <note>meaning to sharpen our focus or effort to understand</note> is the same plea used for <term>mysteries</term>; as if time hung heavily on our hands, and we had no other occupation but <term>criticism</term> <note>original: "Criticism"; here referring to the scholarly analysis and decoding of difficult texts</note>. 

Besides, if one or the other can be explained, why do they not become contemptible after they become known, according to the reasoning of <term>Dionysius</term> and others? Or if both of them are incomprehensible and unfathomable, our diligence is in vain, and we are exercised about nothing; or, which is the same thing, to no purpose.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the use of fables and allegories in religion. <header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

<image-desc>A decorative rectangular woodcut headpiece featuring elaborate scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

### X.

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial letter 'I' set within a frame of foliage and vines.</image-desc>
IN conceiving, explaining, and declaring the **DIVINE NATURE** and **ATTRIBUTES**, I readily admit that symbols and metaphors are not only appropriate and useful, but the latter are even absolutely necessary. In truth, no ideas of them can be shared with the uneducated in any other way—though this is not the place to explain the reasons for that. However, as far as this matter concerns our subject of *the Exoteric and Esoteric Doctrine* <note>The "Exoteric" refers to external, public teachings for the masses, while the "Esoteric" refers to internal, private teachings reserved for the initiated or learned.</note>, the most ancient philosophers were highly aware of these difficulties regarding common perceptions. They were also rightly cautious of the slander, malice, violence, and rage of the superstitious. Consequently, there was nothing they were more anxious to conceal than their real judgment concerning the **DIVINE NATURE**. They observed that few people maintained a calm temperament when discussing this point, especially when they were unable to defend their own opinions or disprove the opinions of others. 

We have explained this temperament previously in our discussion of the priests, who stamped this same impression onto the minds of the people. What reason cannot support, force must; and no one will be permitted to say anything that shows the multitude to be ridiculous or their guides to be impostors. This kept the philosophers everywhere on their guard. 

<margin>*On the Nature of the Gods* <note>original: "De nat. Deor."; Cicero's "De Natura Deorum."</note>, book 1, chapter 22.</margin>**SIMONIDES** <note>A famous Greek lyric poet known for his wisdom.</note>, as we are informed by **TULLY** <note>original: "TULLY"; Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and philosopher.</note>, *was once asked by the tyrant* **HIERO** *what* **GOD** *was, or what his nature was. Simonides requested the space of one day to think about the subject. When the same question was asked the next day, he begged for two days.*

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while metaphors are necessary to describe God to the public, philosophers historically hid their true theological views to avoid the violent reactions of superstitious people and their leaders. He begins an anecdote from Cicero about the poet Simonides being unable to define the nature of God for King Hiero.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument about why ancient philosophers concealed their true beliefs regarding the divine. # CLIDOPHORUS.

But when he had frequently doubled the number of days, and Hiero, wondering, asked why he did so? "Because," he said, "the longer I consider it, the more obscure this matter appears." It was not that he had nothing to answer, but that it was dangerous to tell the truth—or to do so without ambiguity and indirectness—as could be easily proven by the examples of various philosophers. <margin>Tertullian, *Against the Nations*, book 2, chapter 2.</margin> Thales of Miletus, when Croesus asked him "What are the <term>GODS</term>?", answered "Nothing," after obtaining several days for deliberation—provided that <term>Tertullian</term> (in his usual manner) has not confused Hiero with Croesus, or Simonides with Thales. When Euclid of Megara was asked by someone, "Of what nature are the <term>GODS</term>, and in what things do they chiefly delight?" he answered that he was ignorant of all other things, but one thing he knew for certain: they hated curious <note>original: "curious," here meaning meddlesome or overly inquisitive into divine secrets</note> people. For Euclid was well aware that a great questioner is a great babbler.

<margin>Johannes Stobaeus, *Sermons*, Transcurt edition, page 164.</margin><margin>Diogenes Laertius, book 2, section 117.</margin> <term>Stilpo</term> was likewise asked by Crato whether the <term>GODS</term> took pleasure in prayers and prostrations <note>Acts of bowing or laying face-down in worship.</note>. "You fool," he said, "do not consult me about such things in the street, but at home." He used almost the same expressions here as <margin>The same author [Diogenes Laertius], book 4, section 46.</margin> Bion the Borysthenite, who answered someone asking if there were any <term>Gods</term>:

> Old man, have you sent the crowd far away?

These philosophers could have all given proper answers, but they were unwilling to cause offense by declaring the truth, lest they should bring the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note> against them. The thoughtlessness of the masses has in all ages proven to be the greatest support for priests, so much so that even in a certain Christian Church it has become a proverb that "the ignorance of the laity is the revenue of the clergy." This is consistent with what has been related regarding several philosophers:

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that philosophers throughout history have hidden their true beliefs from the public. While the previous page discussed the evasiveness of Thales, Euclid, and Stilpo, this page moves to the examples of Rabbi Hillel, Anaxagoras, and Plato to show that complex truths about the nature of the universe were often kept secret to avoid offending the religious sensibilities of the common people.</meta>

# CLIDOPHORUS.

...philosophers, I have somewhere read of the famous Rabbi Hillel, that to one inquiring of him with great curiosity, *What was the nature of GOD?*, he answered, "If I knew it, my son, yet I should be far from telling you my thoughts about it." <margin>Letters to SERENA, page 114.</margin> To illustrate this point yet further, by what I observed on another occasion: the true reason why, at a certain time, the theory of the stars and planets was so little known in Greece—or at least not generally known—was that the common people would not endure to hear those things made subject to a philosophical examination. They refused to have them explained by the ordinary laws of nature, by involuntary causes <note>Natural laws that operate without a conscious will.</note>, and blind faculties; for they were taught that the stars were intelligent, eternal, and immortal <term>GODS</term>. And therefore, when **Anaxagoras** discovered that the moon had but a borrowed light <margin>In the *Life of Nicias*.</margin> <note>original: *In vita NIAE.*, referring to Plutarch's *Life of Nicias*.</note> from the sun, and so gave the reasons for its waxing and waning, such a doctrine (as **Plutarch** assures us) dared not be made public. Instead, it was secretly committed to very few, and even to them only under a promise of fidelity. What examples could I not produce to the same purpose!

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that philosophers throughout history have used "double-speak" to protect themselves from religious persecution. Having established that early thinkers kept their discoveries about the heavens secret, the author now introduces Varro’s classification of theology to explain how philosophers categorized public myths versus private truths.</meta>

## CLIDOPHORUS.

To begin any <term>discourse about God</term> <note>A formal discussion or debate about the nature of divinity.</note> among men of prejudiced opinions is by no means safe; for whether truth is told or falsehood, it is equally dangerous. From this difficulty—or rather, from a lack of confidence and a need for caution—sprang Varro’s <term>threefold Theology: the Mythical, Physical, and Political; or the Fabulous, Philosophical, and Civil</term> <note>The Roman scholar Varro categorized "theology" into three types: the myths of poets (fabulous), the logical inquiries of philosophers (physical/natural), and the rituals required by the state (civil).</note>. 

<margin>In Augustine's *City of God*, book 6, chapter 5.</margin> <note>original: *Apud Augustin Civitate Dei, lib. 6. cap. 5.*</note>

Of these three, the first and the last sorts are certain masks for the truth, or rather, ingenious excuses for not telling it. Varro says that they call "mythical theology" that which is chiefly used by the poets, "physical" by the philosophers, and "political" by specific nations. He absolutely rejects the first and the last, but he approves of the second, which men's ears can more easily tolerate within the walls of a school than abroad in the open market-place. This is because, as he says there, <term>natural theology</term> relates to the UNIVERSE; and Augustine very rightly says that those philosophers judge nothing more excellent than the universe to exist. 

<margin>Ibid.</margin> <note>original: *Ibid.*, meaning "in the same place" as the previous citation.</note>

Among the rest, this is most notoriously true of the Stoics, who reduced all the mythical and popular theology to natural theology. They explained all the fables of the poets and the common people in such a way that they appeared to have been originally meant to describe natural causes and effects. 

<margin>Ibid.</margin>

They were too sharp-witted to accept the truth of such myths in the literal sense, and too prudent to reject them all as nonsense. This led them, of course, by the principle of self-preservation, to impose a tolerable meaning of their own upon these myths. In this way, they might not be judged to have completely denied the religion currently in fashion, but only to differ from others regarding the purpose and interpretation of it. This artifice, which I suspect has not died out with the Stoics, could not escape the penetration of **Cicero**, who yet sometimes had recourse to it himself. 

<margin>On the Nature of the Gods, book 3, chapter 24.</margin> <note>original: *De Nat. Deor. lib. 3. cap. 24.*</note>

First **Zeno**, after him **Cleanthes**, and then **Chrysippus**, were at great pains to no purpose to give a reasonable explanation...

<summary>The author explains Varro’s "threefold theology" as a survival strategy used by philosophers to hide their true naturalistic views behind popular religious myths. He specifically highlights how the Stoics reinterpreted public fables to avoid being accused of atheism or heresy.</summary>

<keywords>Varro, Augustine, Cicero, Zeno, Cleanthes, Chrysippus, threefold theology, mythical theology, physical theology, political theology, natural theology, Stoics, religious persecution, self-preservation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Varro's "threefold theology" (mythical, physical, and civil), focusing on how ancient philosophers like Cicero used "physical" or natural explanations to decode religious myths for an elite audience while recognizing their potential to spread superstition among the public.</meta>

# CLIDOPHORUS

...explanation of made-up fables <note>original: "commentitious"</note>, and to account for the origin <note>original: "etymology"</note> of the names of every god. When you do this, he adds, you plainly suggest that the truth is quite different from what the majority of mankind believes. You are claiming that those who are held to be gods represent the nature of specific things rather than the actual history of divine beings. 

The same Cicero often expresses his dislike of fables elsewhere, considering them to be—if not the parents—then certainly the promoters of superstition. <margin>Ibid, book 2, chapter 24.</margin> For another reason, he says—and a natural <note>original: "physical"</note> one at that—a great number of gods has emerged. These gods, presented in human form, have provided the poets with stories, but at the same time have filled the lives of men with all kinds of superstition. 

For anyone who does not wish to go through the trouble of reading many volumes, but still wants to learn the natural <note>original: "physical"</note> explanations that the Stoics and other philosophers gave for pagan mythology—including the names and histories of every god and goddess—I recommend the short book written by Cornutus (commonly called Phurnutus) called *On the Nature of the Gods*. It is not only the most clever but also the best in every way. 
<margin>Phurnutus, *On the Nature of the Gods*; or, as in some manuscripts, Cornutus’s *Summary of Traditions Concerning Greek Theology* <note>original Greek: Φουρνουθου περι Θεων Φυσεως... Κουρνουτου επιδρομη των κατα την Ἑλληνικην Θεολογιαν παραδεδομενων</note>.</margin>

<image-desc>Large ornate initial capital T, with a sun-like face at the top, enclosing scrolling patterns.</image-desc> To return now to Varro: his account of ancient triple theology is fully approved by Plutarch, who even copies his words—though rearranging the order slightly—as the following passage makes as clear as day.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "threefold theology" (mythical, natural, and civil) as analyzed by ancient Roman thinkers, specifically focusing on how religious tradition was used as a tool of statecraft.</meta>

## CLIDOPHORUS.

The worship of the **Gods** made use of three kinds of **forms**: the **natural**, the **fabulous**, and the **legal**. The **Philosophers** teach the **natural**, the **Poets** the **fabulous**, and the **several Governments** appoint their **own laws**. They do this by inserting into the **calendars** <note>original: "Calenders"</note> the **heroes** of their own country, the **inventors** of **useful arts**, and those who became **benefactors** to **mankind** in any way.

Consequently, these individuals were first **consecrated** to **immortal memory** in the *Fasti* <term>Fasti</term> <note>Official Roman calendars that recorded festivals and legal days</note> by the **magistrates**. Next, they were advanced by the **populace** into the same **immortal nature** of the **Gods**, whose **anniversary festivals** were **marked there**. Meanwhile, the more **knowing** or **learned** exposed the **folly** of these beliefs; yet at the same time, through their **allegories**, they **pretended** to **justify** <note>original: "vindicate"</note> those **Gods** from contempt. In this way, they no less **skillfully** <note>original: "dextrouſly"</note> than **prudently** **concealed** their own **opinions**.

Not **only** before **Plutarch**, but likewise before <margin>In St. Augustine, *The City of God*, book 4, chapter 27.</margin> **Varro**, the **High Priest** <note>original: "Pontif"</note> **Scaevola** distinguished these **three kinds** of **Gods**: *the first owing their being to the Poets, the second to the Philosophers, and the third to the Legislators or Magistrates*. The **first sort he esteemed** **idle** and **ridiculous**; nor **could he help judging the last to be most false**, or **in other words, an imposture**. **Nevertheless**, he **would not have it much disturbed**, much less **totally abolished** <note>original: "abrogated"</note>.

For he held the same **opinion**, **which was afterwards openly defended** <margin>Ibid, chapter 31.</margin> **by Marcus Varro**: *that many things were true which it was not only useful that the people should not know, but also that, though absolutely false, it was expedient that the people should believe otherwise*. **This is** **at present the favorite maxim**, **not of two or three persons only in a country** who juggle with the **public** and imagine that deceiving the people is the highest **Reason of State**; **but it is likewise the common talk of many others**, **elevated indeed** **above the vulgar rank, but naturally timorous**, or...

<summary>This page discusses the ancient Roman distinction between the "natural" gods of philosophers, the "fabulous" gods of poets, and the "legal" gods of the state. It explains that high-ranking officials like Scaevola and Varro viewed civil religion as a useful fiction or "pious fraud" intended to maintain social order.</summary>
<keywords>threefold theology, Scaevola, Marcus Varro, Reason of State, religious imposture, Fasti, allegories, St. Augustine, political philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that philosophers throughout history have used "occult" or hidden modes of communication to protect themselves from institutional religious authorities and the potential violence of the uneducated public.</meta>

# CLIDOPHORUS <note>Greek: *Klēidophoros*, meaning "The Key-bearer"</note>

...or corrupt time-servers <note>original: "prostitute time-servers," referring to those who compromise their integrity for social or political gain</note>. In the meantime, we should observe most carefully that the priests were always the reason why the philosophers invented those hidden ways of speaking and writing. While the priests industriously concealed their mysteries—lest, if they were clearly understood, they might be exposed by the philosophers to the laughter of the people as being based on fables, false, and useless—the philosophers, on the other hand, concealed their opinions on the nature of things under the veil of divine allegories. They did this so that they would not be accused of impiety by the priests (which often happened) and thus be exposed, in turn, to the hatred, if not the fury, of the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note>. 

Now, since philosophers held this cautious conduct to be lawful and even necessary for self-preservation, I can easily imagine that a true philosopher experienced three distinct passions during a religious sacrifice: despising the **MOB**, detesting the **PRIEST**, and delighting in his own **LIBERTY**.

## XIII.

This reminds me of what I was told by a close relative of the old Lord **SHAFTESBURY** <note>Anthony Ashley Cooper, 1st Earl of Shaftesbury (1621–1683), a prominent Whig politician</note>. ...or prostitute time-servers. For while the priests diligently hid their mysteries to prevent philosophers from exposing them to public ridicule as mythical, false, and useless, the philosophers, on the other hand, hid their true thoughts on the nature of reality behind a veil of religious allegories. They did this so they would not be accused of impiety by the priests (which happened often) or exposed to the hatred—and perhaps the fury—of the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note>. Since philosophers considered this cautious behavior both lawful and necessary for their own self-preservation, I can easily imagine that a true philosopher, while attending a religious sacrifice, felt three distinct passions: contempt for the mob, detestation for the **priest**, and delight in his own **liberty**.

## CLIDOPHORUS <note>Greek: *Klēidophoros*, meaning "The Key-bearer"</note>

...notwithstanding those infinite divisions caused by the self-interest of the priests and the ignorance of the people. **ALL WISE MEN ARE OF THE SAME RELIGION**: whereupon a lady in the room, who seemed to pay more attention to her needlework <note>original: "mind her needle"</note> than to their conversation, asked with some concern what that religion was? To whom the Lord **SHAFTESBURY** <note>Anthony Ashley Cooper, 3rd Earl of Shaftesbury (1671–1713), a prominent philosopher and politician</note> immediately replied, "*Madam, wise men never tell*." And indeed, considering how dangerous it is made to tell the truth, it is difficult to know when any man declares his real opinions on things. 

I have often, I confess, read of desires expressed by some, and requests <note>original: "desirances"</note> made to others (who are not tolerated by the laws) to produce their arguments. But this was, in other words, asking them to get themselves disgraced or punished, deprived of their positions, or excommunicated. Nay, I have read of invitations to heretics, deists, and atheists to speak openly <note>original: "above-board"</note>; and heard it much lamented that the books of such people were formerly destroyed, or that they are not allowed to write now: because Truth would thereby triumph all the more, and these unfortunate men be more easily reclaimed <note>original: "reclaimed," meaning brought back to the orthodox faith</note>. 

Many of those who expressed such desires, being very good men, would be not a little sorry if the others had complied; they would find they had drawn them into a snare out of which they could not free <note>original: "extricate"</note> them. And as for those whose invitations are intended to decoy and trap <note>original: "trepan," an archaic word for ensnaring or cheating someone</note>, they must be left to the conscience of their own base designs; just as the persons deluded by them ought to suffer without pity for their folly. 

Now, if it is a desirable thing to have the truth told without disguise, there is but one method to procure such a blessing. *Let all men freely speak what they think, without ever being branded or punished except for wicked practices, and leave their theoretical <note>original: "speculative"</note> opinions to be confuted or approved by whoever pleases: then you are sure to hear the whole truth, and*
