## CLIDOPHORUS <note>Greek: *Klēidophoros*, meaning "The Key-bearer"</note>

...until then, only very scantily or obscurely, if at all. Until then, I say, *Transubstantiation* <note>The Catholic doctrine that the bread and wine of the Eucharist become the actual body and blood of Christ</note> will be professed in one country by those who believe it as little as many in another country believe the *Athanasian Creed* <note>A Christian statement of faith focused on the Trinity and Incarnation, often criticized in this period for its "damnatory clauses" against those who disagree</note>. But why do I speak of this or that country, when examples in all countries are countless? Nor will the latest example in our own country be that of a certain Doctor, who wrote about *Difficulties Esoterically* <note>meaning: written for a private, learned audience</note> and preached a *Sermon* exoterically <note>meaning: intended for the general public</note>. 

In this state of affairs, while liberty in its full extent is more to be wished for than expected—and since, through human weakness, people will prefer their peace, reputation, or career advancement <note>original: "preferments"</note> over speaking the Truth—there is nonetheless one observation left to us by which we can make a probable judgment of the sincerity of others in declaring their opinions. It is this: *When a man maintains what is commonly believed, or professes what is publicly enjoined <note>meaning: commanded by law or authority</note>, it is not always a sure rule that he speaks what he thinks; but when he seriously maintains the opposite of what is established by law, and openly declares for what most others oppose, then there is a strong presumption that he is speaking his mind.* 

Yet even this observation may be found to fail unless full and impartial liberty exists—which I could show, were this the place for it, to be very consistent with national religious establishments. But to provide in one person an example of both liberty and reserve—of external and internal doctrines, an ample profession of speaking what he thinks, and an absolute uncertainty whether he actually does so or not—I shall bring <term>SYNESIUS</term> <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373 – c. 414 AD), a Neo-Platonist philosopher who became a Christian bishop despite holding views that contradicted church dogma</note> here onto the stage. He was a man of high social standing and brilliant intellect who, from being a pagan philosopher of the Platonic school, became Bishop of Ptolemais <note>A city in ancient Cyrenaica, modern-day Libya</note> at the beginning of the fifth century. By that time, bishops had become ministers of state rather than overseers of their flocks. This made the office of bishop <note>original: "Episcopate"</note> a position of great honor and authority, but subject to...

<summary>Toland argues that public religious profession is often insincere due to social pressure, suggesting that truth is only likely found in those who challenge established laws. He introduces Synesius of Cyrene as a historical example of a philosopher who navigated these conflicting internal and external doctrines.</summary>

<header>CLIDOPHORUS.</header>

He expresses his aversion to the crosier <note>original: "Croſier," the hooked staff carried by a bishop as a symbol of office</note> for several reasons, being led by his own inclination to remain in the modesty of philosophy rather than being raised to the grandeur of the episcopacy <note>the office or rank of a bishop</note>. He feared that the latter would ruin the former by making him proud and arrogant. He seems most unwilling to leave his wife, which he protests he will not do; instead, he wishes to have many children by her, as he already had two or three.

<margin>Ibid. <note>Latin: "In the same place," referring to the previously cited letters.</note></margin>

But this is not all. "It is difficult," he says, "or rather absolutely impossible, to unsettle those doctrines which, through knowledge, are rooted in the soul to a point of demonstration. But you know," he continues, "that philosophy is diametrically opposite to common doctrines." Well, what were those doctrines he could not stomach <note>original: "digeſt"</note>? 

<margin>Ibid.</margin>

"Truly," he says, "I shall never be persuaded that the soul had no being before the body; I shall not profess that the world and all its parts shall perish together; and the threadbare doctrine of the resurrection I conceive to signify something sacred and secret, but I am very far from approving the prejudices of the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note>."

<margin>He believed in preexistence.</margin>

As you can see, these are not trivial or unimportant points at which he hesitates <note>original: "boggles"</note>. Since the resurrection of the dead was one of the articles essentially required to be believed by those who were baptized, how was it possible he could be considered a Christian, however he might—as things went then—be a very good bishop? 

<margin>Codex 26.</margin>

The memory of **Photius** <note>Photius I (c. 810–893 AD), a Patriarch of Constantinople known for his *Bibliotheca*, a collection of summaries of ancient books</note> failed him when he wrote that it was in a letter to **Theophilus** <note>Theophilus of Alexandria (died 412 AD), the Patriarch who ordained Synesius</note> that **Synesius** denied the resurrection, whereas it was actually in this letter to his brother. Or perhaps he also did so in a letter to that Patriarch of Alexandria which has since been lost. 

The same Photius plainly asserts that, although he persisted in his denial of the resurrection, they ordained Synesius as a bishop anyway. They did not doubt that such a good man, endowed with such uncommon virtues, would...

"*Therefore*," he says, "*if this approach is consistent with the rights of the episcopal office <note>original: "Epiſcopal dignity"</note>, I may be consecrated. He repeats this rule over and over. Despite this permitted freedom among philosophers, he calls God and men to witness that if his episcopal duties require otherwise, he will not falsely represent doctrines. So far, so good. He also repeats this resolution more than once in the strongest terms—if swearing by God counts as such. 

<margin>In the same place.</margin>
I will skip over his reluctance to give up his weapons <note>original: "arms"</note>, his horses, and his favorite dogs as trivial matters compared to his other objections.

But some might ask, why will you not accept his final resolution? I answer: because he teaches me to do otherwise. In all his works—both those he wrote after becoming a bishop and those from before—he teaches, explains, and praises the distinction between *exoteric* <note>intended for or likely to be understood by the general public</note> and *esoteric* <note>intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge</note> doctrine. This, I say, he does everywhere, and especially where...

# HYPATIA.

### I.

<image-desc>Large ornamental initial 'I' depicting an interior scene with several robed figures, possibly ancient or biblical, standing around a vessel. The figures appear to be conversing or observing.</image-desc>

I am going to give a short account—though as full as ancient books provide us materials for—of the life and death of HYPATIA, <note>Hypatia of Alexandria (c. 350–415 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher, astronomer, and mathematician who was murdered by a mob.</note> who will always be the glory of her own sex and the disgrace of ours. For women have no less reason to take pride in the fact that there existed a lady of such rare accomplishments, without the slightest blemish to serve even as a foil <note>original: "foil"; a contrast used to enhance the luster of a gem.</note> to her countless perfections, than men have reason to be ashamed that any could be found among them of such a brutal and savage disposition. Far from being struck with admiration at such beauty, innocence, and knowledge, they stained their barbarous hands with her blood and their impious souls with the permanent mark of sacrilegious murderers. A bishop, a patriarch—indeed, a saint <note>The author is referring to Cyril, the Patriarch of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD), who was later canonized but is historically implicated in the tension leading to Hypatia's death.</note>—was the mastermind behind such a horrific deed, and his clergy were the executioners of his relentless fury. The authors from whom I gather my account (and I omit none that has come to my knowledge—

## II.

After **Alexander the Great** founded Alexandria in Egypt as the center of commerce for the empire he was planning, this city soon became a flourishing marketplace for learning as well as for trade. The fame of the *Alexandrian School* and the *Alexandrian Library* reached much further than the name of **Alexander** himself; or at least, they carried his name to places it could never have reached without them. This was the most appropriate tribute that could be paid in gratitude to the memory of a ruler so ambitious for glory. Indeed, no private individuals, any more than powerful monarchs, will ever do anything praiseworthy without the prospect of

The list of great men who presided over this School can be found in the works of those who have specifically written on such subjects. My plan, however, requires me to mention one of them here: **THEON**, who governed that Academy with great acclaim during the latter part of the fourth century. He was particularly famous for his extensive knowledge of astronomy, as the records made of those who excelled in this science clearly show. But what has contributed to making him more famous to all future generations is that he was the father of the incomparable **HYPATIA**. Whether following the custom of those times or, more likely, prompted by the encouragement he received from her own promising genius, he educated her not only in all the skills expected of her gender but also had her instructed in the most complex <note>original: "abstruse"</note> sciences. These subjects are generally considered the proper occupation of men, as they were thought to require too much labor and focus for the delicate constitution of women.

# IV.

<margin>In the places already cited <note>original: "In locis jam citatis"</note>.</margin>
<image-desc>An ornamental drop-cap letter 'A' set within a square decorative woodcut frame with foliage patterns.</image-desc>
AND truly, if this matter were not so well attested by those writers we have just named, and by others we shall presently have occasion to cite, no one could any longer doubt it after being informed by these very same people that **HYPATIA** succeeded to the leadership of the **PLATONIC** school at Alexandria, the place of her birth and education. This was a far different thing <note>original: "another-guess thing" — an archaic idiom meaning "of a different sort"</note>, God knows, than taking a doctoral degree in any of the academic faculties—which one or two women have not long since done, and for which they have been loaded with fulsome praises <note>original: "elogies"</note>, though producing no results suitable to the titles they so greatly desired.

But what greater glory could there be for a woman—what greater honor redounding to all women—than to see a lady teaching from that chair where **AMMONIUS** and **HIEROCLES** (to name no more, for it is a mistake in **SOCRATES** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, the church historian</note> or his transcriber to include **PLOTINUS** among them) once sat? I say, it was a chair where so many professors uttered the oracles of learning more like divine intelligences than mortal men. What infinite merit must she have possessed to be preferred for that prominent position at a time when men of immense learning abounded both in Alexandria and in many other parts of the Roman Empire? Therefore, considering the novelty of the situation and the fact that **HYPATIA’s** worth was universally acknowledged, it is no wonder that she soon had a crowded audience <note>original: "auditory"</note>. She explained to her hearers, says **SO-**

This shows an incredible intellectual height and capacity, as each of these schools separately was thought to be a large enough field to test the hard work of any one man, no matter how skilled in literature he might be.

### V.

<margin>Suidas in the place cited <note>original: "SUIDAS in loco citato"</note>.</margin>
<margin>Companions <note>original: "Ἑταῖροι"</note>.</margin>
NOW, I cannot help but imagine here with pleasure—let whoever will criticize me for it—the flower of all the youth in Europe, Asia, and Africa, sitting at the feet of a most beautiful lady (for we are assured HYPATIA was such). They were all greedily swallowing instruction from her mouth, and many of them love from her eyes. How she dealt with one of this latter sort shall be told in its proper place. It was undoubtedly impossible not to improve under such a teacher; one would have to be equally stupid and insensitive not to be powerfully affected by a charming mind in a charming body. I am sure this reflection is very much in line with the Philosophy she specifically practiced; and accordingly, the Alexandrian School never flourished more. Her disciples entered into a strict bond of intimacy with one another, styling themselves *Companions* <note>original: "Ἑταῖροι"</note>, or, as in our colleges,

## VI.

One of these students, who has preserved for us the names of several others, is the celebrated **Synesius**. He was a native of Cyrene in Africa, on the borders of Egypt—a very ancient Greek colony and the birthplace of **Aristippus** <note>A student of Socrates and founder of the Cyrenaic school of philosophy</note> and **Carneades** <note>A skeptical philosopher who headed the Academy in Athens</note>—which **Synesius** does not forget to mention in his writings. He traveled for <margin>Against Andronicus <note>One of Synesius's political speeches</note>.</margin> improvement to the neighboring country of Egypt (the undisputed mother of the sciences), where he successfully completed his studies at Alexandria under **Hypatia**. This person alone may serve as a specimen of the extraordinary spirits that she shaped. If we may rely on the judgment of no less a man than **Nicephorus Gregoras**, Patriarch of Constantinople (who wrote elaborate <margin>Scholia <note>Explanatory notes or commentary</note>.</margin> annotations on Synesius's treatise *On Dreams*, a work filled with uncommon learning), he says, *“there was nothing <margin>In the introduction to the Scholia.</margin> he did not know, no science in which he did not excel, and no mystery in which he was not initiated or skilled.”*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Synesius of Cyrene's correspondence with Hypatia, specifically focusing on the letters he sent to her alongside his scholarly works.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>HYPATIA</header>

<meta>The text continues from the previous page's mention of Synesius's correspondence with Hypatia.</meta>

He wrote to her with the reasons for his writing two books, which he sent to her with that letter. One was his mystical treatise *On Dreams*; and the other was his *Dion*. This last work is a most ingenious apology <note>original: "Apology." Here meaning a formal justification or defense of a position.</note> for learning, directed against two types of men who, though they took very different paths, both arrived at the same point of ignorance. 

The first group, under the pretense of being "reserved" toward "unworthy" listeners, used this as a veil to conceal their own lack of real knowledge. They accused Synesius of being too communicative and of "prostituting" philosophy by making it accessible. 

The others wanted him to chatter eternally like themselves. This was not because they studied more than others—or even as much—to provide themselves with subject matter for discourse. Instead, they talked by rote out of certain "systems," the truth of which they took for granted and which no one was allowed to contradict. In this way, they could exhaust the patience of their hearers without making their audience or themselves a single bit wiser. 

Both groups charged him with focusing too much on elegance and oratory in his writings. At that time, the theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> were rapidly replacing philosophy and other learning with legends and "enthusiasm" <note>In the 18th-century sense, "enthusiasm" often referred to misplaced religious fanaticism or claims of direct divine inspiration without reason.</note>, fables and fantasies, which they sanctified with the name of *Divine Contemplation*. Metaphysical distinctions about the *Trinity* and extravagant notions about the essence of **GOD** (whose majesty they blasphemed with their profane definitions) were the only studies then in fashion, to the irreparable damage of refined and useful literature <note>original: "Letters"</note>.

### VIII.

<margin>In the same work</margin>
Therefore, he asks for Hypatia’s judgment on his work *Dion*, deciding not to publish it without her approval. He further informs her that she is the first among the Greeks—or rather, the pagans <note>original: "Heathens"</note>—to whom he has shared his *Treatise on Dreams*. And so that he might complete, he says, the sacred number *three*, he adds to these two his account of the *Astrolabe* <note>An ancient astronomical instrument used for solving problems relating to time and the position of the sun and stars.</note> presented to Peonius. It will not be a digression entirely foreign <note>The OCR "soren" is a transcription error for "foren," an archaic spelling of "foreign."</note> to the subject (as we shall see later) if we include here part of the fine description he gives of the second sort of people who criticized him. These men, he says, though full of ignorance yet armed with confidence, are more ready than all other men to lecture about God. If you happen to encounter them, you will immediately hear some of their illogical arguments <note>original: "unreasonable reasonings"</note>, which they insist on forcing upon those who have no desire for such things; because, I suppose, it is in their interest to do so. For on the basis of such things, they are made preachers in towns—which is the same thing as possessing Amalthea’s horn <note>In Greek mythology, the cornucopia or "horn of plenty" which provides an unending supply of food and drink.</note>, or an abundance of all things, which these men think themselves entitled to use. I imagine that by now you perceive what kind of bold men these are who criticize my noble purpose. They invite me to join their discipline, promising that in a short time I will appear most confident in matters relating to God, and will forever after be capable of arguing incessantly both night and day. I believe this breed of men is not yet extinct; but another time, they...

# HYPATIA.

He explains to her the reasons for his writing two books, which he sends to her with his letter. One was his mystical <term>Treatise on *Dreams*</term>, and the other was his <term>*DION*</term>. This latter work is a most clever defense <note>original: "Apology"</note> for learning against two types of men who, following very different paths, arrived at the same point of ignorance. 

The first group, under the pretense of being selective toward "unworthy" listeners, concealed their own lack of real knowledge. They accused him of being too communicative and of "prostituting" philosophy <note>That is, making complex philosophical truths too accessible to the common person.</note>. 

The others wanted him to babble incessantly just as they did. It was not that they studied more than others—or even as much—to prepare themselves with material for discussion. Instead, by talking from memory <note>original: "by rote"</note> using certain <term>*Systems*</term> of thought—the truth of which they took for granted and which no one was allowed to contradict—they could exhaust the patience of their listeners without making their audience or themselves any wiser. 

Both groups accused him of focusing too much on elegance and public speaking in his writings. During that time, the <term>theologians</term> <note>original: "Divines"</note> were rapidly replacing philosophy and other fields of learning with legends, religious fervor <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note>, fables, and fantasies, which they sanctified with the name <term>*Divine Contemplation*</term>. Complex metaphysical distinctions about the <term>*Trinity*</term> and wild ideas about the nature of <term>GOD</term> (whose majesty they insulted by their worldly <note>original: "profane"</note> definitions) were the only studies then in fashion, much to the permanent damage of refined and useful literature <note>original: "Letters"</note>.

### IX.

Upon his promotion—or, as he himself considered it, his banishment—to the bishopric of Ptolemais, he was forced to leave the company of his fellow students <note>original: "Condisciples"</note> and the presence of his dear Hypatia. He soon lost his wife, followed by his children shortly after; he loved them very dearly, and he did not bear their deaths with the same strength of mind <note>original: "fortitude"</note> reported of some other philosophers. On this occasion, feeling a perceived neglect from his friends, he wrote the following letter to Hypatia the Philosopher—to use the very words of the address <note>original: "inscription"</note>.

<margin>Epistle 10</margin>
> I greet you, happy Lady, and through you, those most happy companions. For a long time, I have intended to scold you, because I have received no letters from any of you. But now I see that I am neglected by everyone; not because I have failed in any of my duties, but because I am unfortunate in many ways—indeed, as unfortunate as anyone can be. Nevertheless, if I could be considered worthy of receiving your letters and being informed of how you lead your lives (feeling confident, however, that you live in the best possible way and do not fail to exercise your lively intellect <note>original: "sprightly genius"</note>), I would only think myself half as unhappy, since I would enjoy some happiness through you. But now I must count this also as one of the misfortunes in which I am entangled. For I am deprived not only of my children, but also of my friends, and of every

"There was also a time when I could be useful to my friends, and when you were accustomed to call me the 'blessing of others' <note>original: 'Others good'—referring to someone who acts as a benefactor to everyone</note>; for I used my influence with persons in great authority for the benefit of other men, making those officials serve me as if they were my own hands. ### XIII.

<image-desc>Large ornamental initial 'T' decorated with a pattern of leaves and scrolls.</image-desc> It would be as great a miracle <note>original: 'prodigy'</note> in nature as <term>Hypatia</term> was herself, if a lady of such beauty, modesty, wisdom, and virtue were not eagerly sought after in marriage. # HYPATIA.

...to be true. This **Isidorus** succeeded **Marinus** in the school, and his *Life* was written by **Damascius**, one of **Theon's** students, who therefore had every possible opportunity to know whatever concerned **Hypatia** <margin>In Codex 242.</margin> and **Isidorus**. His *Life* was abridged by **Photius**, but we do not have it in the perfect state he left it. Besides the extreme confusion and many errors appearing throughout the work, the <margin>In the notes to Socrates, Book 7, chapter 15.</margin> learned **Valesius** <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French classical scholar known for editing ecclesiastical histories.</note> led the world to expect that he would eventually publish a version twice as long as the one we currently read in **Photius**.

However, in its current form, **Damascius** offers such high praise <note>original: "Elogies"</note> for **Isidorus** that he places him almost above humanity. Yet, because these praises do not concern **Hypatia**, I will pass over them in silence. I frankly confess that I highly suspect many of the things he reports. I know that **Damascius** was a great visionary and—much like **Philostratus** did for **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A Neopythagorean philosopher and miracle-worker from the 1st century AD often compared to Jesus.</note>—intended to present **Isidorus** as a rival to those Christian *Saints* who were celebrated for their miraculous and supernatural achievements. 

But this should not affect his credibility regarding ordinary matters. Therefore, I do not hesitate at all to believe him when he positively affirms <margin>In Photius, as cited above <note>original: "Apud Phot. ubi supra."</note></margin> that **Hypatia** was the wife of **Isidorus**.

### XIV.

<margin>In the entry for "Hypatia" <note>original: "In ‘Yπα-τια"</note></margin>
<margin>In Photius, as cited above <note>original: "Apud Pʜᴏᴛ. ubi supra."</note></margin>
**Suidas** likewise claims she was the wife of the same **Isidorus**, though he is the very man who tells us she died a virgin. Given the great uncertainty in which we are left by the deliberate destruction or natural decay of authentic writers, I believe the matter stands like this: **Damascius** says that **Isidorus** had another wife named **Domna**, by whom he had a son called **Proclus**. She died five days after giving birth, and, according to his eulogist <note>original: "Panegyrist"</note>, *she freed the philosopher from an evil beast and a bitter marriage.* Now, supposing this happened some time before the tragic end of **Hypatia**, and that she was betrothed to **Isidorus**, it might very well be said that she was his wife, and yet that she died a virgin. The author of an *epigram* <note>a short, witty poem; original: "Epigram"</note> written about her seems to have held the same opinion.

> *Whenever I see the Virgin's starry constellation,*

> *For all your virtuous works are heavenly,*
> *As are your learned words beyond compare,*
> *Divine **Hypatia**, who appears far and near*
> *As the spotless star of virtue and learning.*

For this reason, I cannot let pass without criticism a reflection by **Damascius** <margin>In Photius, as cited above <note>original: "Apud PHOT. ubi supra."</note></margin>, who gravely says that **Isidorus** was far superior to **Hypatia**—not only as a man to a woman, but as a *philosopher* to a *geometer* <note>original: "Geometrician"</note>. 

What remarkable reasoning! As if her skill in geometry or astronomy had been any hindrance to her improvement in every part of philosophy, in which she is confessed by so many to surpass those of her own time, if not of former times. It is as if we in England, for example, should consider King **James** superior to Queen **Elizabeth** just because the first, forsooth <note>indeed; used here sarcastically</note>, was a man and the last a woman. But I observed before that **Damascius** was a wretched visionary.

### XV.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' featuring a classical design with birds on either side.</image-desc>
A lady of such uncommon merit and accomplishments as **Hypatia** was daily surrounded by a circle of young gentlemen, many of them distinguished by their fortune or social status <note>original: "quality"</note>. Even the strictest virtue cannot avoid such attempts, though it can deny encouragement and make the suitor despair of success. We are left to imagine, rather than to...

One instance, however, has escaped the common wreckage of good books: nor can I doubt but several others might have been contained in the *Life of Isidorus*, from which there is reason to believe that **Suidas** <note>The *Suda* (or Suidas) is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note> picked 
<margin>In *Hypatia* <note>original Greek: 'Υπατια</note></margin>
what I am going to relate. He informs us, therefore, that one of her own scholars fell deeply in love with her. She endeavored to cure him of his passion by the precepts of philosophy; some reported she actually reclaimed him by music, but Suidas judiciously rejects this <note>original: "explodes"</note>, as music has always been deemed more of an incentive to love than an antidote against it. 

But he says with much greater probability that the young suitor <note>original: "spark"</note>, vehemently pursuing her (surely pleading the irresistible power of her beauty), approached her at a time when she happened to be having her menstrual period <note>original: "under an indisposition ordinary to her sex"</note>. She took a cloth <note>original: "handkerchief"</note>, which she had been using for that purpose, and throwing it in his face, said: *“This is what you love, young fool, and not anything that is truly beautiful.”* For the **Platonic** philosophers held goodness, wisdom, virtue, and other such things—which are desirable for their own sake because of their intrinsic worth—to be the only real *beauties*. They believed the most superlative charms that appear in *bodies* are but faint resemblances of that divine symmetry, charm, and perfection. 

This is the correct notion of **Platonic love**. Therefore, **Hypatia's** method might very well put a student of philosophy at Alexandria to the blush, 
<margin>As cited above <note>original Latin: "Ubi supra"</note></margin>
and quite cure him too (which **Suidas** assures us was the effect); but it would never discourage a fashionable gentleman <note>original: "Beau"</note> in **St. James's Park** <note>A famous royal park in London; the author is comparing the high-mindedness of ancient Alexandria to the more worldly society of 18th-century England.</note>, nor perhaps some Bachelors of Divinity at our modern universities.

### XVI.

At the time that **HYPATIA** thus reigned as the brightest ornament of Alexandria, **ORESTES** was Governor of the city for the Emperor **THEODOSIUS**, and **CYRIL** was the Bishop or Patriarch. As **ORESTES** was a man whose education was suitable to his high rank, he could not help but notice those perfections in **HYPATIA** which the rest of the world also admired. Furthermore, as a wise Governor, he would not be so neglectful of his duties as to fail to ask her advice in difficult or dangerous matters, especially when everyone else consulted her as an oracle. This naturally created an intimacy between them that was highly displeasing to **CYRIL**, who hated **ORESTES** to the death. But because this rivalry <note>original: "emulation"</note> proved fatal to **HYPATIA**, I shall trace the subject back a little further.

<margin>Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 7, Chapter 7. Nicephorus, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 14, Chapter 14.</margin>
It is observed by **SOCRATES**, **NICEPHORUS**, and other historians that **CYRIL**—who was raised to the bishop's seat through sedition and force, over a certain **TIMOTHY**, an Archdeacon of no great reputation—interfered more in secular <note>original: "temporal"</note> or civil matters than his predecessors had ever done. His example was greedily followed by his successors,

<margin>Socrates, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 7, Chapter 13.</margin>
who, not keeping *within the bounds of their priestly ordination*, took it upon themselves to exercise *an arbitrary kind of sovereignty and the absolute management of affairs*. The first act of authority that **CYRIL** exercised was to shut up the churches of the Novatians <note>A strict early Christian sect that believed those who had renounced their faith under Roman persecution could not be readmitted to the church.</note>. From that step, he proceeded to seize their sacred vessels and Church-

# HYPATIA.
->XVII. ONE<-

...church ornaments, until at length he robbed their bishop, **THEOPEMPTUS**, of all he had. Yet these **Novatians** professed the exact same doctrine <note>original: "to a tittle"</note> as he did, and differed only in some points of discipline. But they must be mere novices in ecclesiastical history <note>original: "Ecclesiastical History"</note> who do not know that discipline has always been considered of greater consequence than doctrine, if one may judge by the commotions that have happened in churches or the duration of their schisms <note>meaning: formal splits or divisions within a religious body</note>. 

The reason is obvious. For if a man believes differently than his teacher and yet prudently conforms to the public ritual and discipline—or perhaps eagerly insists <note>original: "stickles"</note> on it, thinking it most conducive to order—whatever his private thoughts <note>original: "speculations"</note> may be, he still preserves the unity of the church. Or, in other words, he obeys his spiritual governors and teaches others by his example to do the same. 

On the other hand, even if his belief is perfectly correct—or at least aligns with what is prescribed by the society of which he is a member—yet if he hesitates <note>original: "boggles"</note> at any part of the public ritual and discipline, he then promotes a spiritual rebellion and tears apart the unity of the church; that is, he weakens the government of the clergy. These were the principles <note>original: "maxims"</note> of those times, and from this it followed that schism is considered such a damnable sin in their writings—a sin more dreadful than any other—so that it might better serve as a scarecrow <note>meaning: a "bugbear" or warning used to frighten people into obedience</note>.

<summary>This page describes how Bishop Cyril seized the property of the Novatian bishop and argues that the Church often prioritizes strict adherence to discipline and clerical authority over actual doctrinal agreement.</summary>

->### XVII.<-

<margin>As cited above <note>original: "Ubi supra"</note></margin>
<margin>In the same place <note>original: "Ibid."</note></margin>
<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial O with ornate foliage and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

**ONE** main reason why **Cyril** <note>Cyril of Alexandria, the powerful and often controversial Patriarch/Bishop of the city (reigned 412–444 AD)</note> could not stand the Governor, as we are told by the historian **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian church historian</note>, was that the Governor **Orestes** *hated the political power <note>original: "Principality"</note> of the bishops. This was because they took for themselves much of the authority belonging to those appointed as governors by the Emperor, and specifically because* **Cyril** *insisted on prying into his administrative actions.* Their mutual hatred became well known to the public through a riot <note>original: "sedition"</note> raised against **Orestes**. This was triggered by a man named **Hierax**, a schoolmaster who was a self-confessed admirer of the Bishop and a very frequent <note>original: "diligent"</note> attendee at his sermons, where he was sure to applaud loudly and repeatedly <note>original: "clap and reclap"</note>, following the unusual custom of those times. 

The Jews, spotting him in the theater while the Governor was there on public business, cried out that he had come there specifically to cause trouble. The resulting uproar—the details of which may be read in the work of **Socrates** just mentioned—ended with **Cyril** expelling all the Jews from the city. They had lived there in great wealth <note>original: "opulence"</note> since the time of **Alexander the Great**, providing significant benefit to the city. If I were not so accustomed to reading monstrous lies about this unfortunate nation—especially that tired <note>original: "thread-bare"</note> fiction about them crucifying a child (an accusation made against them here, as it has been thousands of times afterward)—I would think they were treated justly <note>original: "rightly served"</note>. But even if the charges were true, who can justify **Cyril** giving the mob permission to seize their property? And yet, why do I even ask such a question?

## HYPATIA.

...when this has ever been the true motive of the cruelties to which they have been exposed, though zeal for religion has been as shamelessly as it was wickedly used as a pretext. **Orestes**, as was fitting for a <margin>In the same place <note>original: "Ibid."</note></margin> good Governor, *being grievously concerned at what had happened (to speak in the words of the historian) and sadly afflicted that so great a city should be so suddenly emptied of such a great number of inhabitants, gave the Emperor an account of the whole matter.* We might be certain, even if we were not expressly told, that **Cyril** was not <margin>In the same place <note>original: "Ibid."</note></margin> lagging behind in telling his side of the story. He was compelled to do this, as **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Greek historian who wrote a history of the church</note> observes, by the people of <margin>Church History, book 14, chapter 15 <note>original: "Hist. lib.14. cap. 15."</note></margin> Alexandria, who loved their Governor. Meanwhile, the Emperor might thank himself for the disorders that ruined one of his principal cities; for when was it ever otherwise, when the clergy were permitted to share in the government of civil affairs?

### XVIII.

Now the revenge which **Cyril** took against **Orestes** served as the prelude to poor **Hypatia’s** tragedy. I choose to relate it, as I have done with other passages, in the words of the honest **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian church historian known for his relative impartiality</note>.

<margin>Church History, book 7, chapter 14 <note>original: "Hist. lib. 7. cap. 14."</note></margin>
> "Certain monks (he says) living in the Nitrian mountains <note>A region in the Egyptian desert that was a major center of early Christian monasticism</note>, leaving their monasteries to the number of about five hundred, flocked to the city. There they spotted the Governor traveling in his chariot. Approaching him, they called him a 'sacrificer' <note>original: "sacrificer" — an accusation that he still performed pagan sacrifices</note> and a 'heathen,' using many other scandalous words. The Governor, suspecting that this was a trick played on him by **Cyril**, cried out that he was a Christian and that he had been baptized at Constantinople by Bishop **Atticus**. But the monks paid no attention to what he said. One of them, named **Ammonius**, threw a stone at **Orestes**, which struck him on the head. As he was covered with blood from the wound, his guards—with a few exceptions—fled in every direction, hiding themselves in the crowd for fear of being stoned to death. In the meantime, the people of Alexandria ran to defend their Governor against the monks. Putting the rest to flight, they seized **Ammonius** and brought him before **Orestes**. As the laws prescribed, the Governor publicly put him to torture, racking him until he died. Not long after, he sent an account of everything that had been done to the Emperors."

<margin>Theodosius II and Honorius <note>The Eastern and Western Roman Emperors at the time</note></margin>

But the wiser sort of Christians themselves did not approve of the zeal which **Cyril** showed on this man’s behalf. I shall only observe, alongside a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> philosopher, that at that time the **monks** (the fittest executioners of **Cyril’s** cruelty) were indeed human in their form, but swine in their way of life. They openly committed thousands of execrable crimes, not fit to be named. "Whoever," he says, "put on a black robe <note>original: "habit"</note> and wished to present a bizarre <note>original: "grotesque"</note> appearance in public, obtained a tyrannical authority; such was the reputation for virtue that race of men achieved!" This picture, though drawn by an enemy’s hand, is allowed by all good judges to be a portrait true to life; and we shall soon have more than enough reason to agree with that opinion.

### XIX.

<margin>History, Book 7, Chapter 13 <note>original: "Hist. lib. 7. cap. 13."</note></margin>

<meta>This page continues the account of the escalating conflict in Alexandria, shifting from the death of the monk Ammonius to the brutal conspiracy against the philosopher Hypatia by the followers of Bishop Cyril.</meta>

<center># HYPATIA.</center>

...the mob of the Christian Church, as if she were an obstacle to a reconciliation between Bishop **Cyril** and **Orestes**. Therefore, certain hot-headed men, led by a man named **Peter**, a Lecturer <note>original: "Lecturer," referring to a "Lector," a member of the lower clergy responsible for reading scriptures</note>, entered into a conspiracy against her. Watching for their opportunity while she was returning home from some place, they dragged her out of her chair <note>original: "chair," likely a sedan chair or carriage</note>, hurried her to the church called **Caesar's**, and stripping her completely naked, they killed her with tiles <note>original: "tiles," likely referring to *ostraka*, or sharp pottery shards used for roofing</note>. Then they tore her to pieces and, carrying her limbs to a place called **Cinaron**, burned them to ashes. 

Nothing less than this treatment—unparalleled even among the most savage nations against a woman (and against a woman of such distinction, it would be barely credible if two or more of her contemporaries did not testify to it)—nothing, I say, but the blood of **Hypatia**, shed in the most inhuman manner, could satisfy <note>original: "glut"</note> the fury of **Cyril’s** clergy. For these were the monsters who, casting off all humanity, committed this barbaric murder. It is true that **Socrates** <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian historian and contemporary of these events</note> specifically names only one clergyman, **Peter the Lecturer**; but <margin>Nicephorus, *History*, Book 14, Chapter 16 <note>original: "Hist lib. 14. cap. 16."</note></margin> **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a 14th-century historian who compiled earlier accounts</note> explicitly tells us that the zealots led by this **Peter** were **Cyril’s** clergy, who hated her for the influence <note>original: "credit"</note> she had with **Orestes**. He states that they were the ones who blamed her for the misunderstanding between the Governor and their Bishop; and finally, that they butchered her during a time of solemn fasting. This, added to the way they "sanctified" their villainy by committing it in a church, shows the "glorious" state of religion in those "pure and primitive times"—as some people, with as much hypocrisy as falsehood, are pleased to call them. The citizens of Alexandria—whom some writers would like to blame for this act of "popular heat," as they call it to downplay the crime <note>original: "by way of extenuation"</note>—were too great admirers of **Hypatia’s** virtue and too much in favor of **Orestes** to have any part in such a foul business, however...

### XX.

<margin>
In the *Life of Isidore* <note>original: "In vita ISIDORI"</note>.
In the entry for *Hypatia* <note>original: "In Ὑπατία"</note>.
In the entry for *Hypatia* <note>original: "In Ὑπατία"</note>.
</margin>
Suppose it is true that the clergy of Alexandria were the murderers (as some may say), and that their affection for **Cyril** drove them beyond what can be justified. How, then, does it appear that he himself had any hand in this dark <note>original: "black"</note> deed, which perhaps he neither knew of nor could prevent? For the sake of our common humanity (since true Christianity is not involved at all), I wish it were so. However, there is evidence that will not allow any man—unless he willfully shuts his eyes against the truth—to believe it. 

**Damascius** (who is the other contemporary witness of her murder I mentioned besides **Socrates**) positively affirms that **Cyril** vowed **Hypatia's** *destruction*, as he bitterly envied her. **Suidas**, who writes the same thing, says that this envy was caused by her *extraordinary wisdom* and skill in *astronomy*. Similarly, **Hesychius**, when he mentions her limbs being carried all over the city in triumph, writes that *this* befell her on account of her *extraordinary wisdom*, and especially her skill in *astronomy*. For **Cyril** was a great pretender to *learning* <note>original: "Letters"</note>, and he was one of those clergymen who will neither acknowledge nor endure the superiority of any layperson in this respect.

<center># HYPATIA.</center>

...be it ever so incontestable to others. However, certain circumstances of **Hypatia’s** death, not mentioned in **Socrates Scholasticus**, are preserved in the abridgment of **Isidore's** *Life* found in the works of **Photius**, as **Valesius** <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French classical scholar and historian</note> had it. I provide them here, reader, though it should cost you the tribute of one more tear to her memory. 

<margin>In Annotations on **Socrates**, Book 7, Chapter 15.</margin>

"One time," says **Damascius**, "**Cyril**, while passing by the house of **Hypatia**, saw a great multitude before the door, including men on foot and on horseback; some were arriving, some were leaving, and others remained. When he inquired what that crowd was, and what occasioned so great a gathering, he was answered by those accompanying him that this was **Hypatia** the Philosopher’s house, and that these people came to pay their respects to her. When **Cyril** understood this, he was moved with such great envy that he immediately vowed her destruction, which he accomplished in the most detestable manner. 

For when **Hypatia**, as was her custom, went out in public, several men—fearing neither divine vengeance nor human punishment—suddenly rushed upon her and killed her. Thus, they brought upon their country both the highest infamy and the guilt of innocent blood. 

<margin>See **Suidas** in the entry for *Hypatia* <note>original: "Vide SUID. In Ὑπατία"</note>.</margin>

And indeed, the Emperor was grievously offended by this matter, and the murderers would certainly have been punished, but **Edesius** corrupted <note>bribed</note> the Emperor’s friends. So, while it is true his Majesty remitted <note>canceled or pardoned</note> the punishment, he drew vengeance upon himself and his descendants, as his nephew paid dearly for this action." 

**Valesius** believes this nephew to have been **Valentinian III**, whose mother **Placidia** was the aunt of **Theodosius II**.

## XXI.

<image-desc>Decorative initial T at the start of the paragraph, featuring ornate scrollwork inside a square frame.</image-desc>
Thus ended the life of **HYPATIA**, whose memory will last forever. Her murder happened in the fourth year of **CYRIL**'s episcopate <note>The period of time a bishop holds office.</note>, during the tenth consulship of **HONORIUS** and the sixth consulship of **THEODOSIUS**, in the month of March, during Lent, in the year 415. 

<margin>**SOCRATES**, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 7, Chapter 15.</margin>

"*That action*," says **SOCRATES**, "*brought no small infamy not only upon* **CYRIL**, *but also upon the whole Church of Alexandria; for slaughters, and fightings, and such things are completely foreign to the Christian institution.*" There is nothing surer, there is nothing truer; but very little of genuine Christianity remained at that time, unless Christianity is defined by nothing more than a bare name and profession. For, were I inclined to take this trouble upon me, I should think it no difficult task to show that neither the doctrines nor the distinctions then in fashion <note>original: "vogue"</note> were ever taught by **CHRIST** or his **APOSTLES**, and that the ceremonies enjoined or practiced were all utterly unknown to them. 

No, no, they were not Christians who killed **HYPATIA**. Nor are any Christian clergymen now to be attacked through the actions of her murderers, except for those who resemble them by substituting questionable traditions, scholastic fictions, and an usurped dominion for the wholesome <note>original: "salutiferous"</note> institution of the holy **JESUS**. **PHOTIUS** is very angry with

<center># HYPATIA.</center>

But is he not an *impudent man*, or something worse, who dares to deny this, when none were more remarkable supporters of the *Homoousion* <note>original: "Homoouſion"; a theological term meaning "of the same substance," used to describe the relationship between God the Father and God the Son</note> than **CYRIL** and his adherents? However, this much may be gathered from this passage: that this same* **HYPATIA** *was no Catholic.*" 

Admirable **GOTHOFRED**! To say nothing of your "Arian poison" (for which I am not at all concerned), nor of the people's guilt (whom I have sufficiently cleared before), nor even of the fine distinction between the "populace" and the "Catholics" (as if the majority of Catholics were not the populace): your conclusion that **HYPATIA** was not a Catholic is incredibly sharp—considering that, in reality, she was not even a Christian. Her father was a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> philosopher, and she herself was the wife of one, with no evidence that she ever held any other belief. 

As for a ridiculous letter, claimed to have been written by her to **CYRIL** about the Easter cycle <note>original: "Paſchal Cycle"; the calculation used to determine the date of Easter</note>, it is a clear forgery. She was murdered in the sixth year of **THEODOSIUS**, and therefore twenty-one years before the exile of **NESTORIUS**, who is nonetheless mentioned in that letter with the title of "*Impious*."

## XXII.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'A' decorated with vines and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

AND now that **CYRIL’S** name reminds me of him once again, what an insufferable mockery of **GOD** and **HUMANITY** it is to revere a man so ambitious, so turbulent, so treacherous, and so cruel as a *Saint*? History shows that this was his true character. But in all seriousness, this same title of *Saint* has often been most wretchedly bestowed. The majority of the Saints recognized after **CONSTANTINE’S** reign—and especially since *Canonization* <note>The official process by which the Christian Church declares a deceased person to be a saint</note> came into fashion—are made up of three types of people who, least of all others, deserve such veneration.

First, men have been dubbed Saints for promoting the grandeur of the Church by all their endeavors, especially through their writings. Instead of employing their talents for the happiness or instruction of their fellow citizens, they prostituted their work to magnify spiritual authority, thereby degrading and enslaving people's minds. 

The second sort that have been honored with *Sainthood* were *Princes* and other powerful or wealthy men—however vicious or tyrannical—who gave large possessions and legacies to the Church; or those who, using legal incapacity, the stake <note>original: "faggot"; a bundle of sticks used for burning heretics</note>, the gallows <note>original: "gibbet"</note>, the sword, and exile <note>original: "proscription"</note>, punished the audacity of those who dared to question the Church’s decrees. 

The third sort were poor, low-minded visionaries who boasted of their delirious enthusiasms and ecstasies, or who imposed on the ignorant with formal acts of self-denial <note>original: "mortifications"</note> that were falsely considered devotion. They were rewarded with this imaginary prize of sainthood by those who actually despised their au-

-> ### MANGONEUTES. <-
<note>original: "Mangoneutes"; This title is derived from a Greek word meaning a juggler, charlatan, or trickster. In the polemical writing of the 17th and 18th centuries, it was often used to label an opponent as deceptive or fraudulent.</note>

I.

No one can justly wonder, my Lord, at the address I now take the liberty of making to you, when they consider that of all the arguments used in favor of Diocesan Episcopacy <note>original: "Dioceſan Epiſcopacy"; a system of church government where a Bishop oversees a specific geographic area known as a diocese.</note>, these two are not the least important: first, the necessity of an authority to regularly admit Presbyters <note>original: "Preſbyters"; ordained ministers or elders who serve under the authority of a Bishop.</note> into the ministerial function, who is consequently to be a judge of their capacities and abilities; second, the necessity of an inspector into the behavior and conduct of those Presbyters, who are therefore to be subject to his government. 

For these reasons, no Bishop can take it ill if the misconduct or incapacity of any of his clergy be dutifully laid before him, especially by one who has been grievously injured by them in both these respects. And, whatever they may imagine (some of whom are all too inclined not to think the best of their superiors), I would look upon myself as a disparager of Episcopacy if I did not take it for granted that every Bishop could not only effectively regulate the behavior of his clergy—which requires no greater talent than what lay magistrates <note>original: "Lay-magiſtrates"; civil officials or judges who are not members of the clergy.</note> can...

<summary>The author begins a formal address to a Bishop, arguing that the very nature of the Bishop's office—to judge the ability and conduct of the clergy—makes it necessary for him to hear complaints about clerical misconduct.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's address to a Bishop, arguing that high-ranking church officials are expected to be experts in theology and language. The author then introduces his book, "Nazarenus," and complains about the unfair criticism it received from the clergy.</meta>

# MANGONEUTES.
<note>original: "Mangoneutes"; A Greek term meaning a "juggler," "trickster," or "charlatan." In this context, it is used as a polemical title to accuse opponents of being deceptive.</note>

...can exercise; I also take it for granted that every one of them understands all the learned languages to perfection—particularly those in which the Scriptures were originally written—and that they are completely informed about the various systems of theology <note>original: "Divinity"</note> and all church history from the time of the Apostles to the present day. 

Anyone who thinks otherwise must not believe that a single person is more fit to rule and examine than a select group, no matter how prudent, pious, or capable they might be. Furthermore, such a person should be strongly suspected of criticizing <note>original: "reflecting on"</note> your Lordship, whose frequent and numerous ordinations we learn about from the public newspapers. For my part, I do not doubt in the least that you take as much care to ensure that young preachers are properly qualified by secular achievements and spiritual gifts as you do by their canonical titles and the special calling of the Holy Spirit. 

But lest I be thought to be seeking your favor—when I desire nothing but a fair trial and an even playing field—I humbly inform your Lordship that some time ago I wrote a book entitled *NAZARENUS* <note>original: "NAZARENUS"; John Toland's 1718 work which argued that the early "Nazarene" Christians followed Jewish law and that this was the original form of Christianity.</note>. The responsibilities of your diocese and the examination of so many candidates for the ministry <note>original: "candidates for the gown"</note> may not have left you time to read it; even so, many people who have read it no more than you have were the first to blame and condemn it. 

Nevertheless, however fickle <note>original: "giddy"</note> and prejudiced some of the laity <note>original: "Laity"; members of the church who are not ordained clergy</note> may be, none of them were guilty of this gross injustice until a crude outcry was raised by one or two of your Lordship’s clergy—men who are as little masters of the subject as they are of their own tempers. Only then did some of the most ignorant and blindly following <note>original: "sequacious"; describes people who lack independence of thought and follow a leader or an idea slavishly</note> laypeople join in the clamor. I shall completely forget and heartily forgive them, as they did not know what they were doing. But to my great comfort on the other hand, several people—distinguished by their character as much as their number—found no...

# MANGONEUTES.

There was no harm in my book; indeed, many found much to approve of in it, and have readily adopted the plan of Religion laid down there, or *the essential distinction between Gentile and Jewish Christians* <note>Toland argued that the original "Nazarene" Christians continued to follow Jewish Law, while Gentile converts were not required to, creating two distinct but valid branches of early Christianity.</note>. I have letters of thanks from several learned men on this subject, which I prefer to the applause of all superficial pretenders, as they provide sufficient compensation for their outcry and senseless slanders <note>original: "calumnies"</note>.

The occasion of my book was the discovery I made of an apocryphal *Gospel* fathered upon the Apostle BARNABAS, and interpolated if not entirely forged by Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note>. I could not imagine that communicating this discovery to the world would be any crime, after the voluminous discussion of such apocryphal pieces by COOK, RIVET, DAILLÉ, SIMON, ITTIGIUS, GRABE, MILL, FABRICIUS, and others. Among these, the excellent PFAFFIUS, a Professor at Tübingen, is pleased to include myself, on account of the *notable Catalog* he says *I gave in AMYNTOR* <note>original: *Amyntor*; Toland's 1699 work which included a controversial list of apocryphal books, questioning why they were excluded from the Biblical canon.</note>. 

And that same no less celebrated than able and industrious FABRICIUS, whom I have just named, has very lately—in the third volume of his ²*Apocryphal Code of the New Testament*—inserted the entire historical part of *NAZARENUS*, without altering or omitting one word. But some of your—

...my religion on the *Scripture*, which I make the sole rule of faith, by which the Church Fathers, Councils, and all Christian writings and determinations ought to be tested <note>original: "try'd"</note>. But I hope to convince your Lordship before I have finished that I embrace, as I profess, the religion truly taught by JESUS and the Apostles—stripped of all mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note> or superstitious disguises, and free from all human additions, mixtures, and inventions. 

That I did not write my book in Latin is the specific objection of certain mongrel theologians who study Machiavelli <note>original: "MACCHIAVEL"</note> more than the Bible, and who have an admirable project of telling only half-truths. This makes them considered as only "half-politicians" by those who are in favor of telling no truth at all. I sincerely believe Dr. MANGEY when <margin>page 1.</margin> he says in his Preface that *he undertook his answer not only without the encouragement and urging of friends, but without the knowledge of some, and against the judgment of others.* Whatever the reasons of those gentlemen were (for discreet friends will not always give their true reasons, but rather those most likely to persuade), it is certain they knew the Doctor better than he did himself. His apology for not listening to them will never hold <margin>Ibid. Ibid. page 2.</margin> water. ## MANGONEUTES.

Is this a suitable idea of Christianity: that a man who writes weakly and contemptibly, and in no way deserving of notice, should be able to do it irretrievable mischief? I am a stranger to the discipline used regarding chaplains, but I beg your Lordship would be mild in correcting him, though the blunder is so egregious—to give it no harsher name—as to suggest that the best religion, supported by the greatest learning, should run imminent danger from a weak and illiterate creature. I will not say that the Doctor is one of those who would rather pass for knaves than fools; yet since he, who is so sharp-sighted in discerning the weakness of others, cannot be supposed weak himself, what at first sight looks like a blunder must rather be counted an artifice. It is very plainly the fifth of the twelve deceptive arts laid to the charge of those who use them in the Preface to *NAZARENUS*. It is that, <margin>Pages 18, 19, 20.</margin> almost in the same breath, they make the same man equally stupid and cunning; telling you on this page that his whole performance is so insuperably dull and incoherent as to scarcely deserve criticism <note>original: "animadversion"</note>, yet on the next page they contradict themselves. They do this not only by the "oil and sweat" <note>This refers to the "midnight oil"—the hard work and long hours spent on scholarly writing.</note> they expend to refute him, but by claiming his plot is laid so deep and reporting his skill as so formidable that it calls for abler hands—nay, sometimes even for the magistrate <note>The civil legal authorities.</note> to take him to task. Your Lordship sees I was a prophet.

I spoke to the same purpose before, and therefore concluded that the Gospel of *BARNABAS* was not yet extinct, <margin>Page 9.</margin> as all Christian writers had previously imagined. 

But what Gospel of *BARNABAS*? Not one written by himself, since we have no evidence that he left any such record. Rather, I meant the *Gospel* that was anciently attributed to him, fathered upon him, and forged under his name—which are the expressions I so often use. I supposed, moreover, that *BARNABAS*'s disagreement with *PAUL* and his leaving his company probably gave an <margin>Page 34</margin> opportunity to *IMPOSTORS* to frame a Gospel in his name.

IV. I HAVE so far focused on things falsely blamed on me, and now I come to a point justly attributed to me, but which is nonetheless amazingly denied by my adversary—even though the unanimous consensus of all qualified witnesses, ancient and modern, is against him. It is that I call *Mahometanism* <note>original: "Mahometanism," a term formerly used by Europeans for Islam</note> the *Religion of the four books*. Although the *Mahometans* <note>Muslims</note> do hold by *tradition* that **ADAM**, **NOAH**, **ENOCH**, **ABRAHAM**, and other *patriarchs* and *prophets* had several books divinely sent to them (even to the number of 104) containing divine revelations; yet the only obligatory ones are, according to them, the *Pentateuch* of **MOSES**, the *Psalms* of **DAVID**, the *Gospel* of **JESUS**, and the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> of **MUHAMMAD** <note>original: "MAHOMET"</note>. This I confess to have said, and I will stand by it.
<margin>Page 25.</margin>
But let us hear the Doctor. To prove that the *Mahometans* receive the Gospel, he observes that although they believe 104 [he should have added "books," since all books are not *Gospels*] were sent from heaven, yet only four of them are obligatory. He quotes the names of several writers to prove this particularly in respect of the *Gospel*, but he skillfully omits their words. The cause, my Lord, requires no trickery, and I used none; nor have I therefore omitted the words of my authors, except where they were irrelevant to the point. **RELAND'S** *Compendium* <note>Adriaan Reland (1676–1718), a Dutch scholar of Middle Eastern languages and Islam</note> (for example) and **GABRIEL SIONITA**, whom he quotes after me, list along with others

<page-num>152</page-num>

# MANGONEUTES <note>original: "Mangoneutes," a Greek term for a "faker" or "dealer in trash," used here by Toland to mock his opponent</note>

Others mention the ten books sent to **ADAM**, fifty to **SETH**, thirty to **ENOCH**, and ten to **ABRAHAM**. I passed these by because they are no longer obligatory, as I noted before. Instead, I insisted only on the four books with which both of them conclude and whose titles they provide, as these are the books on which the Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note> explicitly claim to base their religion. To those two writers, I added the words of **ALGAZEL** <note>Al-Ghazali (c. 1058–1111), a major Persian theologian and philosopher</note>, and a certain *Formulary* <note>a collection of set forms for religious ritual or doctrine</note> from **MARACCI’s** *Prodromus* <note>Ludovico Maracci (1612–1700), an Italian priest and scholar who produced an influential Latin translation of the Quran</note>. 

I omitted the words of **LEVINUS WARNER** <note>a Dutch orientalist and diplomat (1619–1665)</note> for the same reason—to avoid unnecessarily crowding my book. But since the Doctor seems as fond of words as others are of things, I will now accommodate him with the words of several more authors whose authority is beyond question. 

The first shall be the *Treatise concerning the manners, conditions, and wickedness of the Turks*, printed at Paris in the year 1511 by **HENRY STEPHENS** <note>Henri Estienne, a famous French printer</note>, along with **RICOLDUS** <note>Riccoldo da Monte di Croce (c. 1243–1320), a Dominican missionary</note>, whom the Doctor himself quotes (following others) under the Gothic name of **RICHARD**, and about whom I shall take the opportunity to say something before I am finished. 

The author of the *Treatise concerning the manners and conditions of the Turks* was a captive among them for twenty years. He gained such knowledge of their doctrine and their language (even to the point of forgetting his own) that he was judged fit for the priesthood and deserving of no small income, should he choose to stay among them, as he once intended. His words are as follows:

*The general opinion of almost all the Turks* <note>original Latin: "Communis opinio omnium ferè Turcorum de Lege eorum talis est. Dicunt enim primum Prophetam magnum, cui data fuit Lex primitùs a Deo, fuisse MISSAH; id est, MOSES, cui datus est liber Tefrit, quem nos Pentateuchum nominamus: et omnem hominem, qui eo tempore legem observasset, salvatum esse."</note> *concerning their Law is this: They say that the first great Prophet, to whom the Law was originally given by God, was **MISSAH** <note>likely a phonetic spelling of 'Musa' or Moses</note>; that is, **MOSES**, to whom the book **Tefrit** <note>likely 'Tawrat,' the Arabic name for the Torah</note> was given, which we call the **Pentateuch**. They believe that every person who observed the law at that time was saved.*

<meta>catchword: Cùm [Since/When]</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>

# MANGONEUTES <note>original: "Mangoneutes," a Greek term for a "faker" or "dealer in trash," used here by Toland to mock his opponent, Dr. Mangey</note>

The view of the *Turks <note>The author uses "Turks" as a general term for Muslims, common in the 18th century.</note> concerning their Law is this. They say that the first great Prophet, to whom* God *originally gave a Law, was* **Moses** <note>original: "Missah"</note>, *to whom was given the book* **Torah** <note>original: "Tefrit," referring to the Tawrat</note>, *which we call the* **Pentateuch**; *and that every person who then observed this Law was saved. But after the malice and negligence of men had, over time, corrupted this Law, the second great Prophet* **David** <note>original: "Dajit"</note> *was chosen as a remedy for such transgression; to him was given the book* **Zabur** <note>original: "Czabúr"</note>, *which we call the* **Psalter**. *After this, in the same manner as the former, was observed and then corrupted, there was also added the third great Prophet* **Jesus** <note>original: "Yesse"</note>; *to whom was sent the third Law with the book* **Gospel** <note>original: "Vigilis," likely a corruption of "Injil"</note>, *and which in its time was the means of salvation for everyone. This being at length made void <note>original: "antiquated"</note> like the preceding books*, **Muhammad** *was chosen as the fourth Prophet, who received a Law from* God *with the book called the* **Quran** <note>original: "Alcoran"</note>; *which alone—all the former books having been superseded <note>original: "evacuated"</note>—is by them fully and firmly preached and asserted to contain the necessary means of salvation until another follows. They believe this shall not happen in this world: and as Christians believe the* **New Testament** *is come...*

But when, through the passage of time, human malice and negligence had corrupted this law, as a remedy for this transgression, a second great prophet was chosen, David <note>original: "Dajit"</note>, to whom was given the book Zabur <note>original: "Czabúr"</note>, which we call the Psalter. When this, in the manner of the first, had been observed and then corrupted, a third great prophet was also added, Yesse, that is Jesus; to whom the third law was sent with the book Vigilis <note>original: "Vigilis," meaning the Injil or Gospel</note>, which we call the Gospel, which in its own time was the cause of salvation for all. This finally being voided <note>original: "evacuata"</note> in the manner of those preceding it, a fourth was chosen, Muhammad, who received from God the law with the book called the Quran; which, all previous books having been voided, is fully and without doubt preached and said by them to contain the necessity of salvation (until another shall come). <note>original Latin: "Cùm autem, per successum temporis, humana malicia et negligentia hanc legem corrupisset, in hujus praevaricationis remedium electus est secundus Propheta magnus Dajit, quem nos David dicimus... Ea tandem, praecedentium modo evacuata, quartus electus est Mahumetes... quae, evacuatis omnibus antehabitis, sola necessitatem salutis... habere."</note>

<page-num>154</page-num>

# MANGONEUTES <note>original: "Mangoneutes," a Greek term for a "faker" or "dealer in trash," used here by Toland to mock his opponent, Dr. Mangey</note>

...into the place <note>original: "room"</note> of the *Old*, which is still the foundation upon which the other is built; likewise, the Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note> believe in the succession of the four books, the authority of the first three being frequently alleged in all their writings. Nothing is more certain, despite what some say.

<margin>Page 24.</margin>
> *Dr. Mangey ventures to affirm that, although the Muslims acknowledge a Gospel was formerly sent from heaven to Jesus, they grant that Gospel no force or authority; they produce no evidence from it, nor do any of them read it as a sacred book.*

They neither read nor quote our current *Gospels*, which I noted in several places in my book *Nazarenus* that they label as corrupted and falsified. However, their writings do contain numerous passages from a *Gospel* they claim belongs to Jesus. They consider this Gospel divine, whether it still exists <note>original: "extant"</note> or not—a fact I promise to demonstrate to your Lordship whenever you honor me with your commands. Even his own source, Friar Richard <note>Ricoldo da Monte Croce (c. 1243–1320), a Dominican missionary and author of works against Islam</note>—who is so far from being sensible and sincere that he is often ignorant and prone to telling tall tales <note>original: "fabulous"</note>—admits in his second chapter that not only Christians but also the "Saracens" <note>An archaic term for Muslims</note> themselves hold the *Law of Moses*, the *Prophets*, and the *Gospel* to be from God.

> *We have the truth of the Gospel,* he says, *to which the Quran <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> itself bears witness and approves.*

His entire third chapter aims to prove that the *Quran* is not the Law of God because it is not supported by the *Old* and *New Testament*—books which the Muslims themselves must necessarily believe in according to their own principles. However, as Ricoldo <note>original: "Ricoldus"</note> rightly remarks, this refers to a different Gospel than our current one, which they claim has been corrupted in many ways—specifically because they believe the name of Muhammad <note>original: "Mahomet"</note> was erased <note>original: "raz'd"</note> from it. I leave it to the Doctor to verify these quotations in the original text.

I asserted this alongside the best authorities, yet Dr. Mangey has the audacity <note>original: "temerity"</note> to deny it. I could bring many witnesses <note>original: "vouchers"</note> to show that they have more books than the majority of Christians imagine, or at least that they claim to have such. He was chaplain to the Ambassador Sir Thomas Roe and Rector of Greenford in Middlesex.

> *The Muslims say that they have the books of Moses; but they have greatly corrupted that story by ascribing to Ishmael <note>original: "ISMAEL"</note> what is said of Isaac—as if Ishmael should have been sacrificed and not Isaac. They say that they have the book of David’s Psalms and some writings of Solomon, with other portions of the Old Testament; which, if true, I believe are made to vary much from the original story.*

I believe so too, and I affirmed as much in my book *Nazarenus*; yet I am fully persuaded that they have a Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> and Psalms, such as they are, and that they often sing portions of the latter—though that is not the subject currently before me. <page-num>158</page-num>

# MANGONEUTES

...disposition of mind has brought the late, no less learned than modest, **Adrian Reland** <note>Adrian Reland (1676–1718) was a pioneering Dutch scholar of Oriental languages whose objective study of Islam was highly influential.</note> under **Dr. Mangey’s** displeasure. Indeed, he is not alone in facing this anger; nor do I wonder at it, coming from a tribe of men who spoke out against *Moderation* during **Queen Anne’s** reign, and against *Sincerity* under **King George**. In this, however, they are very consistent with their own principles and practices. **Reland**, in the Preface to his *Two Books Concerning the Muslim <note>original: "Mahometan"</note> Religion*, expressly detests the false accounts that most men give of other men's religions. He illustrates this through the slanders <note>original: "calumnies"</note> of the Heathens against the **Jews**, of the Heathens and **Jews** against the first **Christians**, of the **Catholics** <note>original: "Papists"</note> against the **Reformed Protestants**, and of almost all sects against their adversaries. "And I fear," says he, "that my present labor in examining the Muslim <note>original: "Mahometan"</note> religion, and setting it forth as it really is in itself, may give our adversaries an occasion to spread the same slander with greater confidence. Yet I shall not allow myself to be frightened from continuing my undertaking by such a vain fear. We ought to search for the Truth wherever it lies, and it is a laudable endeavor, in my opinion, to put a stop to lies, and to acquaint everyone who desires it with a religion that is spread far and wide: not to show it disguised, nor obscured by the clouds of false—"

---

"And I fear that this very work of mine in investigating the Muslim <note>original: "Mahomedica"</note> religion, and presenting it as it truly is, will give our adversaries an opportunity to spread the same slander <note>original: "calumniam"</note> with greater confidence. Yet I shall not allow myself to be frightened away from my beginning by such a vain fear. Truth, wherever it is, ought to be searched for; and it seems to me a praiseworthy effort to put a stop to lies, and to show the religion spread far and wide to anyone who wishes to look upon it: not painted over, not covered with the clouds of spite or error, but exactly as it is taught in Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedicis"</note> mosques <note>original: "Templis"</note> and schools; so that we may strike it with accurate blows, and—if not persuade the minds of the Turks themselves—we may at least persuade our own people of its vanity." *Second Edition, page 8, number 4.* <note>original Latin: "Et vereor, ne ipse hic labor meus in indaganda Religione Mahomedica, & ea exhibenda uti revera est, occasionem daturus sit adversariis nostris, eandem calumniam majori fiducia propagandi. ...hood and mistakes, but as it is taught in the Muslim <note>original: "Mahometan"</note> temples and schools; so that we may strike at it with certainty. Even if we cannot persuade the *Turks* to abandon it, we can at least convince ourselves of its emptiness <note>original: "vanity"</note>. 

Then, a little further down, he says $^7$: "It remains for me to add a word or two about myself. I ask the reader not to misinterpret the effort I have put into writing these two books. It was not my intention to gloss over or romanticize—much less to promote or defend—the *Muslim <note>original: "Mahometan"</note> religion*, which I find detestable <note>original: "execrate"</note>. Anyone who judged me in that way would do a great injustice to both me and the truth. 

If my goal was to write the truth, I was obligated to defend <note>Reland uses "apologize for" in the sense of an *apologia*, a formal defense or explanation, rather than an expression of regret.</note> the *Muslim religion* against many claims that are falsely attributed to them. I see more clearly every day that the world loves to be deceived and governed by preconceived opinions."

Qui hoc de me judicium ferret, nae ille gravem mihi injuriam faceret, & a vero aberraret. Debui patrocinium causae Mohammedicae suscipere, si vera scribere vellem, in multis quae falso iis tribuuntur. Si quis illa mendacia, quae nulla autoritate suffulta de Turcis narrantur, malit propagare, ut bardi illi, & asini, & insani, & diaboli, & si quid pejus, audiant, per me licet; qui quotidie magis magisque experior mundum decipi velle, & praeconceptis opinionibus regi."</note> 
"It remains that I add something about myself, and ask the reader not to interpret in a bad way my labor, whatever it may be, spent on these two books. It was not my purpose to gloss over or color the Muslim religion (which I detest), much less to champion or defend it. He who would pass this judgment on me would truly do me a grave injury and wander from the truth. I ought to take up the defense of the Muslim cause, if I wished to write the truth, in many things which are falsely attributed to them. If anyone prefers to propagate those lies, supported by no authority, which are told about the Turks—such as that they are blockheads, and asses, and insane, and devils, and if anything is worse—he may do so as far as I am concerned; I who daily experience more and more that the world wants to be deceived, and to be ruled by preconceived opinions." *Ibid. p. 47. n. 13.*

<summary>In this passage, Adrian Reland argues for an objective study of Islam based on its actual doctrines rather than common European slanders, while defending himself against charges of being a secret supporter of the faith. John Toland uses Reland’s words to highlight the narrow-mindedness of critics like Dr. Mangey.</summary>
