I experience more and more every day that the world loves to be cheated, and to be governed by preconceived opinions."

This last part of the accusation is true enough—which should be no great surprise, since Christians grossly belie one another every day—but as for the first part, that he has given a false account of them in any point, neither Dr. Mangey nor anyone else can ever prove against this most sincere of men. 

In the meantime, he truly <note>original: "from his heart"</note> detested the actual fables and absurdities of the Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note>, as did his and my teacher $^8$ Spanheim <note>Friedrich Spanheim the Younger (1632–1701), a professor of theology and sacred history at Leiden.</note>; who yet, as was fitting for a person of genuine learning and a noble spirit, instead of railing against Muhammad <note>original: "Mahomet"</note> as a mad and senseless fellow (though sincerely admitting him to have been a wicked impostor), says $^9$: 

> "It is certain that he was remarkably endowed with natural gifts: of an excellent appearance, having a sharp mind, pleasant manners, showing generosity to the poor, courtesy to everyone, courage against his enemies, and above all, a deep reverence for the name of God."

# MANGONEUTES

...murderers, backbiters, prodigals, misers, false witnesses, and so on. He [Muhammad] was a great preacher (he continues) of patience, charity, mercy, generosity <note>original: "beneficence"</note>, gratitude, reverence to parents and superiors, and of the praises of God. 

The deceptions, lies, disguises, and inventions of Muhammad <note>original: "Mahomet"</note> in other respects did not prevent even the most zealous Protestant historians from giving him a good character where they thought he deserved it. Bypassing Dr. Prideaux <note>Humphrey Prideaux (1648–1724), an English clergyman whose work *The Old and New Testament Connected* was a massive bestseller of historical scholarship.</note>—whose immortal work on the connection between the Old and New Testaments is as learned as it is entertaining, and the most significant to appear in Britain for a long time—and omitting several others who refused to take unfair advantage of Islam <note>original: "Mahometanism"</note>, or rather refused to discredit themselves by supporting the poorly-invented stories of lying friars and irritable <note>original: "petulant"</note> but servile and shallow chaplains: I shall only quote a passage from Maracci <note>Ludovico Maracci (1612–1700), an Italian priest and scholar who translated the Quran into Latin; though a critic of Islam, he was respected for his linguistic accuracy.</note>. In another place (also quoted by Reland <note>Adrian Reland (1676–1718), the Dutch scholar whose objective approach to Islamic studies was controversial at the time.</note> before me), Maracci says that $^{11}$ "this superstition [Islam] contains everything found plausible or probable in the Christian religion, and everything that seems conformable to the law or light of nature." 

This is enough, I think, to consider them a kind of Christian—and not the worst kind, either. But the passage I specifically mean is this: $^{12}$ "I have always been of the opinion—persuaded by both experience and reason—that if the Quran <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> and the Gospel were proposed to those nations [namely the Jews and the Gentiles], they would still embrace the Quran and the Islamic <note>original: "Mahometan"</note> superstition sooner than the Gospel and the Christian [religion]..."

<meta>This page continues a discussion of why Islam (referred to as "the Alcoran") might appear more attractive to non-believers than Christianity. It specifically contrasts the rational and sensory appeal of Islamic tenets with the "difficult" mysteries and moral demands of the Christian Gospel, referencing the work of the scholar Ludovico Maracci.</meta>

# MANGONEUTES

...religion; unless they are carefully instructed beforehand regarding the truth of the *Gospel* and the lies and frauds of the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note>. For at first sight, those things contained in the *Quran* will appear more consistent with the dictates of our flawed human nature than what the *Gospel* proposes.

For example, [the *Quran* teaches] that there is one God—omnipotent, omniscient, the creator and ruler of all things—who has nothing in common with created beings; that fervent and frequent prayers should be addressed to him; that alms be distributed to the poor, holy pilgrimages undertaken, the body disciplined <note>original: "mortify'd"</note> with fasting, and justice observed; that modesty, kindness <note>original: "beneficence"</note>, piety, and other virtues be practiced; that no one should be wronged; and that thefts, adulteries, murders, and other crimes should be avoided. It teaches that worldly things should be despised as temporary and that good works should be performed. Moreover, it says that all people must give an account of their actions to God; that in heaven, eternal happiness is prepared for the good, involving the enjoyment of those things that human nature desires with the greatest passion; and that eternal punishment is prepared in hell for the wicked—along with other similar things which certainly occur throughout the *Quran*.

But if a non-believer <note>original: "Heathen"</note> hears a Minister of the *Gospel* propose to him one God in three persons; God made man; a poor, crucified God who was dead and buried; the mystery of the *Eucharist*; the necessity of the *Sacrament of Penance*; marriage to only one wife; the unbreakable bond <note>original: "indissoluble knot"</note> of wedlock; a life joined to constant suffering; doing good to one's enemies; and the highest happiness consisting of things that "no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor entered into the heart of man" <note>A quotation from the New Testament (1 Corinthians 2:9) describing the spiritual nature of heaven.</note>. When such a person hears these things—which either surpass human understanding or are most difficult, if not impossible, for the natural state or weakness of man—and compares them with the doctrines of the *Quran*, he will immediately reject the former and embrace the latter. 

How well **MARACCI** <note>Ludovico Maracci (1612–1700) was an Italian priest and scholar famous for his linguistically accurate Latin translation of the Quran, though he wrote from a critical Catholic perspective.</note> has judged...

There was nothing more common.

17. <note>original: "In Evangeliis & apostolis multa de veteri Testamento legimus quae in nostris codicibus non habentur, ut est illud, ex Aegypto vocavi filium meum [Mat. 2. 15.] et quoniam Nazaraeus vocabitur [Ibid. 2. 23.]—— Interrogemus ergo eos, ubi haec scripta sint? et cùm dicere non potuerint, de libris Hebraicis proferamus. Commentar. in Esai. 11. 2."</note> In the Gospels and the writings of the Apostles, we read many things said to be from the Old Testament which are not found in our manuscripts, such as, "Out of Egypt I have called my son" [Matthew 2:15] and "because he shall be called a Nazarene" [Matthew 2:23]. Let us ask them, therefore, where these things are written? And when they are unable to say, let us bring them forth from the Hebrew books. The first testimony is in HOSEA [11:1]. The second is in ISAIAH [11:1], etc. — *From Jerome's Preface to the Pentateuch. See also the same author on the same subject in Book 4 of his Commentary on Isaiah 11:2.*

<summary>Toland argues that the title "Nazarene" refers to Jesus’s hometown of Nazareth rather than the Old Testament concepts of a "branch" (Netzer) or a "Nazarite" (Nazir). He critiques Saint Jerome for trying to force a prophetic Old Testament origin for a term that Matthew clearly attributes to geography.</summary>

<header>MANGONEUTES.</header>

<margin>So do **JEROME** and **AUGUSTINE**.</margin>
<margin>Matthew 2:23. Mark 1:24.</margin>
**ELIJAH** and **ELISHA**. Certainly, my Lord, it does not reflect well on the dignity of his office <note>original: "honor of the Tippet and Scarf," referring to the formal vestments worn by high-ranking clergy and chaplains</note>, that a Doctor of Divinity and a Bishop’s Chaplain should know so little about the *Bible* and the history of the early Church <note>original: "Ecclesiastical Antiquity"</note>. I cannot even credit him with having learned from *dictionaries* <note>original: "Lexicons"</note>, though he may be confused by words that sound similar in *Indexes*. We know there are many such words, and that they are sometimes confused or mistaken for one another. Some authors write these words as close to their original roots as possible; others adapt them to the grammar of the language they are writing in; and many (without being so precise <note>original: "nice"</note>) use either form interchangeably. This is not entirely blameworthy, provided they take care to distinguish their subject when a real difference exists. Examples of this are <sup>23</sup> *Esseans* and *Essenes*, which are two ways of expressing the same thing. And, to bring the point closer to home, the same is true for <sup>24</sup> *Nazaren*, *Nazarean*, *Natzarean*, and *Nazarite* or *Nazirite*, and so on. Beyond any doubt, these terms are often confused—even by the same authors—which I am far from approving, though I can tolerate it. For my part, I think it makes no difference which of the two words, *Nazarean* or *Nazaren*, anyone uses when speaking of the person of **JESUS** of *Nazareth* or those named after him. The first word is found in **MATTHEW**, and the second in **MARK**. Therefore, I have consistently written *Nazaren*, not only because it is equally authorized by the *Gospel* (though Dr. **MANGEY** attributes it to the negligence of transcribers) but also because it is used much more often by the most accurate Church writers in Latin.

The *Old Testament Nazarites* or *Nazirites* <note>People who took a specific vow of separation to God, such as Samson or Samuel</note> should never be confused with the *New Testament Nazarenes*. Your Lordship will readily believe that I shall proceed as I have begun, despite the criticism of your Chaplain, who claims <margin>Page 9.</margin> that *I have made a blunder in my very title; he says that if I wanted to name my book after the sect I defend, I should have called it Nazaraeus and not Nazarenus*.

His other comments are of the same sharp insight and fairness, such as when he says, <margin>Page 55.</margin> the author of *NAZARENUS was aware that it would not be honorable for his sect to be named after any single person, since that alone is a mark of some departure from the truth*. Is that so? I protest that I never knew this before, and I would now be sorry if it were true. Why? Is not the name *Christian* derived from a single person? This would only be false if the Doctor distinguishes between a man’s name and his surname, or between those and his title or office, all of which are designations for a single person.

# MANGONEUTES

The Old Testament Nazarites or Nazirites should never be confused with the New Testament Nazarenes; this is another reason why I have always used the latter word instead of *Nazarean*, which others may prefer if they wish, as it is appropriate enough. Your Lordship will readily believe that I shall proceed as I have begun, despite the criticism of your Chaplain, who says that:

<margin>Page 9.</margin>
*I have blundered in my very title since, if I wanted to name my book after the sect I defend, I should have called it* **Nazaraeus** *and not* **Nazarenus**. <note>The Chaplain argues for the Latin form *Nazaraeus* over Toland's *Nazarenus*.</note>

His next statement is of the same sharpness and fairness:

<margin>Page 55.</margin>
***Nazarenus*** *was aware that it would not be for the honor of his sect to be named after any single person, since that alone is a sign of some departure from the truth.* <note>Mangey suggests that naming a religious group after a human founder is a mark of heresy.</note> Is that so? I declare I never knew this before, and would now be sorry if it were true. Why? Is not the name of *Christian* derived from a single person?—unless the Doctor distinguishes between a man’s name and his surname, or between these and his title or office, which are all various ways of identifying a single person. But I wonder how such a strange idea <note>original: "crotchet"</note> entered his head, for I never imagined anything like this. Furthermore, my sect is certainly named after a single person, namely **JESUS** of *Nazareth*, as I declared in these very words in ***Nazarenus***.

<margin>Page 26.</margin>
It was not, therefore, an imaginary or irrational fear of a single founder <note>original: "author"</note> that led me to say the *Nazarenes* were also called *Ebionites* because of their poverty; rather, it was because they said so <gloss>25</gloss> themselves.

<margin>See *Nazarenus*, pages 26, 27, 28, 29.</margin>

**ELIJAH and ELISHA.** Certainly, my Lord, it does no credit to the clerical robes <note>original: "honor of the Tippet and Scarf," referring to the vestments worn by high-ranking clergy and chaplains</note> that a Doctor of Divinity, and a Bishop’s Chaplain at that, should know so little about the Bible and church history <note>original: "Ecclesiastical Antiquity"</note>. We know many such words exist, and they are sometimes mixed up or mistaken for one another. Some authors write them as close to their original roots as possible; others write them according to the rules of the language they are currently using; and many (without being so precise <term>nice</term>) use either way interchangeably. This is not entirely blameworthy, provided they take care to distinguish their subject when there is a real difference.

<margin>So do Jerome and Augustine.</margin>
Of this kind are <note>23</note> **Essaeans** and **Essenes** <note>original: "Essans and Essenes"</note>, which are two ways of expressing the same thing. And, to come closer to home, in the same category are <note>24</note> **Nazaren**, **Nazarean**, and **Natzarean**; and **Nazarite** or **Nazirite**, and so on. Undoubtedly, these terms are often confused, even by the same authors. While I do not approve of this, I can tolerate it. As for myself, I think it matters little which of the two words, **Nazarean** or **Nazaren**, anyone uses when speaking of the person of **JESUS of Nazareth**, or of the people named after him;

<margin>Matthew 2:23. Mark 1:24.</margin>
the first word is found in **MATTHEW**, and the second in **MARK**. Therefore, I have consistently written **Nazaren**, as it is not only equally authorized by the Gospel (though Dr. Mangey attributes this to the negligence of scribes) but is also much more frequently used by the most accurate church writers in Latin.

<margin>Acts 2:44, 45 and 4:34, 35, etc.</margin>
...with which I have adorned my margin, is not in the *Syriac* <note>an ancient dialect of Aramaic used by early Christians in the Near East</note> copy [and did I say it was, or will he say that copy is as old as the time of the Apostles?] nor in the Hebrew text of MUNSTER <note>Sebastian Münster, a 16th-century scholar who published a Hebrew translation of the Gospel of Matthew</note> [admirable! a translation made just the other day] nor does it anywhere signify *Evangelical Poverty*. It certainly signifies the poverty under which they labored: beggary, or the lack of their goods which they laid at the Apostles' feet. For this reason, they were not ashamed of this name, as EPIPHANIUS <note>a 4th-century Church Father known for his writings against heresies</note> relates.

<margin>Page 124.</margin>
I could adorn my margin with seven other Hebrew words for *Poor* from the common dictionaries, and in my text show that one of them signifies a poorness of mind that is neither evangelical nor philosophical. I have no need to point out an example of it after the drudgery I have now been obliged to undergo in weighing words and measuring syllables. The Doctor will, in time, see my margins very much adorned with Hebrew, and so have an occasion to "detect" my ignorance in Oriental learning, since he cannot do so in Irish. I am amazed he hesitates at this language, since undoubtedly he has undertaken a task infinitely more difficult to perform than Irish is to learn. *There was not a doctrine,* says he, *received by the whole Church during the first three centuries, that I will not take upon me to defend.* Ah, indeed! Here is a master of defense able to play at theological quarterstaff <note>a traditional English stick-fighting weapon; Toland is comparing Mangey's theological arguments to a physical brawl</note> with any person who dares enter the ring with him! I do not wonder, therefore, that a champion of such redoubtable prowess (who yet will be humbly asked: what was the "whole Church"? How many witnesses are required to show her uniform belief? And what were the doctrines she universally believed?)—some of which, notwith-

I have learned from the same Sage to whom I owned my obligations once before, *not to be more afraid of the threats of fools than of the blows of the sick.* <note>The quote is from Demophilus’s *Similitudes*: "Neither the blow of a sick person, nor the threat of a fool, should be feared." original: "Ουτ’ αρρωστου πληγην, ουτ’ ανοητου απειλην δει ευλαβεισθαι."</note> But though I shall never enter into argument with him, I consider myself bound at any time to detect his fallacies for more reasons than your Lordship, whose hours are better employed, cares to hear.

VIII. AS for the summary account I gave of the ancient *Irish Christianity* <note>Toland argued that the early Irish church was "Protestant" in character and independent of Rome before the 12th century</note> before the *Papal Corruptions* and *Usurpations*, it is worthy of observation that I quoted no others but Latin or English writers to prove it. However, he declines commenting <note>original: "animadverting"</note> on that part, under the pretense of not understanding the *Irish language* and *customs*, leaving me elsewhere to *spend my critical conjectures upon Irish, where I may hope few will examine me.* Notwithstanding his contemptible contempt, I shall continue to do so; and in such a manner, I hope, that the most able critics will not only think my time well spent, but thank me as well, regardless of how different their nations or religions may be. ...and private confession <note>original: "auricular confession," referring to confession whispered in the ear of a priest</note> for the purpose of penance and the authoritative absolution of a single priest. Besides those other Irish holy men whom I mentioned in the second part of *Nazarenus* <note>A work by John Toland published in 1718, which argued that the early Irish church was independent of Rome.</note>, and who converted to Christianity so great a share of Britain, of Upper and Lower Germany, and other countries; it is undeniably acknowledged by scholars that **Sedulius the Younger**, **Samson**, **Virgil**, and **Claudius Clemens**, along with his companion and assistant **Adelbert** (a Frenchman educated in Ireland), were the most noted witnesses of truth and the most vigorous opponents of error in that age. They openly denounced the abuses that had already taken hold, as well as those that were daily creeping in. 

They especially distinguished themselves against the usurped authority and tyranny of the Roman See <note>The jurisdiction or office of the Pope.</note>, the superstitious feasts and fasts ordained by it, the ignorance and lewdness of the priests and friars, empty traditions, the worship of images, saints, or angels, prayers for the dead and purgatory, the celibacy of the clergy, the honoring of relics, going on pilgrimages, and the like. On the other hand, they highly recommended the reading of the *Scriptures* as the only sure antidote against all these fictions and abuses. And though they were not entirely free from the "infection" of those dark and dismal times—or perhaps did not dare to declare themselves so—yet, by the confession of many of their own enemies, they were the most learned and pious people living at that time. 

Much later than this, in the 14th century, we had among many others **Richard FitzRalph**, a native of Dundalk (where his anniversary is still observed), who was for a time Chancellor of Oxford, Dean of Lichfield, and finally Archbishop of Armagh. He was a student of the famous **John Baconthorpe**, styled *the Resolute Doctor*, and a contemporary of the glorious **Wycliffe** <note>John Wycliffe (c. 1320s–1384), an English reformer and "Morning Star of the Reformation" who translated the Bible into Middle English.</note>, before whose...

IX. If this is a digression, it is one of the most excusable for several reasons—particularly because it is not so long that we lose sight of the subject we last discussed. I am referring to the fact that Christians were first called *Nazarenes* <note>original: "Nazarens"; a term derived from Jesus's hometown that was used for the earliest Jewish followers of Christ</note> by themselves and everyone else, after JESUS of Nazareth, their founder. This was not done out of contempt at first, though later this name became a mark of reproach when the *Jewish Christians* were pushed out of the Church and declared heretics by the Gentile converts. These converts (with two or three exceptions) were completely ignorant of the original Hebrew and only slightly familiar with translations of the *Bible*. 

I will now leave this subject, only asking your Lordship's permission to remark that the name *Christian*—in which I glory no less than in the now more specific name of *Nazarene*—has not lacked "orthodox" doctors who misinterpret its origin. What we know for <margin>Acts 11:26</margin> certain is that *the disciples were first called by that name at Antioch*. However, it is uncertain whether they were named by themselves or by others, by friends or by enemies. Some believe it was the latter, based on the words of Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a Roman historian who wrote that the "common people" called them Christians</note>, whose words are ambiguous, even if he were a reliable judge of the matter,

...that <note>original: footnote 31 quoting Tacitus, *Annals* 15.44: "Quos, per flagitia invisos, vulgus Christianos appellabat" — "Whom, being hated for their atrocities, the common people called Christians."</note> being hated for their crimes, the common people called them *Christians*. "*The Prophet anticipated this objection, saying: 'To those who serve me will be given a new name, which shall be blessed on the earth.' Let us ask the Jews: 'Do you serve the Lord or not?' Show us, therefore, your new name; for you are called 'Jews' and 'Israelites' by Moses and by the other Prophets, both after the return from Babylon and to this day. Where then is your new name? This name encompasses the whole world; for the name of the Jews extends only to a certain country, but that of the Christians extends to the ends of the earth, seeing that the name of the only-begotten Son of God is preached.*"

Surely the *Jews* must have been extremely ignorant or willfully blind to resist the evidence of such "admirable" reasoning and such a "profound" understanding of the *Scriptures* from Patriarch Cyril. <note>The author is using irony here to mock Cyril's logic and his use of Isaiah to justify the name "Christian."</note> What is left for us to do in this case? ...the more dangerous of the two. But lest Cyril might stand alone (as he does not) in giving a wrong account of the name *Christians*, there were afterwards certain forged canons, claimed to have been decreed in the second Council of 33 Antioch. For if only two or three Apostles, or a few more Christians, met on any special occasion, they must immediately be transformed into a Church "Council." The first of these canons ordains *that those who believe in the Lord* JESUS, *who were by those of his time called Galileans, should for the future be called Christians*. 

To much the same purpose writes 34 Suidas <note>original: "SUIDAS," referring to the *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note>, who (though the Doctor overlooks it) makes *Nazaraeus* and *Nazarenus* to be the very 35 same. This is also the case in the *New Testament* and in Jerome’s <note>original: "Jerom."</note> translation of it, along with many others. So far is *Nazarenus* from being a blunder of transcribers, as we sufficiently proved before.

*...that the Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note> charge our Gospels with corruption and alteration in many things, and particularly that Muhammad’s <note>original: "MAHOMET'S"</note> name was erased from them, as likewise from the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible (Genesis to Deuteronomy).</note> and the Psalms; you are to inquire of the most learned, judicious, and candid among them, how they can prove such deletions <note>original: "expunctions"</note> or insertions <note>original: "interpolations"</note>, if they have no authentic copies to compare against ours? That the Muslims charge our Gospels with those alterations is undeniable by everyone. The Doctor vehemently suspects that the prohibition of "strangled things" <note>Refers to the dietary restriction in Acts 15:20 against eating animals that were killed by strangulation rather than butchered according to Jewish law.</note> does not belong to the original text of the Bible. Yet, the learned Étienne de Courcelles <note>original: "Curcelleus," a 17th-century theologian and critic of the New Testament text.</note> (whom the Doctor has copied without naming him) worked on this point over fifty years ago. Near the very beginning of his chapter on the subject, he says that *he deservedly* 38 *suspects this clause, as it is not only unacknowledged by many ancient Fathers, but even directly rejected as a forgery or a later addition.* I shall not enter into the merits of this specific case here, but I am convinced that if I had used so little formality in questioning the authenticity of a passage in the Bible, it would be loudly proclaimed that I was letting my own whimsies override the **Scripture**. 

I previously noted one of the Doctor's supposedly "fine" criticisms regarding the origin of the word *Nazarene*, which is widely different from the reason given for it by the Gospel of Matthew. His claim that I used the word *Periclete* instead of *Paraclete* is typical of his conduct. I never said that; rather, I noted that the Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note> pretended that *Periclyte* <note>The Greek word "Periklytos" (famous/celebrated) is similar to "Parakletos" (advocate/comforter). Muslim polemicists argued the New Testament originally predicted the coming of Muhammad using this term.</note> should be read instead of *Paraclete*. For him to charge me with <margin>by the *Periclyte* and not by the *Paraclete* <note>original: περικλυτῷ & non παρακλήτῷ</note>, Nazaren. p. 13.</margin> a lack of skill in **Greek** for this is consistent with the rest of his behavior. I scorn to stop and address his frequent nitpicking and falsifications of this sort elsewhere, as all judicious readers laugh at his boastful <note>original: "thrasonic," a term derived from Thraso, a character in Roman comedy known for bragging.</note> and arrogant way of writing—a style supported by nothing but his own ignorance of the facts or his belief that others will not bother to examine them.

...never be his Lordship’s case. That first Principle of the Reformation <note>The "Principle of the Reformation" refers to the right of private judgment—the idea that individuals should interpret scripture and religious matters for themselves rather than relying solely on church authority.</note> which he maintains daily gains ground, even through the opposition made to it; and he, in pursuing it, shows himself to be swayed by none of those sordid aims which might slacken his ardor, or by such mean politics as would be sure to sully his fame. Nor is he the only one among our present Bishops for whose piety and learning I have, on many occasions, testified my unfeigned respect. What if I differ from their Lordships on some speculative points of a dubious or difficult nature? I sincerely agree with them on the life of Christianity: in mutual charity, in the toleration of tender consciences <note>A common 17th and 18th-century term for individuals who had sincere religious scruples that prevented them from conforming to the established church.</note>, in the love of knowledge, the hatred of superstition, and the preservation of civil liberty. It is doubtless more unjustifiable <note>original: "unwarrantable"</note> for my adversaries to differ from them regarding these eternal truths than for me to differ from them regarding any disputable institutions, even if they were in the right and I happened to be ever so much in the wrong. Their disapproval of me on some points would never induce me to disown what I approved of in them. Some of the best friends I have in the world differ from me on many things, which does not hinder them from loving and esteeming me (affections that are reciprocal) on account of other things more important to life and society. I frankly declared in <margin>Preface, pages 23, 24.</margin> **NAZARENUS**, that I conversed with men of all nations and religions, provided they were masters of any art, science, or other good quality from which I might reap benefit or entertainment. Those who teach their followers to avoid anyone, except men of wicked <note>original: "flagitious"</note> lives, are afraid that their own ignorance or their deceit might be discovered. But whatever the motive may be, it cannot proceed from the knowledge of truth, which

Translation: I also wonder at this: that a Christian man could cultivate a friendship with Symmachus, a most bitter enemy of this name [Christianity] (for it is well known that he was such, from his own report to the Emperors and the response to it by Prudentius and Ambrose), and not just a common friendship, but one so intimate that they addressed each other as father. Moreover, in friendship, the highest agreement of divine and human things is required. From the Letter to Solomon Pantherus.</note></margin>
*“I particularly wonder,”* he says, *“that a Christian man should cultivate a friendship (and no ordinary one, but so intimate that the one called the other father) with Symmachus <note>Quintus Aurelius Symmachus, a 4th-century Roman statesman who was a prominent advocate for traditional Roman paganism.</note>, a most bitter enemy to this profession <note>The "profession" refers to the Christian faith.</note>: for that he was such an enemy appears from his own Address to the Emperors, and from the answers made to it by Prudentius <note>Aurelius Prudentius Clemens, a Roman Christian poet.</note> and Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose, the Bishop of Milan.</note>. Pray, who has a right to require such a chimera <note>A mythological monster made of disparate parts; here used to mean a wild, unrealistic, or impossible fancy.</note>, such an impossibility? XI. This opposite behavior makes many, who would like to be seen as men of letters, both savage and senseless misanthropes <note>original: "Misanthropes"; people who dislike and avoid human society.</note>. I remember Mr. Wotton <note>William Wotton (1666–1727), an English scholar and critic.</note> (whom I do not include in this group) has this passage <note>Note 40: *Letter to Eusebia*, occasioned by Mr. Toland's *Letters to Serena*.</note> speaking <margin>Page 47.</margin> of Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a Dutch philosopher whose rationalist views were often seen as heretical or atheistic in the 18th century.</note>, which perhaps he would not justify today: *"Mr. Toland pretends to treat him kindly, and says he thinks all men ought to be treated so in matters of mere speculation, leaving their immoral actions (if they are guilty of any) to the care of the law and the criticism <note>original: "animadversion"; a formal expression of disapproval or official censure.</note> of the magistrate."* I still think so, even though I disagree with Spinoza on the very foundation of his philosophy, and was refuting him at that very same time. I can praise a man in many things even while I leave or oppose him in others; nor is there any habit that exposes writers to more ridiculous extremes than thinking themselves obligated to answer every point made by someone with whom they disagree on a single issue. If Mr. Wotton had reflected on this, he would not think it inconsistent for me to call Spinoza a "great man," <margin>Letter to Eusebia, Page 47.</margin> yet still say his system of matter without motion is unrefined and unphilosophical. Forbearing all other remarks on these lines, I beg your Lordship to observe that RELIGION is here, by a Clergyman of the Church of England, made to exist only through the women; and that, without their influence, it would—despite the men—be irrecoverably lost even in the next generation. I am as much ashamed and concerned, my Lord, for this singular passage as if it had been written by myself or on my behalf, considering what is proverbially meant in everyone’s mouth by the "devout female sex." 

Women have been noted in all ages for supporting SUPERSTITION, which is grounded on fanciful, flashy, fearful, and weak notions of the governing powers. This, therefore, ought to be ever carefully distinguished from RELIGION, which is grounded on the clearest ideas of God and the strongest reasonings about his goodness, power, and providence <note>original: "providence"; the catchword "dence" on the original page is the completion of this word.</note>.

In the meantime, I will provide my friend <note>Toland is referring to his critic, William Wotton.</note> with a couple of eminent writers whose authority he may use against me if he wishes. One is a pagan, the other a Christian. It is a thing universally taken for granted, says ⁴¹ STRABO <note>Strabo (c. 64 BC–24 AD) was a Greek geographer and philosopher.</note>, *that women are the ringleaders of superstition; they tease their husbands into all sorts of worship of the gods, and into the observation of feasts and fasts. But it is a rare thing for any man living a single life to be found as such a person.* Despite my interest in bachelors and my respect for the ladies, I am sorry that the religious zeal of women should, for the most part, appear the very same in Turkey <note>In this context, "Turkey" refers broadly to the Islamic world or the Ottoman Empire.</note> as in Christendom, and among pagans as among Jews. 

But not to forget the other writer: when the priests enter a family, ⁴² says Mr. SELDEN <note>John Selden (1584–1654), an English jurist and polymath known for his intellectual wit.</note>, *they act like a man who wants to set a house on fire. He does not set fire to the brick wall, but thrusts it into the thatch. They work upon the women and leave the men alone.* HIPPOCRATES <note>Hippocrates (c. 460–370 BC), the ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note>, a greater man than either of them, attributes this proneness of women to superstition ⁴³ *to their very nature, as being of a weaker and more fearful disposition than men.*

...is as much owing to education as to nature, if not more; as I showed in the Preface to those *Letters to* **Serena**, which gave Mr. **Wotton** an opportunity of complimenting the women at the expense of his own order <note>Wotton was a clergyman; Toland implies he insulted his fellow priests to flatter women.</note>. In a Letter concerning the origin and force of *Prejudices*, the first of those to <gloss>44</gloss> **Serena**, as I was showing the successive growth and increase of prejudices through every step of our lives, I began with the impressions we receive in the womb, from the constitution of both our parents, or from accidents particularly befalling our mothers, and then

proceeded thus:

> We are, immediately after our birth, delivered to *nurses*—ignorant women of the lowest class <note>original: "meanest vulgar"</note>—who infuse into us their errors along with their milk. They frighten us into being quiet with the threats of *Raw-head* and *Bloody-bones*, *bugaboos* <note>original: "buggle-boes"</note> and *bull-beggars* <note>These were all legendary monsters or "bogeymen" used in English folklore to frighten children into obedience.</note>. <meta>This page continues Toland’s defense against William Wotton. ...in the nighttime. What is invented at the start to keep children under control (a control that indeed makes them miserable slaves forever after) is believed by them in all seriousness when they grow older. In this way, the whole generation and country eventually become persuaded of it—and to such a degree that many people, who are otherwise sensible enough, dare not sleep alone in a room or travel except by daylight. Much less do they have the courage to enter empty houses or churches. From our *nurses*, we are brought home, where we are placed in even worse hands among idle and ignorant servants. Their chief entertainment consists of talk about fairies, elves, witchcraft, walking ghosts, fortune-telling, consulting astrologers, or other such imaginary <note>original: "chimerical" (referring to the Chimera, a mythical beast; meaning wild, fantastic, or unreal)</note> activities. They delight in frightening or deceiving one another, often to carry out their own private schemes <note>original: "intrigues"</note>. These things, no matter how they are intended, never fail to make a disastrous impression on children—and for the most part, our parents are no wiser. Then we are sent away to school, where all the young people arrive equally infected from home. There they hear of nothing but demons, nymphs, spirits <note>original: "genii" (in classical mythology, guardian spirits or minor deities)</note>, satyrs, fauns, apparitions, prophecies, transformations, and other astounding miracles. We repeat all our stories among ourselves; whatever might be hidden from a child in a sensible family, he is certain to hear about it at school, and so on.

I continue in the same manner, without the slightest allegory or other disguise, to describe the specific prejudices acquired in universities, churches, royal courts, and all walks of life. I have produced this long passage now, not only because your Lordship was out of England when my book was published, but also to show that a man must have abandoned all sense of conscience—and believe others to be completely lacking in common sense—when he solemnly claims (as cited above <margin>as cited above <note>original: "Ubi supra"</note></margin>) that when I speak of bugbears, elves, spirits, and similar nonsense <note>original: "cant" (jargon or hypocritical talk)</note>, I really mean the doctrines of **JESUS CHRIST**

<meta>This page continues John Toland’s defense against his critics, specifically William Wotton. Toland is arguing that his previous writings focused on social superstition rather than attacking core Christian doctrines, and he is now accusing his detractors of hypocrisy.</meta>

...God and Man, of the three persons in the **Trinity**, and of the **Redemption** wrought by our blessed **Savior** on the **Cross**, and so forth. I have never, from the beginning to this day, treated these doctrines in any of my books, nor even mentioned them except where I denied the charge of **Socinianism** <note>Socinianism: A rationalist Christian movement that denied the Trinity and the full divinity of Christ. In the 17th and 18th centuries, calling someone a "Socinian" was a common way to label them a dangerous heretic.</note>. I now, in turn, charge the authors of that slander <note>original: "aspersion"</note> with falsehood, hypocrisy, fraud, and impiety. After shamelessly defaming a man and doing their utmost to ruin him by the most despicable practices, even when they are detected or disappointed in their aims, they do not make the least effort toward restoring the good name of the person they have injured.

<margin>Proverbs 30:20</margin>
*But, like the adulterous woman in the book of Proverbs,* they wipe their mouths and say, "We have done no wickedness."

XII. My Lord, what I have written here regarding Mr. Wotton <note>William Wotton (1666–1727): An English clergyman and scholar who engaged in several high-profile intellectual disputes with Toland.</note> is no digression. Rather, it serves as an example of the subject of the last and the preceding paragraphs: namely, that certain men will neither allow themselves nor others to commend anything in someone from whom they differ; and that they do not hesitate to say anything to his prejudice, no matter how improbable or even false it might be. 

These are the men who give religion its deepest wounds. They are not only the real and most dangerous unbelievers themselves, but they also tempt the unwary and thoughtless to become such. For if they were truly persuaded of the doctrines of Christianity, they would not, in direct opposition to them, abandon all truth and charity. Nor would others think that they only made a profitable business <note>original: "gainful trade"</note> of teaching those holy doctrines—a suspicion that only arises because people perceive that their practice is so glaringly contrary to their profession. Now, since I am on this subject, and since at the beginning of this *Letter* I established my right to

# MANGONEUTES <note>original: "Mangoneutes" is a Greek term for a "trickster," "cheat," or "deceiver," often used to describe someone who falsifies or misrepresents goods—here likely used to label Toland's critics as intellectual frauds.</note>

...demand justice of those among your Lordship's Clergy who had injured me; I shall lay before you the cause of some reasonable complaint I believe I have against Dr. **Hare**, a Prebendary of your own Cathedral. This learned gentleman, dragging me into a work of his without the least occasion or provocation, led me to publish the following advertisement on the 2nd of last February in the *Post-man* and in *St. James's Evening Post*: 

"Whereas the Reverend Dr. **Hare**, in the Postscript to the 4th edition of his Sermon (*Church-Authority Vindicated*) published last Friday, uses the following words on page 48, speaking of the right reverend the *Bishop* of **Bangor’s** <note>Benjamin Hoadly (1676–1761), the Bishop of Bangor, was a key figure in the Bangorian Controversy regarding church authority.</note> writings: 'It must be allowed his Lordship judges very truly when he says they are faint resemblances <note>original: "resemblances" here means imitations or likenesses</note> of Mr. **Chillingworth** <note>William Chillingworth (1602–1644): A famous theologian known for his defense of Protestantism based on reason and the Bible alone.</note>; for envy itself must admit his Lordship has some likeness to that great man; just such a likeness as Mr. **Toland** has to Mr. **Locke**, who, in *Christianity not Mysterious*, is often quoted to support notions he never dreamed of.' 

Now, this is to inform all those who have not read *Christianity not Mysterious* that I have never named Mr. **Locke** in any edition of that book; and far from 'often quoting him,' I have not even included a single quotation from him to support notions he never dreamed of. I hope Mr. **Locke** himself may be heard in a matter where he is so nearly concerned. In the dispute between him and the then Bishop of Worcester, Dr. **Stillingfleet**, his Lordship was pleased to affirm that what he opposed in my book was built on Mr. **Locke**; an allegation of which the latter, in his second Reply, sufficiently shows the falsity. 'The Author of *Christianity not Mysterious*,' says he,

'supposes that we must have clear and distinct ideas of whatever we pretend to any certainty of in our mind. Your Lordship calls this a new

‘ both the dignitaries of *Worcester;* though I should never importune anyone to violate the rules of candor and decorum in doing me a similar kindness.

JOHN TOLAND. Everyone did me all the justice then that I could require on this occasion, except Doctor HARE himself: who, far from giving glory to God and ingenuously acknowledging his fault, had these words inserted in the *Courant* the next day: ‘*Instead of "is often quoted," read "makes great use of Mr.* LOCKE’S *Principles."* First, Mr. LOCKE peremptorily disowns that I made any use of his principles to *support notions he never dreamed of*; and secondly, it appears by the whole connection that this emendation was not in the Doctor’s thoughts at the beginning. Or, even supposing it were, it serves his cause as little as the other way of speaking, since I proceeded upon different principles from Mr. LOCKE—and principles that are better, if you believe the then Bishop of *Worcester*. In short <note>original: "In fine"</note>, no *Slip of the Pen*, nor any of the methods laid down by an ingenious writer, can possibly save the Doctor from dishonest <note>original: "oblique"</note> dealing. Bringing me into his pamphlet by the head and shoulders <note>This idiom means to force someone into a conversation or argument where they do not naturally belong.</note> was unnecessary, if not spiteful, with regard to me or someone else. I say it again: it would have been no condescension below his dignity (since he chose to take notice of me at all) if he had blamed his memory or in any other manner owned his mistake, instead of having recourse to excuses <note>original: "shifts"</note> that deserve a coarser name than I am willing to give, out of the respect I pay him on other accounts. ### MANGONEUTES <note>original: "Mangoneutes" is a Greek term for a "trickster," "cheat," or "deceiver."</note>

> ...way of reasoning. "The Gentleman of this new way of reasoning" <note>Locke is referring to Toland here, whose book *Christianity Not Mysterious* was often linked to Locke's own philosophy.</note>, in his first chapter, says something which aligns with certain notions in my book; but it should be observed that he speaks them as his own thoughts, and not based on my authority, nor even taking any notice of me. Thus again, granting that I used words somewhat like his (as anyone who has read any good philosopher does), "I also humbly conceive," he says, "that he used them as his own, and not as my words; for I do not remember that he quotes me for them." 
<margin>In the same place, page 440.</margin>
> I am certain that in the words quoted from him by your Lordship—the basis on which my book is brought into this—there is not one syllable concerning "Certainty by Ideas" <note>This refers to the epistemological debate over whether knowledge is founded on the agreement of ideas, a central theme in Locke's *Essay Concerning Human Understanding*.</note>. The Bishop himself was forced at last to admit that Mr. LOCKE and I proceeded on different grounds; in fact, he even declared that mine were the better (whether out of fairness to me, or merely to oppose him, I leave to the judgment of the public). 
<margin>In the same place, page 443.</margin>
> To this, Mr. LOCKE replied: "I am supposed to say that the reason I remain unsatisfied is because the author mentioned followed a different path than mine; and to satisfy me, I am told his way is better than mine—which can hardly be considered an answer likely to satisfy me." In short, he shows that I was misrepresented just as much as he was, and he urges the Bishop of Worcester to produce the parallel passages from both his work and mine. Likewise, I hereby call upon the Dean of Worcester to show where I have often, or even once, quoted Mr. LOCKE to support notions he never dreamed of. Just as Mr. LOCKE noticed then that his name and mine were being linked regardless of the context, people cannot help but observe now that the same trick is being used regarding the Bishop of BANGOR <note>Benjamin Hoadly, Bishop of Bangor, whose 1717 sermon arguing that Christ did not delegate authority to any earthly church sparked the "Bangorian Controversy."</note>. For the favor of introducing me into such good company, I thank

### MANGONEUTES <note>original: "Mangoneutes" is a Greek term for a "trickster," "cheat," or "deceiver."</note>

"...both the dignitaries of Worcester; although I would never pressure anyone to violate the rules of fairness and decency by doing me a similar favor."

**JOHN TOLAND.** Everyone gave me all the justice I could have asked for on that occasion, except for Doctor HARE himself. Far from giving glory to God and honestly admitting his mistake, he had these words inserted into the *Courant* <note>The *Daily Courant* was the first daily newspaper in England, starting in 1702.</note> the following day: "Instead of *is often quoted*, read *makes great use of Mr.* LOCKE'S Principles." 

First, Mr. LOCKE flatly denies that I made any use of his principles to *support ideas he never imagined*; and secondly, it is clear from the overall context that this correction was not in the Doctor's mind at the start. Even if it were, it helps his case as little as his previous phrasing, since I followed different principles from Mr. LOCKE—principles that were better, if you believe the then-Bishop of Worcester <note>Edward Stillingfleet, the Bishop of Worcester, famously debated Locke and sometimes used Toland’s arguments to show the alleged dangers of Locke’s philosophy.</note>. 

In short, no *slip of the pen*, nor any of the excuses proposed by a clever <note>45</note> writer, can possibly rescue the Doctor from the charge of dishonest dealing. Dragging me into his pamphlet by the hair <note>original: "by the head and shoulders"—an idiom meaning to force something into a discussion or context where it does not naturally belong.</note> was unnecessary, if not outright spiteful toward me or someone else. 

I say it again: it would not have been beneath his dignity—since he chose to take notice of me at all—if he had simply blamed his memory or admitted his mistake in some other way. Instead, he resorted to dodges that deserve a harsher name than I am willing to give, out of the respect I still have for him in other matters. He will find no man more ready to acknowledge his genuine merits, as I shall have some...

...occasion to do so before I finish this letter. Agreement in opinions <note>original: "Uniformity of sentiments"</note>, as I have already told your Lordship more than once, shall never be the basis of my respect; and honesty <note>original: "Candor"</note> shall always carry more weight with me than learning or intellectual talent <note>original: "Parts"</note>, which, like the rest of the world, I still highly admire. How noble was that saying of Prince EUGENE <note>Prince Eugene of Savoy (1663–1736) was a celebrated general of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>! When sending a mark of his favor from *Leicester* House to the Reverend Mr. WHISTON <note>William Whiston (1667–1752) was a mathematician and theologian who was persecuted for his Arian beliefs (denying the orthodox Trinity).</note>, he said to an annoying fanatic: *"Though I do not approve of all his opinions, yet I respect him as someone suffering for what he is persuaded is the truth."* What a reproach this is to Whiston's Protestant persecutors, coming from the mouth of someone of the Roman Catholic faith <note>original: "Roman communion"</note>!

XIII. I do not know if I should include a certain Mr. PATERSON—who dedicated his book to your Lordship—among my adversaries, whether one considers his intent or his actual performance. The book was not originally intended to be against me; it was even submitted to the press before the small amount said about me at the beginning and the end ever occurred to him. The main body of the work is entirely patched together from Mr. LESLEY’s *four Marks* <note>Referring to Charles Leslie's "four marks" or criteria for proving the historical truth of Christianity, a popular apologetic argument at the time.</note>, which are miserably distorted, as anyone reading quickly <note>original: "one running"</note> can see. I believe whoever told him that framing it as an *Answer* to *NAZARENUS* might help sales intended to do him a kindness. I would not even begrudge him making a penny by using my name, though I think he used it so clumsily that he failed in his goal. He is so poorly informed <note>original: "appriz’d"</note> about the subject I am handling that he wrote this passage:

<meta>This page continues John Toland’s defense of his book *Nazarenus* against his critics, specifically focusing on his definition of "Gentile Christianity" and the role of Jewish law. He transitions into a critique of Mr. Paterson’s understanding of the Protestant Reformers.</meta>

# MANGONEUTES.

The Gentile race, who believed in **CHRIST**, were not bound to observe the ceremonial and national laws of the Jews. In this respect, they were called *Gentile Christians* to distinguish them from the Jews. The Jews, however, were to add the doctrine of **CHRIST** to their specific ritual and religious system <note>original: "Oeconomy"</note> (sacrifices excepted). They are always unconditionally obligated to follow this system to the best of their power, wherever they live. How well I prove this point is another consideration, but I have offered so much evidence toward it that I imagine none of my readers, except Mr. **PATERSON**, could be at a loss to guess what I meant by *Gentile Christianity*.

<margin>Page 70.</margin>

This is especially true when, a few pages later, he counts **ZWINGLI** <note>original: "Zuinglius"</note> and **BEZA** among *the holy successors of the Apostles*. Let him find some assistants who will undertake to show that **CALVIN** stood on a different foundation than **ZWINGLI** and **BEZA**, or else acknowledge that he was, no less than they, one of *the Apostle's holy successors*. If he is not able to do the first, nor anyone else for him, your Lordship will admit that your Dedicator <note>Paterson, who dedicated his book to Toland's correspondent.</note> understood nothing of what he wrote, and that his quarrel is merely with the sound of the word **CALVIN**. <meta>This page continues the defense of the Protestant Reformers, specifically John Calvin, against the criticisms of Mr. Paterson and others. It follows the argument that those who attack the Reformers often do so out of a secret affinity for Roman Catholicism.</meta>

-># MANGONEUTES.<-

...modest malignity, easily descending into festering <note>original: "canker'd"</note> malice. Meanwhile, those who looked further with a longing eye toward Roman Catholicism <note>original: "Roman Popery"</note> never showed him any restraint; instead, they vented all the rage of their hearts in the most scurrilous and insulting terms whenever they had occasion to mention him. I could produce a mountain of scandal against him from the writings of those who claim to be advocates for the Church of England, but who are in fact her most dangerous enemies! I never lacked a surer sign by which to identify an unsound Protestant or a concealed Catholic <note>original: "Papist"</note> than to hear someone speak disrespectfully of those two blessed instruments of restoring Christian Liberty: **LUTHER** and **CALVIN**. 

Just as they courageously argued against the claimed infallibility of others and honestly admitted their own fallibility, being men of the same nature as the rest of the human race, they had their passions and mistakes. The fair rule for judging all people is to characterize a person by their most dominant qualities. The wisest person is the one guilty of the fewest follies, and the most virtuous is the one who commits the fewest faults. Those Reformers had their follies and their faults, which their true admirers are as far from denying as they are from following them as examples. 

But when I compare their failings with their excellencies, and above all when I consider their courage in attempting to deliver from the lowest slavery a world foolishly spellbound <note>original: "sottishly bewitch'd"</note> by fraudulent priests—the most mean-spirited, relentless, and cruelest of enemies—I do not hesitate to rank them above all the conquerors of ancient history. Merely planning what they actually achieved is greater than the most complete victory over a massive army of barbarians. Considering the deep ignorance of the ages immediately before the Reformation, the bigotry of the most powerful princes, the vast influence of the clergy, and the stupendous...

...superstition of the people; I am far more amazed that those heroes discovered so many truths than that they remained in some errors. And on the whole, I am fully persuaded that if they had been as vigorously supported by those who followed them as they themselves began—indeed, had their strategies not been largely changed through lack of foresight, and a shameful compromise <note>original: "composition recreantly made"</note> made in many things with the enemy—Europe would have long ago enjoyed the glorious peace of the *Gospel*, or UNIVERSAL TOLERATION. This toleration is perfectly consistent with NATIONAL ESTABLISHMENTS, but both of them, regarding their final goal of making people wiser and better, are completely incompatible with any ECCLESIASTICAL MONARCHY <note>Ecclesiastical Monarchy: A term describing the absolute power of the Pope or any church hierarchy that claims authority independent of or superior to the civil government.</note> or any other church constitution that is independent of the State. 

I have finished with Mr. PATERSON, after begging the favor of your Lordship (whose authority he claims to revere) to make him point out the place—not only in *Nazarenus* <note>Nazarenus: A 1718 book by John Toland exploring Jewish Christianity and religious history.</note>, but in any book I have ever written—where I oppose the *Anabaptists*? He claims I pretend to do so, along with the Donatists <note>Donatists: A 4th-century Christian sect that argued the church must be a society of saints and rejected those they deemed "traditors" during Roman persecution.</note>. As for the Catholics <note>original: "Papists"</note>, it is freely admitted; but if he cannot show you anything like this, then I would like to know for what purpose he told such a blatant lie? Also, I wish to further convince him that no good end can be served by such wicked means. 

The question here is not what I think of the Anabaptists, but why I should be brought onto the public stage as if I were opposing those with whom I never had a dispute? I have willingly done them justice (as I shall always be ready to do for all parties) when they are mistaken or misrepresented by others. I

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his work, "Nazarenus." He moves from refuting specific theological critics to addressing personal rumors and the "fictions" spread about his supposedly disrespectful treatment of the Apostle Paul.</meta>

-># MANGONEUTES.<-

I would never finish if I collected all the fictions of this sort, which are spread by others with such a composed air of seriousness and assurance as if they were undeniable facts. Nor are they only wandering clergymen <note>original: "vagrant Service-readers"; lower-ranking clergy who read church services for hire, often viewed by the author as unlearned or opportunistic.</note> (the noted scandal of the Church, because they will say or do anything for a morsel of bread) who authorize this detestable practice. Have not some foolish laypeople—after Dr. Mangey <margin>Dr. Mangey</margin> <note>Dr. Thomas Mangey (1688–1755): A clergyman and scholar who published a sharp critique of Toland's "Nazarenus" in 1718.</note> insinuated as much without shame—reported that I did insult the Apostle Paul and called him completely worthless <note>original: "call'd him all to nought"; an archaic idiom meaning to verbally abuse someone or treat them as having no value.</note>? I heard this expression with my own ears in an inn where I was not known. 

However, in the first place, everything of that kind in my book is taken from the works of the *Church Fathers*, who are so highly praised by my adversaries and whose works are commonly edited by clergymen. In the second place, I presented those statements as the slanders or false notions of Paul's opponents. Therefore, if I have abused him in this respect, then the writers of the *New Testament* and the early defenders of the faith <note>original: "primitive Apologists"</note> have treated Jesus Christ just as badly, since they freely recorded the slanders of his enemies whenever the occasion required. 

But in the third place, I have—with a fairness that has not been returned to me, and which I did not expect (since certain people I need not name seldom use it toward each other)—defended the Apostle to the Gentiles against the prejudices <sup>47</sup> of the very sect whose principal beliefs I support regarding the original plan of Christianity. I have defended Paul, I say, against the *Nazarenes* <note>Nazarenes: An early Jewish-Christian group who followed the Mosaic Law while believing in Jesus; Toland's book "Nazarenus" explores their history.</note>. Those adventurers who were not able to defend him themselves—or rather, who expose him even more to the Jews—are now excessively angry with me: a violent presumption...

-># MANGONEUTES.<-
<note>original: "Mangoneutes"; from the Greek word for a "trickster" or "juggler." Toland uses this title as a pun on the name of his critic, Dr. Thomas Mangey.</note>

...the conclusion that I am in the right. <meta>This page continues John Toland's defense of his book Nazarenus against the criticisms of Dr. Thomas Brett. Toland is clarifying his position on "Tradition," distinguishing between unreliable oral tradition and reliable written historical evidence.</meta>

-># MANGONEUTES. <note>The title of Toland's work, from a Greek word meaning "the exposer of impostors."</note> 204 <-

[Suffici]ently attested, not to speak of the inconsistency of most of them with what the Apostles did, said, and wrote. As for written testimony—uninterrupted and unquestioned, properly conveyed and honestly preserved (which anyone may call *Written Tradition* as far as I am concerned)—I have never denied it, nor will I ever. I give him my promise that I shall not dispute the authenticity of any book that can be proven to me by the same type of evidence by which I have accepted the works of HOMER or VIRGIL, DEMOSTHENES or CICERO. 

Therefore, it was not to destroy the evidence for the books of the *New Testament* that I reject *Oral Tradition*, as the Doctor [Thomas Brett] asserts both rashly and uncharitably. Rather, I reject it because I reject certain things established by this "tradition" that are outside of, in addition to, or against the authority of those books. Well then: if I am not discarding *Oral Tradition* to destroy the books of the *New Testament*, then surely (the Doctor claims) it is at least to introduce some other *Scriptures* as having equal authority.

Where have I ever made such an attempt? Or where did I offer the slightest hint that I ever intended to do anything of the kind? At the words of Institution <note>The specific words spoken during the Eucharist: "This is my body..."</note>, not only is the sign of the **Cross** made over the bread while pronouncing "THIS IS MY BODY," but the image of a cross actually appears between the syllables of the word **BODY**, like this: **BO✠DY**. This is done just as it appears in the Latin word **COR✠PUS** <note>original: *Corpus*; Latin for "body."</note> in the **Missal** <note>original: "Mass-book."</note>. The same is done with the word **BLO✠OD**. It seems as if they believe that without these crosses—and if they were not timed <note>original: "nick’d"</note> for that exact instant of pronunciation—the Elements <note>The bread and wine used in the sacrament.</note> might have remained ordinary bread and wine.

I will let others judge for themselves what kind of impression is meant to be made by these crosses and the words being printed in capital letters. For my part, I consider it to be nothing more than conjuring, and just as effective as any magic trick.

In the *Prayer for the Whole State of Christ’s Church*, among other alterations, are these words: *We commend to your mercy all your servants who have* **DEPARTED** *from us with the sign of faith, and now rest in the sleep of peace.* This old and strange fancy gave birth to those prayers for the dead which eventually brought forth the doctrine of **Purgatory**. Along with special or private **Confession** (which these gentlemen have already declared their support for), this is the most profitable trick that priests ever invented.

The consecrated bread and wine are to be reserved and carried to the houses of those who, through sickness or some other urgent cause, cannot come to church. The next step will be a bell ringing <note>original: "clinking"</note> before the procession, and the step after that will be people being violently forced down <note>original: "devoutly knock'd down"; the author is sarcastically referring to the use of force to compel reverence</note> if they will not bow; that is, if they will not worship the priest who carries "God."

In the ritual for the visitation of the sick, the words of the Apostle James are manipulated <note>original: "interpolated"; meaning altered by inserting new words.</note> like this: *“Is any sick among you? The sick person (*after the company leaves*) is to make a special confession of his sins, *with the priest assisting him [which means interrogating him] by asking whatever questions the priest thinks appropriate.*

The Church of Rome has specialized <note>original: "rare"</note> instructions for such "soul-and-sin-pumpers" <note>A mocking term for priests who extract confessions from the laity.</note> that suggest crimes an ordinary sinner would never even imagine, giving him a taste for "delicacies" he has never tried. In pronouncing absolution <note>The formal forgiveness of sins by a priest.</note>, the priest is to lay his "sacred" hand on the sick person’s head—a fitting symbol of a soft spot there <note>The author is calling the person a fool for believing in the ritual.</note>. The oil (for they also practice anointing <note>original: "unction"</note> of the sick) must be sweet olive oil. spent; which is not the way to keep it fresh, unless the act of Consecration <note>The religious blessing of the oil.</note> has the power of preserving it. The priest shall anoint the sick person upon the forehead, making the sign of the Cross and saying a prayer that I hope shall never be said over me. The priest, when no others can be found, may receive Holy Communion alone with the sick person. But it would be endless, my Lord, to show how the Church Liturgy is mangled and transformed by these "super-churchmen." I do not have their ritual books for public or private *Baptism* (if they have printed any) to see whether they make the children taste milk and honey, or give them the communion—rites as old as their use of the sign of the cross and chrism <note>original: "chrism"; consecrated oil used in religious rites.</note>, and indeed mentioned by the same ancient authors. Nor do I know whether they exorcise or conjure the *Devil* out of those they baptize; which, though not as ancient as those other rites, was yet kept in the first edition of King Edward VI's Liturgy <note>The 1549 Book of Common Prayer, which contained more traditional rituals than later versions.</note>, for which those of this newest separation <note>The "Non-jurors," a group of Anglicans who refused to swear allegiance to the new monarchs after 1688.</note> pretend to have an extraordinary respect. I would very much like to know further whether they order the baptized not to be washed for a whole week afterward, or whether they kiss them, crown them, and clothe them in white. These practices are as old as the sign of the cross or the use of oil, and all based on the same reasoning; let them make whatever exceptions or restrictions they please, as I shall show in my treatise on *Tradition*. The truth of the matter is, the leaders of this new schism <note>A split or division within the church.</note> grew ashamed of having a distinction without a difference. They soon found that scruples about the King's royal title alone <note>The Non-jurors’ political refusal to recognize the reigning monarch.</note> would not draw people away from the public churches to their own meetings; therefore, the Church itself had to be represented as being just as disordered as the Monarchy, so that these gentlemen could "repair" the defects of both. To this end, the deep respect expressed by some prominent churchmen for the *Church Fathers* <note>The influential early Christian theologians of the first few centuries.</note>,

<header>MANGONEUTES. <note>original: "Mangoneutes"; a Greek word meaning one who exposes impostors or "manglers" of the truth.</note></header>

contributed significantly: and since Dr. Brett took the liberty of speculating about my intentions, I hope I may be as bold with his. I believe his intentions are well expressed by the common saying, that *half a loaf is better than no bread*. Since they could not immediately recover that pomp and pageantry, that authority and dominion, so suitable to the pride of priests—which they lost at the *Reformation*, and which people like themselves have mourned ever since—they went about introducing them again by small degrees, almost imperceptibly. 

Since Catholicism <note>original: "Popery"</note> was hated, they had to pretend to be Protestant <note>original: "Protestantcy"</note>; and they especially used the name of King Edward VI, a name still dear to all who hate idolatry and superstition. Therefore, the first edition of his *Liturgy* <note>The 1549 Book of Common Prayer, which retained many traditional rituals later removed in the 1552 version.</note>—which represents the first, and therefore imperfect, steps toward a *Reformation*—must be treated as the standard of perfection. 

If King Edward’s name is anything more than a mask (as it clearly serves no other purpose here), we learn from all historians that, had he lived, he would not have stopped even at the second edition of the *Liturgy*. For this reason, and certain others, some High-Church writers came very close to thanking God for his early death. Thus, "half-Catholicism" <note>original: "Half-Popery"</note>, in the view of these men, is preferable to full Protestantism. 

After giving your Lordship what I think to be the true account of this matter in a few words, I know Dr. Brett will cry out that he is no "Papist," or that he does not accept the twelve articles added by the *Council of Trent* <note>A 16th-century Roman Catholic council that defined many doctrines in opposition to the Reformation.</note> to the *Apostle's Creed*, and I readily believe him. But it means nothing to good Protestants whether unjustifiable authority is established at Rome, or Canterbury <note>The seat of the Church of England.</note>, or Wittenberg <note>The birthplace of the Lutheran Reformation.</note>, or Geneva <note>The center of the Calvinist Reformation.</note>. It does not matter whether the superstitions introduced into the Church are ancient or modern, of Eastern or Western origin, or derived from the Church Fathers or Councils—

# MANGONEUTES

But in one thing I am persuaded that all Christian churches are at present defective, and this is the notion they have about the *Jewish Law*: as if JESUS CHRIST had abolished it, even in respect to that same nation to whom it was uniquely given. I take this to be a palpable error, but not a sufficient motive for making a separation <note>original: "separation"; referring to a schism or starting a new sect</note>; since none of the *Gentiles* were required to observe this Law, no Christian churches now in the world are much affected by the mistake. However, in ancient times, this error was harmful to both *Jews* and *Gentiles*, and this was not just for a short season, nor in matters of mere triviality. 

The little that has been said by my adversaries concerning the distinction I maintain was originally intended between JEWISH AND GENTILE CHRISTIANS has served only to confirm me even more in my notion of this matter. I mean: 

> "That the Jews, though associating with the converted Gentiles and acknowledging them as brothers, were still to observe their own Law throughout all generations; and that the Gentiles—who became 'Jewish' only so far as to acknowledge one God—were not, however, to observe the Jewish Law. Instead, both groups were to be forever after united into one body or fellowship, particularly in that part of Christianity which (better than all the preparatory purifications <note>original: "purgations"</note> of the philosophers) requires the sanctification of the Spirit and the renewal of the inner man. In this alone, the Jew and the Gentile, the civilized and the barbarian, the freeman and the slave are all one in CHRIST, however much they otherwise differ in their circumstances."

In *NAZARENUS* <note>John Toland's book, *Nazarenus: or, Jewish, Gentile, and Mahometan Christianity*, published in 1718</note>, I showed the advantages of this framework for a better understanding of the *Scriptures*, and for reconciling many difficulties that are otherwise in— <meta>catchword: soluble</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Jewish Law was never intended to be abolished for ethnic Jews who became Christians, though it was never binding for Gentile converts. He asserts that while both groups are spiritually one in Christ, they were meant to maintain their distinct legal obligations.</summary>

### MANGONEUTES

...soluble, the uniting of the Jews and Gentiles in those principles—which are no less useful and noble than they are eternally true—and which alone must constitute the most perfect or universal religion. I suggested that many of the observances in the *Old Testament*, though generally mistaken for the essence of religion, were only national and commemorative ceremonies. No other people were involved in these, any more than they would be in the Jews' local laws <note>original: "municipal sanctions"</note>, which were solely designed for that particular government. I also suggested that the MOSAIC LAW was perpetually binding to the Jews, with the exception of animal sacrifices. 

Based on this foundation, the first converts to Christianity among the Jews—the NAZARENES and EBIONITES—did exactly this. Regarding them, the candid, able, and hardworking Dr. Whitby rightly says (in his commentary <margin>On 2 Corinthians 12:2</margin> on <margin>12:2</margin> 2 Corinthians): 

> "*These are not the names of individuals who founded any particular sects, but rather of the Jews who believed in JESUS and yet remained zealous observers of the Law.*" 

These NAZARENES, I say, observed their national and distinctive rites of circumcision, the Passover, and the like, alongside the doctrine of CHRIST. I am so fully convinced of the justice of their actions in this regard that, had I been born a Jew, I would have continued to live as one. This would not have prevented me from becoming a Christian, which would be the true fulfillment of the Law. 

I would, without any doubt, have defended this plan based on the plain words and scope of both the *Old and New Testaments*, even if the Church *Fathers* <note>The early influential theologians of the Christian Church, such as Augustine or Jerome</note> had never mentioned the NAZARENES at all. This is the meaning and the system that first presents itself when reading the *New Testament* after the *Old*; and were it not for those *Fathers* and their followers, it would still naturally present itself to every reader. I cannot help but wonder, by the way, how such a capable person as Dr. Brett could commit such a great error in reasoning <term>paralogism</term> as I am about to show...

Nothing can be more contrary to the whole conduct of our **SAVIOR** while on earth than this interpretation. He declared on all occasions that he came not to destroy the Law, but to fulfill it. He was very exact in observing it himself and requiring others to observe even the smallest command of it; and there was nothing he avoided with more **CARE** than to give **A SUSPICION** <note>original: "jealousy"</note> to the Jews that he had such **A DESIGN**, as he is supposed to have declared to his Apostles in these words. The abolishing of the ceremonial part of the *Jewish Law* seems to have been one of the things which the disciples were not yet able to bear (*John 16:12*); and it is plain from several places in the *Acts*, particularly chapter 21, verse 20, that they had not the **LEAST APPREHENSION** of this being our Savior’s meaning, neither when he spoke these words nor for several years after his death. The truth is that, at last, when the judicial and ceremonial parts of the Law were to cease, they expired of themselves upon the destruction of the City and Temple—which was a total dissolution of the *Jewish State* <note>original: "Polity"</note>—rather than being abolished by any **AUTHORITATIVE DECLARATION** or **ACT** of the Apostles. Nothing remained but *Synagogue worship*, which, being of human institution, did not require a divine commission either to alter or to end it.
