## MANGONEUTES
<note>The title "Mangoneutes" comes from a Greek word meaning a "deceiver" or "faker," often used to describe someone who manipulates or "doctors" a text or an argument to make it look better than it is.</note>

[As a conse]quence, a Law so solemnly given by God himself, which had continued so long and was so frequently pronounced to be of perpetual obligation, should not be declared null and void by the event—which is most commonly, even in ordinary affairs, a very wrong as well as unfair way of judging. The Jewish political state <note>original: "Polity"</note> was totally dissolved at the Babylonian captivity, and Jerusalem was laid in ashes; yet the Jews did not then conclude themselves dispensed <note>original: "dispens'd"; meaning they were not excused from their duties</note> from observing all that they possibly could of their Law, as they solicitously do at this day. Nor do they lack threats <note>original: "menaces"</note> for their neglect of it, nor prophetic hopes to see it reestablished—in which expectation I profess myself rooted—despite <note>original: "maugre"</note> all the revolutions that shall befall them. 

The shortness or length of their dispersion is no sure rule for judging the case, which, as I said just now, is not to be determined by consequences. Besides, the supporters of this abolition are little agreed among themselves. Most say the Law was not to cease when **CHRIST** was born; some say it was not when he began his ministry; and yet others say it was not until the destruction of Jerusalem. To this day, they are not agreed about the steps of this gradual abolition but go on from supposition to supposition; several of them are contradictory, and all are uncertain <note>original: "precarious"</note>. But they mistake the men with whom they have to deal, and they frequently expose themselves to their laughter. 

It must be granted (says Dr. Kidder, the once worthy Bishop of Bristol, well-versed in Hebrew learning) that <gloss>61</gloss> *some of the ancient, and many of the modern Christians, have defended their own doctrines.*

### THE DECEIVER <note>original: "Mangoneutes" — a Greek term for a "deceiver" or someone who "doctors" things to make them appear better than they are.</note>

[T]he best way I can to satisfy my friends and, at the same time, to perform my duty to the world. For, My Lord, despite the accusations of heresy and infidelity so often published by the clergy—as was recently done in the most boastful manner by someone you know (the fickle <note>original: "whiffling"</note> and the ignorant being always the most arrogant and confident)—I assure Your Lordship that the purity of religion and the prosperity of the state have always been my chief aims. **CIVIL LIBERTY** and **RELIGIOUS TOLERATION** are the most desirable things in this world, being the most conducive to peace, plenty, knowledge, and every kind of happiness; they have been the two main objectives of all my writings. 

But just as by liberty I did not mean lawlessness <term>original: "licentiousness"; a lack of moral restraint or disregard for rules</term>, by toleration I did not mean indifference, and much less the approval of every religion that I could tolerate. To be more specific, I solemnly declare to Your Lordship that the religion taught by **JESUS CHRIST** and his **APOSTLES** is what I infinitely prefer above all others—though not as it has since been corrupted by the subtractions, additions, or other alterations made by any particular man or group of men. 

I repeat again and again: **CHRIST** and his **APOSTLES**, excluding both oral tradition and the decrees of synods <term>synods: councils or assemblies of church officials convened to decide on matters of doctrine or administration</term>. I add, as I have declared to the world before, that religion, as it came from their hands, was no less plain and pure than it was useful and instructive; and that, because it is the concern of every person, it was understood equally by everyone. For **CHRIST** did not establish one religion for the learned and another for the common people <term>original: "vulgar"; the ordinary or uneducated public</term>, as the defenders of *tradition* must maintain, or else give up their argument. On the contrary, it is recorded that the common people heard **JESUS** gladly; and he was not only followed by

<meta>catchword: diverse</meta>

<summary>The author defends his work against accusations of heresy, asserting that his primary goals are civil liberty and religious toleration. He argues for a simple, accessible Christianity based solely on the teachings of Christ and the Apostles, rejecting later ecclesiastical traditions and decrees.</summary>
<keywords>civil liberty, religious toleration, heresy, clerical corruption, oral tradition, synods, pure religion, Jesus Christ, Apostles</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that true Christianity is simple and accessible to everyone, regardless of their social status or education. He contrasts this "pure" faith with the complex traditions and philosophical jargon that he believes have corrupted religion over time.</meta>

->THE DECEIVER<-
<note>original: "MANGONEUTES" — a Greek term for a "cheat," "deceiver," or someone who "doctors" things to make them appear better than they are.</note>

...diverse women, but also among the converts of **PAUL**, quite a few honorable women are reckoned. This suggests that, being well-inclined and without prejudice, they easily understood the evidence of the Christian doctrine, which they preferred to their native but less edifying religions. And certainly one of the most distinguishing advantages of true Christianity is this: that neither poverty, nor lack of learning, nor the pressures of specific occupations <note>original: "hurry of particular callings"</note> can hinder any person from acquiring it. Without this accessibility, it could not properly be a perfect religion; it would instead be on the same level as the traditional worship of the degenerate Jews, the awe-inspiring <note>original: "awful"; in this context meaning dread-inducing or mysterious</note> mysteries of the bewildered pagans <note>original: "Heathens"</note>, or the secret initiations of certain hybrid <note>original: "mungrel"</note> philosophers. Whereas Christianity supplies what is missing in the first, exposes the vanity of the second, and prevents the deceptions <note>original: "impostures"</note> of the third.

Neither the borrowed terms and beliefs of ancient, perplexing philosophers <term>original: "Sophisters"</term>, nor the crude jargon and distinctions of later academic debaters <note>original: "scholastic wranglers"</note>, nor the shaky hypotheses of arrogant theorists, nor the claimed inspirations of wild fanatics <term>original: "Enthusiasts"; in this era, a negative term for those claiming direct, often irrational, divine revelation</term>, were then the established articles of faith. Truth was not decided by a majority vote, nor was anything taught as truth unless it clearly tended to make people either wiser or better. Riddles about the person of **CHRIST** were not substituted for his doctrines; nor were the minor details <note>original: "circumstantials"</note> made into essential fundamentals, nor was the history of Christianity mistaken for its core essence.

And here, my Lord, I would address myself to those who are more knowledgeable, to be informed: for what purpose can anything serve which does not make us either wiser or better than we were before? And if it is granted to be to no good purpose (as I can yet perceive...

<summary>The author argues that true Christianity is inherently simple and accessible to all, unlike the complex rituals of pagans or the confusing jargon of later academic theologians. He asserts that religious truth should be judged solely by its ability to make people wiser or better.</summary>
<keywords>Paul the Apostle, accessibility of faith, religious corruption, Sophisters, Scholasticism, Enthusiasts, essence of Christianity, moral improvement</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that if religion is meant to be accessible to all, there is no reason for society to be divided by incomprehensible doctrines. He asserts that true Christianity is defined by its practical moral benefits rather than by mysterious dogmas.</meta>

->THE DECEIVER<-
<note>original: "MANGONEUTES" — a Greek term for a "cheat" or "deceiver."</note>

...perceive no reason why it should not), then I would again be informed why nations, provinces, families, friends, and acquaintances should be disturbed; why all union should be dissolved, affections divided, laws subverted, or governments unhinged, about such things as no mortal can either conceive himself, or tolerably explain to others? 

Such are the decrees of God, the immediate state of departed souls, the manner of God's existence <note>original: "subsistence"</note>, of his understanding, or of his providence—along with other subjects which, though deemed more easy, are not better known than these. Requiring people to believe what they cannot understand is like ordering them to make bricks without straw. 

It is from this requirement that the divisions of Christians largely proceed, though this is not the only cause. In contrast, nothing is wiser, plainer, truer, and consequently more divine, than what **CHRIST** and his **APOSTLES** have proposed regarding:
* the means of reconciling God to sinners, 
* purifying the mind, 
* regulating manners, 
* directing conscience, 
* illuminating the understanding, 
* stating particular duties, 
* fixing the hope of rewards for the good, 
* planting the fear of punishment in the wicked, 
* propagating mutual love, forbearance, and peace among all mankind, 
* and improving, conducting, and supporting civil society. 

This is not the religion of those 

— *whose art it is to make things unbelievable;* 
<note>original: "Quorum, quid non creditur, Ars est" — likely a reference to those who profit from keeping religious doctrines mysterious and incomprehensible.</note>

nor will they thank me for this description, for they have turned **CHRISTIANITY** into a plan of dominion, a system of gain, and a school of contention. Just as in the first preaching of the Gospel, those who gloried in being the only true Church were those Scribes and Pharisees who most resisted its progress,

<meta>catchword: progress,</meta>

<summary>The author argues that social and political conflict arises from forcing belief in incomprehensible mysteries. He contrasts these "systems of gain" with the practical, moral, and socially stabilizing teachings of original Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>religious mystery, social order, Christ, Apostles, Scribes, Pharisees, religious corruption, morality, civil society</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author’s argument by linking institutional opposition to the Gospel with the corruption of the clergy. He appeals to a high-ranking official for protection against the slander of local priests.</meta>

# THE DECEIVER
<note>original: "MANGONEUTES" — a Greek term for a "cheat" or "deceiver."</note>

...progress, and the priests were, of all others, its fiercest enemies; nor should this be considered a wonder, since there is no such satire in the world against priestcraft <term>priestcraft: the use of religious office for personal gain or political power</term> as the *Gospel* of *Christ*, which made the priests never cease until they brought him at last to the Cross. I beg your Lordship’s pardon if I have been more wordy <note>original: "prolix"</note> than suits your leisure; not doubting, however, that you will obtain reparation for me from those of your clergy who have injured me, and prevent others from falling hereafter into similar gross falsehoods, willful mistakes, or ridiculous follies. I am with all due respect,

->*My Lord,*<-

September 17, 1720.
->*Your Lordship’s*<-
->most obedient servant,<-

->J. T.<-
<note>J. T.: John Toland (1670–1722), a prominent and controversial Irish rationalist philosopher and freethinker.</note>

->THE END<-
<note>original: "FINIS" — Latin for "The End."</note>

<summary>In this concluding passage, John Toland argues that the Gospel naturally opposes the interests of a corrupt clergy and asks a bishop to discipline those priests who have slandered him.</summary>

# ERRATA
<note>original: "E R R A T A" — a list of errors and corrections found in a book after it has been printed.</note>

The author, spending the summer in the country and not always seeing the final proofs <note>original: "last revise"</note>, the reader is requested to correct the following printing errors; or if he finds any more, they are left to his kindness <note>original: "candor"</note>.

->In the Preface.<-

*Page 18, line 44,* political read philosophical. *p. 21, l. 6,* "trinkets" <note>original: "ῥοπνια"</note> read "trinkets" <note>original: "ῥωπνια" (rhōpnia) — small, cheap wares; the correction fixes a spelling error in the Greek text.</note>. *p. 22, l. 3,* "receipts" <note>original: "recepts"</note> read receipts.

->In the Work.<-

*Page 9, line 32,* for RAMUZZI read RAMUSIO <note>Giovan Battista Ramusio (1485–1557), a famous Italian geographer and travel writer.</note>. *p. 12, l. 28,* pervesness read perverseness. *p. 31, l. 31,* attributes read attribute. *p. 59, l. 10,* delete "his". *p. 111, l. 3,* BAROMIUS read BARONIUS <note>Caesar Baronius (1538–1607), an influential Catholic cardinal and historian.</note>. *p. 120, l. 6,* "His" read "This". *p. 122, l. 3,* "oimt" read "omit". *p. 146, l. 33,* "and" read "and an". *p. 168, l 21,* "first" read "last". *Ibid. l. 22,* "last" read "first". *p. 178, l. 1,* "most" read "more".

->In the inner Margin.<-

*Page 10, line 2,* "in the same" <note>original: "eodum"</note> read "in the same" <note>original: "eodem" (Latin).</note>. *p. 14, l. 5,* "pioneers" <note>original: "חלוצם"</note> read "pioneers" <note>original: "חלוצים" (chalutzim) — Hebrew for "pioneers" or "armed men."</note>. *p. 18, l. 15,* "M gos" read "Magicians" <note>original: "Magos" (Latin) — referring to the Magi or sorcerers.</note>. *p, 20, l. 4,* "from heaven" <note>original: "coe itus"</note> read "from heaven" <note>original: "coelitus" (Latin).</note>. *p. 38, l. 2,* "repeats" <note>original: "refieri"</note> read "refers" <note>original: "refiere" (Spanish).</note>. *p. 175, l. 6,* Scotch read Scottish.

->In the outward Margin.<-

*Page 15* "thus" <note>original: "כם"</note> read "thus" <note>original: "כן" (ken) — Hebrew.</note>. *p. 82,* "and" <note>original: "x,αι"</note> read "and" <note>original: "και" (kai) — Greek.</note>. *p. 165, Acts 1:22* read *Acts 2:22. Ibid. John 1:45.* read *John 1:46.*

# ERRATA
<note>original: "E R R A T A" — a list of printing errors and their corrections.</note>

The author, having traveled into the country and not always being able to supervise the printing press, is requested to have the reader correct the following errors; if the reader finds any more, they are left to his own judgment <note>original: "candour"</note>.

->In the Preface.<-

Page 1, line 15: for *polished* read *philosophical*. Page 32, line 9: for *variant* read *various*. The penultimate line <note>original: "l. penult." — the next to last line</note>: for "he discusses" <note>original: "desertat"</note> read "he discusses" <note>original: "dissertat" (Latin)</note>.

->In the Works.<-

Part 1, Discourse <note>original: "Discur."</note> 4, page 81, line 1: for ADMONITION 1 <note>original: "AMMONITIO I" (Latin)</note> read ADMONITION 2 <note>original: "AMMONITIO II" (Latin)</note>.
